general-relativity.bigb
= General relativity
{wiki}

= Israel junction conditions
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{title2=$[h_{ab}]=0,\quad[K_{ab}]-h_{ab}[K]=-\kappa_D^2S_{ab}$}

Across a thin timelike matter layer, the induced metric is continuous while the jump in its <extrinsic curvature> is determined by the surface <stress-energy tensor>. Choose one common spacelike normal and $K_{ab}=\tfrac12\partial_yh_{ab}$ in Gaussian normal coordinates. The singular tangential <Einstein tensor> is $-([K_{ab}]-h_{ab}[K])\delta(y)$; equating it to $\kappa_D^2S_{ab}\delta(y)$ gives the displayed conditions. Reversing the normal or the sign convention for <extrinsic curvature> changes the corresponding jump signs consistently. These conditions determine the cosmological expansion law of a thin gravitating <brane>.

= Trapping horizon
{parent=General relativity}

A trapping horizon is a hypersurface foliated by marginal two-surfaces with one <null expansion> zero and the other nonzero. A future outer trapping horizon has $\theta_+=0$, $\theta_-<0$ and $\mathcal L_{n}\theta_+<0$ for a future ingoing null normal $n$. Unlike an <event horizon>, this is a quasi-local definition. Under the <null energy condition>, a regular future outer horizon is spacelike or null. The ingoing derivative condition distinguishes its outer type from an inner trapping horizon.

= Congruence
{disambiguate=general relativity}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Congruence of curves in general relativity
{synonym}

A congruence in <general relativity> is a family of curves through an open spacetime region such that each point lies on exactly one member. Its tangent field describes the family locally. A <geodesic congruence> is the special case in which every member is a <geodesic>.

= Causal structure
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

The <causal structure> of a spacetime classifies separations and tangent directions as timelike, null or spacelike through its light cones. It determines which events can communicate by causal curves. A <null curve> is one member of this broader geometric structure.

= Flat-background scalar-force theory of gravity
{parent=General relativity}
{title2=$dp^i=\partial^i\Phi\,p_jdx^j-(p^j\partial_j\Phi)dx^i$}

= Scalar-force gravity
{synonym}

This covariant scalar-force model uses the <Minkowski metric> and the displayed momentum law, with $\Box\Phi=-4\pi G T^i{}_i$. For a massive <test particle>, write $p^i=mu^i$, $u^iu_i=1$. Contracting the force law with $p_i$ gives constant <rest mass>; the acceleration is $du^i/d\tau=\partial^i\Phi-u^i(u^j\partial_j\Phi)$, independent of the mass. A static weak field obeys the <Poisson equation> and yields Newtonian acceleration $-\boldsymbol\nabla\Phi$. For a <photon>, $p\cdot dx=0$ and $dx$ is parallel to $p$, so $dp^i=-p^i d\Phi$: photon energies change but their unparametrized paths remain straight. The motion law has the same unparametrized curves as the conformal metric $e^{2\Phi}\eta$, but the field equation must not be silently identified with a different scalar-gravity theory.

= Scalar-force gravitational redshift
{parent=Flat-background scalar-force theory of gravity}
{title2=$\nu_r/\nu_e=e^{\Phi_e-\Phi_r}$}

For a <photon> in the <flat-background scalar-force theory of gravity>, $e^\Phi p^i$ is constant along its path. In a static field, energies measured by stationary laboratories therefore satisfy the displayed ratio. At first order, $\Delta\nu/\nu=-\Delta\Phi$ in units $c=1$, matching the weak-field <Pound-Rebka experiment>. This agreement does not imply light bending: a momentum change parallel to the original null tangent changes its scale, not its direction.

= Taub–NUT space
{parent=General relativity}
{c}

The single-centre Euclidean Taub–NUT geometry is a gravitational instanton with a circle fibre over three-dimensional space. Its fibre shrinks smoothly at the centre when its period is chosen appropriately. Adding flat worldvolume directions gives the local geometry of a <Kaluza-Klein monopole>.

// Target: algebra.bigb

= Near-horizon limit
{parent=General relativity}

A limit retaining the geometry near a horizon while discarding terms describing its distant asymptotic region. For a regular extremal brane throat, removing the constant from its harmonic function can yield an <anti-de Sitter spacetime> factor. Local symmetries and supersymmetries of the limiting geometry need not extend to the full original spacetime.

= Einstein-Maxwell theory
{c}
{parent=General relativity}

The Einstein-Maxwell system couples the metric to an electromagnetic field. In geometrized units its source-free equations are $G_{ab}=8\pi T_{ab}^{\mathrm{EM}}$, $\nabla_aF^{ab}=0$ and $dF=0$, with $T_{ab}^{\mathrm{EM}}=(F_{ac}F_b{}^c-\tfrac14g_{ab}F_{cd}F^{cd})/(4\pi)$. Electrovacuum need not have vanishing Ricci tensor, so a Komar integral need not be unchanged between different enclosing surfaces.

= Majumdar–Papapetrou solution
{c}
{parent=Einstein-Maxwell theory}
{title2=$ds^2=-H^{-2}dt^2+H^2d\mathbf x^2$}

The four-dimensional <Majumdar–Papapetrou solution> is a static multi-black-hole Einstein-Maxwell solution with $H=1+\sum_iM_i/|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|$ and positive geometrized masses $M_i$. The electromagnetic potential can be written $A=\pm(H^{-1}-1)dt$, with a common sign of charge. Its mass at infinity is $\sum_iM_i$. Each distinct centre is an extremal horizon of zero surface gravity rather than a pointlike naked metric singularity.

= Analytic extension of a Majumdar–Papapetrou horizon
{parent=Majumdar–Papapetrou solution}

Near a distinct centre of mass $M$, write $H=M/r+h+rF(r,\theta,\phi)$, where $h$ is the regular part evaluated at that centre and $F$ is analytic in local angular charts. Set $A=M+hr$, $Q=rH=A+r^2F$ and $dv=dt+(h+M/r)^2dr$. The metric becomes
$$
ds^2=-\frac{r^2}{Q^2}dv^2+2\frac{A^2}{Q^2}dv\,dr+F\frac{(Q+A)(Q^2+A^2)}{Q^2}dr^2+Q^2d\Omega_2^2.
$$
All coefficients extend analytically across $r=0$, with $Q(0)=M>0$ and two-dimensional determinant $-1$. The surface is null, its generator is $\partial_v$, and the double zero of its squared norm gives zero surface gravity. The construction uses a constant $h$ so that the coordinate differential is integrable despite the nonspherical regular part of $H$.

= Twistor theory
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

<Twistor theory> encodes the <conformal geometry> of four-dimensional spacetime using incidence geometry in a complex vector space. In the flat correspondence, a spacetime point is a two-plane in a four-dimensional <twistor space>, and hence a projective line in <projective twistor space>. Null separation becomes intersection of these lines. Choosing a spacetime signature is an additional reality condition on the complex geometry.

= Twistor correspondence
{parent=Twistor theory}
{title2=$\operatorname{Gr}(2,\mathbb C^4)\leftarrow F_{1,2}(\mathbb C^4)\rightarrow\mathbb{CP}^3$}

The <flag manifold> of a line $\ell$ contained in a two-plane $W$ has projections $(\ell,W)\mapsto W$ and $(\ell,W)\mapsto\ell$. Their fibers are respectively $\mathbb{CP}^1$ and $\mathbb{CP}^2$. This is the flat <twistor correspondence>: <complexified compactified Minkowski space> is the <Grassmannian> of two-planes in <twistor space>, while <projective twistor space> is its projective space of lines.

= Penrose-Ward correspondence
{parent=Twistor correspondence}
{c}

A <holomorphic vector bundle> on an appropriate twistor domain, trivial on its space-time incidence lines, determines a complex anti-self-dual gauge <gauge connection>. Split its patching <matrix> on each line and apply the tangent vector fields $\pi^{A'}\partial_{AA'}$. They annihilate the unsplit twistor patching <matrix>, so the two logarithmic <derivatives> of the factors agree and define a global degree-one <function> of $\pi$. It is consequently $\pi^{A'}A_{AA'}$. The inverse splitting <bundle frame> is parallel along each <alpha-plane>; commuting these parallel equations proves that the primed <gauge curvature> <two-component spinor> vanishes. Reality conditions recover unitary Euclidean <gauge fields>; Euclidean <instanton> bundles need triviality on the real <twistor lines>, not on every possible complex line.

= Abelian Ward splitting by Laurent series
{parent=Penrose-Ward correspondence}
{c}
{title2=$f_x(\zeta)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}f_n(x)\zeta^n=h_1-h_0$}

On a <twistor line> use $\pi_{0'}=\zeta,\pi_{1'}=1$ and $\omega^A=x^{A0'}\zeta+x^{A1'}$. For a degree-zero <holomorphic> patching logarithm, its <Laurent series> splits as $h_0=-\sum_{n\ge0}f_n\zeta^n$, $h_1=\sum_{n<0}f_n\zeta^n$. The <twistor incidence relation> implies $\partial_{A0'}f_n=\partial_{A1'}f_{n-1}$. Hence $(-\partial_{A0'}+\zeta\partial_{A1'})h_\alpha=\partial_{A1'}f_{-1}$ on both patches. The <gauge potential> is $A_{A0'}=-\partial_{A1'}f_{-1}$, $A_{A1'}=0$. Exponentiating the two additive factors produces the linewise <holomorphic> splitting of $e^f$.

= Contour potential for an anti-self-dual Maxwell field
{parent=Abelian Ward splitting by Laurent series}
{title2=$A_{BB'}=\frac1{2\pi i}\oint_\Gamma\frac{\iota_{B'}}{\iota_{C'}\pi^{C'}}\,f_{,B}\,\pi_{D'}d\pi^{D'}$}

The <Abelian Ward splitting by Laurent series> gives this <gauge potential> through the <Cauchy integral formula>, with $f_{,B}=\partial f/\partial\omega^B$ evaluated on the <twistor line>. The integrand has total projective degree zero. Holding the contour fixed locally and differentiating gives $F_{AA'BB'}=\epsilon_{A'B'}(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_\Gamma f_{,AB}\,\pi_{D'}d\pi^{D'}$, using $\pi_{A'}\iota_{B'}-\pi_{B'}\iota_{A'}=\epsilon_{A'B'}(\iota_{C'}\pi^{C'})$. Its unprimed <two-component spinor> is symmetric, so the primed <gauge curvature> vanishes and the field obeys the <anti-self-dual Maxwell equations>. These signs use $\epsilon_{0'1'}=1$, $\epsilon^{0'1'}=-1$ and raising by left multiplication; other consistent raising conventions can reverse both displayed overall signs.

= Line-trivial bundles in the Ward correspondence
{parent=Penrose-Ward correspondence}
{title2=$E|_{L_x}\cong\mathcal O^{\oplus r}$}

= Line-trivial bundle in the Ward correspondence
{synonym}

On a twistor domain containing the lines $L_x$ for $x$ in a space-time domain $U$, the Ward construction uses a <holomorphic vector bundle> $E$ with $E|_{L_x}\cong\mathcal O^{\oplus r}$ for every $x\in U$. A degree-zero restriction is not sufficient: $\mathcal O(1)\oplus\mathcal O(-1)$ has degree zero but is not holomorphically trivial. A trivial restriction has $r$ independent global <holomorphic> sections; their evaluations give a linewise <bundle frame> and hence a factorization of its patching <matrix>. Nearby factors can be chosen holomorphically because $H^1(\mathbb{CP}^1,\mathcal O)^{r^2}=0$. Lines on which the splitting type is nontrivial are called jumping lines and obstruct this regular reconstruction there. For a globally regular field on affine <complexified Minkowski space> the condition is needed on all its incidence lines; on a smaller domain only those lines are required.

= Holomorphic splitting of a twistor patching matrix
{parent=Penrose-Ward correspondence}
{title2=$F=\widetilde H H^{-1}$}

A bundle trivial on a <twistor line> has a global holomorphic <bundle frame> there. Comparing it with the given two patch <bundle frames> produces invertible <matrices> $H,\widetilde H$, holomorphic on their respective affine patches, with $F=\widetilde H H^{-1}$. The obstruction to varying such a <bundle frame> locally with space-time is $H^1(\mathbb{CP}^1,\mathcal O)^{n^2}=0$, so the splitting factors can be chosen holomorphically in a neighbourhood. If two splittings differ by $H\mapsto Hg$ and $\widetilde H\mapsto\widetilde Hg$, then $g$ is holomorphic on the compact <complex projective line> and hence independent of its projective coordinate. It is the space-time gauge freedom.

= Alpha-plane
{parent=Twistor correspondence}
{title2=$A_\ell=\{W:\ell\subset W\}\cong\mathbb{CP}^2$}

An alpha-plane in the compact <twistor correspondence> is the set of two-planes containing a fixed line $\ell$ in <twistor space>. Its affine portion has tangent vectors $u^A\pi^{A'}$ for fixed primed spinor $\pi$, so any two tangent vectors are orthogonal and null. Two distinct compact alpha-planes meet at the unique two-plane spanned by their defining lines; that point can lie at conformal infinity.

= Twistor line
{parent=Twistor correspondence}
{title2=$L_x=\mathbb P(W_x)\cong\mathbb{CP}^1$}

The projective line associated to a two-plane $W_x$ representing a point of <complexified compactified Minkowski space>. Two distinct <twistor lines> intersect precisely when their spacetime points are null separated. On the Lorentzian real slice, the line lies entirely in <projective null twistor space> and parametrizes the null geodesics through the point.

= Complexified compactified Minkowski space
{parent=Twistor correspondence}
{title2=$\mathbb M_{\mathbb C}=\operatorname{Gr}(2,\mathbb C^4)$}

The four-complex-dimensional <Grassmannian> of two-planes in <twistor space>. Its <Plücker embedding> is the <Klein quadric>. An affine chart consists of graphs $W_x=\{(ix\pi,\pi)\}$. A <Lorentzian reality structure on twistor space> or an <Euclidean reality structure on twistor space> selects different four-real-dimensional compact spacetimes inside this same complex manifold.

= Twistor space
{parent=Twistor theory}
{title2=$\mathbb T\cong\mathbb C^4$}
{wiki}

In flat <twistor theory>, a twistor is a pair $Z=(\omega^A,\pi_{A'})$ of two-component spinors. The <twistor incidence relation> with a spacetime point is $\omega^A=ix^{AA'}\pi_{A'}$. The nonzero vector $Z$ and its projective class play different roles: the latter belongs to <projective twistor space>.

= Euclidean reality structure on twistor space
{c}
{parent=Twistor space}
{title2=$J^2=-1,\quad J(W)=W$}

Choose an antilinear quaternionic structure $J$ on $\mathbb C^4$. It induces a fixed-point-free involution on <projective twistor space>. A real Euclidean spacetime point is a $J$-invariant complex two-plane, equivalently a quaternionic line in $\mathbb H^2$. These form $\mathbb{HP}^1\cong S^4$, the <conformal compactification of Euclidean space>. Every projective twistor lies on the unique invariant line $\mathbb P\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb C}(Z,JZ)$, giving a fibration $\mathbb{CP}^3\to S^4$ with fiber $\mathbb{CP}^1$.

= Twistor Hermitian form
{parent=Twistor space}
{title2=$h(Z,W)=\omega^A\overline{\rho}_A+\pi_{A'}\overline{\eta}^{A'}$}

For $Z=(\omega,\pi)$ and $W=(\eta,\rho)$ the displayed <Hermitian form> has signature $(2,2)$. It is zero on every pair of vectors in $W_x=\{(ix\pi,\pi)\}$ when $x$ is Hermitian, that is, Lorentzian real. Conversely a transverse totally isotropic two-plane is such a graph with $x$ Hermitian. This gives the <Lorentzian reality structure on twistor space>.

= Lorentzian reality structure on twistor space
{c}
{parent=Twistor Hermitian form}

Real compactified Minkowski points are the two-dimensional <totally isotropic subspaces> for the signature-$(2,2)$ <twistor Hermitian form>. In a graph chart this is the usual Hermitian reality condition on $x^{AA'}$. Their <twistor lines> lie in <projective null twistor space>. On the <Klein quadric> the same real form is the projectivized null cone of a real quadratic form of signature $(2,4)$.

= Null twistor
{parent=Twistor Hermitian form}
{title2=$h(Z,Z)=0$}

A nonzero twistor null for the <twistor Hermitian form>. Its projective class determines a real <null geodesic> in compactified Minkowski space. For nonzero $\pi$, its affine real solutions of the <twistor incidence relation> have the form $x=x_0+r\overline\pi^A\pi^{A'}$, $r\in\mathbb R$. Twistors with $\pi=0$ encode generators at infinity rather than finite affine solutions.

= Projective null twistor space
{parent=Null twistor}
{title2=$PN=\{[Z]\in\mathbb{CP}^3:h(Z,Z)=0\}$}

The five-real-dimensional space of projective <null twistors>. A projective line lies entirely in it precisely when its corresponding two-plane is totally isotropic for the <twistor Hermitian form>. Such lines represent real points of <Minkowski conformal compactification>. Two independent null twistors lie on one such line exactly when $h(X,Y)=0$.

= Twistor incidence relation
{parent=Twistor space}
{title2=$\omega^A=ix^{AA'}\pi_{A'}$}

For a fixed affine spacetime point $x$, this linear relation cuts out a two-dimensional subspace $W_x=\{(ix\pi,\pi)\}$ of <twistor space>. Projectivizing gives its <twistor line>. For a fixed projective twistor with $\pi\ne0$, its solutions are $x=x_0+u^A\pi^{A'}$, a totally null affine <alpha-plane>. The compact description replaces affine solutions by the two-planes containing the fixed twistor line in the vector space.

= Projective twistor space
{parent=Twistor space}
{title2=$\mathbb P\mathbb T\cong\mathbb{CP}^3$}

The <projective space> of one-dimensional complex subspaces of <twistor space>. A point corresponds to an <alpha-plane> in <complexified compactified Minkowski space>; a projective line corresponds to a spacetime point.

= Affine projective twistor space
{parent=Projective twistor space}
{title2=$PT_{\rm aff}=\mathbb{CP}^3\setminus\mathbb{CP}^1_\infty$}

For affine <complexified Minkowski space>, the relevant twistor domain is
$$
PT_{\rm aff}=\{[\omega^A,\pi_{A'}]\in\mathbb{CP}^3:\pi\ne0\}
=\mathbb{CP}^3\setminus\mathbb{CP}^1_\infty
\cong\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal O(1)\oplus\mathcal O(1)).
$$
Incidence can be written $\omega^A=x^{AA'}\pi_{A'}$, absorbing the conventional factor $i$ into the definition of $x$. Each space-time point gives a <twistor line> $L_x\cong\mathbb{CP}^1$. On charts $\pi_{0'}\ne0$ and $\pi_{1'}\ne0$, the fibre coordinates transform by $\omega/\pi_{1'}=(\omega/\pi_{0'})/\lambda$, with $\lambda=\pi_{1'}/\pi_{0'}$, giving the two $\mathcal O(1)$ summands. The full <projective twistor space> corresponds to <complexified compactified Minkowski space>.

= Analytic extension of a spacetime
{parent=General relativity}

= Analytic extensions of a spacetime
{synonym}

An analytic extension embeds a spacetime isometrically as a proper open region in a larger analytic <Lorentzian manifold> with a nondegenerate <metric tensor>. A divergence of coordinate components does not obstruct extension if a new chart makes all metric coefficients analytic and the determinant nonzero. For a static spherical metric with an analytic lapse $f$ and a positive simple horizon radius, ingoing coordinates replace $-f\,dt^2+dr^2/f$ by $-f\,dv^2+2\,dv\,dr$. This removes the <coordinate singularity> at $f=0$; transverse metric coefficients must also remain regular. An extension across such a horizon is distinct from extending through a <curvature singularity>.

= Two-sheeted extension of superextremal Kerr spacetime
{parent=Analytic extension of a spacetime}
{title2=$\rho^2/(r^2+a^2)+z^2/r^2=1$}

For $0<M<a$, $\Delta=(r-M)^2+a^2-M^2>0$, so no horizon blocks need adjoining. The oblate Cartesian coordinates of the <Kerr metric> satisfy the displayed relation, where $\rho^2=x^2+y^2$. Keeping only the positive root $r$ leaves a cut along the disk $z=0$, $\rho<a$. Continue $r$ through zero away from its boundary, the <Kerr ring singularity>, onto a negative-radius sheet. Equivalently cut two Cartesian sheets along the disk and join opposite banks, so $r$ changes sign continuously. The metric is analytic at the disk interior in signed oblate coordinates since $\Sigma=a^2\cos^2\theta\ne0$ there. The extension has asymptotically flat ends at $r\to+\infty$ and $r\to-\infty$; only the singular ring remains inextendible by an analytic Lorentz metric.

= Gravitational redshift
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nu_{\mathrm r}/\nu_{\mathrm e}=N_{\mathrm e}/N_{\mathrm r}$}

In a static spacetime with $g_{tt}=-N^2$, a photon has conserved <Killing energy> $E=-p_t$. A static observer with four-velocity $N^{-1}\partial_t$ measures frequency proportional to $E/N$. Hence light emitted at one lapse and received at another obeys the displayed ratio. For $N=1+\Phi/c^2+O(\Phi^2/c^4)$ it gives the weak-field shift $(\nu_{\mathrm r}-\nu_{\mathrm e})/\nu_{\mathrm e}=(\Phi_{\mathrm e}-\Phi_{\mathrm r})/c^2$. This local clock effect does not require light-ray bending.

= Outgoing pulse redshift from a radial Schwarzschild infaller
{parent=Gravitational redshift}
{title2=$\nu_\infty/\nu_{\mathrm{em}}=f/(E+\sqrt{E^2-f})$}

A radial infalling <geodesic> in <Schwarzschild spacetime> has $dt/ds=E/f$ and $dr/ds=-\sqrt{E^2-f}$, where $f=1-2M/r$. An outward <radial null geodesic> has fixed retarded time $u=t-r_*$ with $dr_*/dr=1/f$. Consequently $du/ds=(E+\sqrt{E^2-f})/f$. This factor stretches a short emitted pulse's duration, and its reciprocal multiplies the received frequency at infinity. Near horizon crossing $s_H$, it becomes $du/ds\sim4M/(s_H-s)$, including both <gravitational redshift> and the emitter's <Doppler shift>. The local short-pulse approximation requires its emitted duration to be much smaller than $s_H-s$.

// Target: general-relativity.bigb

= Pound-Rebka experiment
{parent=Gravitational redshift}
{c}
{wiki=Pound–Rebka_experiment}

The experiment compares gamma-ray emission and resonant absorption at different heights in Earth's field, using the <Mossbauer effect> to resolve the small <gravitational redshift>. Its weak-field result is the frequency shift $\Delta\nu/\nu=-gH/c^2$ for an upward ray over height $H$. Any metric theory with the corresponding static lapse predicts this leading effect; the test alone does not distinguish <general relativity> from the weak-field prediction of <Nordstrom theory of gravitation>.

= Planar warped spacetime
{parent=General relativity}
{title2=$ds^2=A(z)^2(-dt^2+dx^2+dy^2)+dz^2$}

For positive smooth $A$, this <Lorentzian manifold> has flat $(t,x,y)$ slices scaled by $A(z)^2$. Translation in each slice coordinate gives a <Killing vector field>. An <orthonormal coframe> is $e^0=A\,dt$, $e^1=A\,dx$, $e^2=A\,dy$, $e^3=dz$. Its <Levi-Civita connection> and <Ricci tensor> are controlled by $a=A'/A$ and $q=A''/A=a'+a^2$.

= Cartan curvature of a planar warped spacetime
{c}
{parent=Planar warped spacetime}
{title2=$a=A'/A,\quad q=A''/A$}

For the <orthonormal coframe> of a <planar warped spacetime>, <Cartan's first structure equation> gives $\omega^i{}_3=a e^i$ and $\omega^3{}_i=-s_i a e^i$, where $i=0,1,2$ and $(s_0,s_1,s_2)=(-1,1,1)$. All other <connection 1-forms> vanish. <Cartan's second structure equation> gives $\Theta^i{}_3=-q e^i\wedge e^3$ and $\Theta^i{}_j=-s_j a^2e^i\wedge e^j$ for $i\ne j$. Lowering the first index makes both the <connection 1-forms> and the <curvature 2-forms> antisymmetric. Contraction yields $R_{00}=q+2a^2$, $R_{11}=R_{22}=-q-2a^2$, and $R_{33}=-3q$.

= Diffeomorphism invariance of general relativity
{parent=General relativity}

Transforming a <metric tensor> and all matter fields together by the pullback of a <diffeomorphism> preserves the <Einstein field equations> and covariant matter equations. Passive coordinate changes only change the description of the fields; active pullbacks produce another representative of the geometry. The treatment of transformations acting on a boundary depends on the boundary conditions and possible charges. A <diffeomorphism> is not necessarily an <isometry> of a fixed metric.

= Timelike geodesic
{parent=General relativity}

A <timelike geodesic> has timelike tangent and vanishing covariant acceleration in an <affine parameter>. Choosing <proper time> gives $g(\dot\gamma,\dot\gamma)=-1$.

= Odd radial perturbations preserve timelike-geodesic periods
{parent=Timelike geodesic}
{title2=$T_\tau=T_t=2\pi$}

For $ds^2=(1+f(x))^2dx^2/(1+x^2)-(1+x^2)dt^2$, with smooth odd $f$ and $|f|<1$, the conserved <energy> gives $dt/d\tau=E/(1+x^2)$ and $(dx/d\tau)^2=(E^2-1-x^2)/(1+f)^2$. Both half-cycle <integrals> over $[-\sqrt{E^2-1},\sqrt{E^2-1}]$ contain an even weight times $1+f$. The odd contribution vanishes, leaving the unperturbed half-period $\pi$ in each clock. The waveforms need not be sinusoidal, but both periods are unchanged for $E>1$.

= Timelike geodesics of an inverse-time conformal metric
{parent=Timelike geodesic}
{title2=$ds^2=t^{-2}(dx^2-dt^2)$}

In a positive-time patch, proper-time first integrals give $\dot x=pt^2$ and $\dot t^2=t^2+p^2t^4$. If $p\ne0$, the unparametrized <timelike geodesics> are $t^2=(x-x_c)^2-p^{-2}$ on an appropriate branch; in the expanded quadratic form their discriminant is positive. If $p=0$, there is the separate family $x=x_c$, $t=t_*e^{\pm\tau}$. A description using only the nonvertical quadratic family omits genuine timelike geodesics.

= Coordinate singularity
{parent=General relativity}

A <coordinate singularity> is a failure of a coordinate description which disappears in a regular chart. The horizon singularity of the <Schwarzschild metric> in $(t,r)$ coordinates is removable; its $r=0$ <curvature singularity> is not.

= Areal radius
{parent=General relativity}
{title2=$r$}

In a spherically symmetric <spacetime>, the <areal radius> $r$ is defined by the area $A=4\pi r^2$ of each symmetry sphere. It is independent of which radial coordinate chart is used.

= Geometrized units
{parent=General relativity}

<Geometrized units> set $G=c=1$, allowing <mass>, length, and time to be expressed in common units. Restore $GM/c^2$ for a mass parameter in a radial <metric tensor>.

= Geometrized luminosity
{parent=Geometrized units}
{title2=$L_{\rm geom}=GL_{\rm SI}/c^5$}

A <geometrized luminosity> is dimensionless because in <geometrized units> an energy and a time have the same dimension. Starting from SI <luminosity>, multiply by $G/c^5$. The corresponding SI power unit is $c^5/G$. Radiating a fraction $f$ of a mass $M$ at constant SI <luminosity> $L$ takes $fMc^2/L$, or $f(GM/c^3)/L_{\rm geom}$ in seconds.

= Vaidya metric
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{title2=$ds^2=-(1-2M(v)/r)dv^2+2dv\,dr+r^2d\Omega^2$}

The ingoing Vaidya metric describes a spherically symmetric spacetime sourced by radial null matter, using advanced time $v$ and areal radius $r$. In geometrized units it is $ds^2=-(1-2M(v)/r)dv^2+2\,dv\,dr+r^2d\Omega^2$. A constant mass function reduces it to the <Schwarzschild metric> in ingoing null coordinates; a varying mass function changes the curvature and the radial <null geodesics>.

= Teleological response of a Vaidya event horizon
{parent=Vaidya metric}
{title2=$\rho(v)=\frac1{2M_0}\int_v^\infty e^{-(s-v)/(4M_0)}\mu(s)\,ds$}

For a small bounded mass perturbation $m(v)=M_0+\mu(v)$ that becomes constant in the future, write the <event horizon> radius as $r_H(v)=2M_0+\rho(v)$. The outgoing <radial null geodesics of the Vaidya metric> give $\rho'-\rho/(4M_0)=-\mu/(2M_0)$ to first order. Future stationarity selects the displayed solution and removes the exponentially growing homogeneous solution. If $\mu'\geq0$, integration by parts gives $\rho-2\mu=2\int_v^\infty e^{-(s-v)/(4M_0)}\mu'(s)\,ds\geq0$. Thus the <event horizon> can grow before local matter arrives, lying outside the instantaneous spherical <apparent horizon> $r=2m(v)$. This is a consequence of the <event horizon>'s global definition, not propagation of a local signal into the past.

= Radial null geodesics of the Vaidya metric
{parent=Vaidya metric}
{title2=$v=\mathrm{const},\quad dr/dv=(1-2M/r)/2$}

= Radial null geodesic of the Vaidya metric
{synonym}

The two radial <null geodesic> families are $v=\text{constant}$ and $dr/dv=(1-2M(v)/r)/2$. The first has affine radius, while the second has tangent $k=\partial_v+(f/2)\partial_r$ obeying $\nabla_kk=(M/r^2)k$, so advanced time is generally a nonaffine parameter. An <affine parameter> $\lambda$ for the outgoing family satisfies $d\log(dv/d\lambda)/dv=-M/r^2$.

= Reconstructing Vaidya mass from a radial null geodesic
{parent=Radial null geodesics of the Vaidya metric}
{title2=$M(v)=\tfrac12r_0(v)(1-2r_0^{\prime}(v))$}

An outgoing <radial null geodesic of the Vaidya metric> prescribed as $r=r_0(v)$ determines the mass along that curve by $M(v)=r_0(v)(1-2r'_0(v))/2$. This inverse relation comes from the radial <null condition>. It determines a function of advanced time wherever the prescribed curve is differentiable and has positive radius.

= Christoffel symbols of the Vaidya metric
{c}
{parent=Vaidya metric}
{title2=$\Gamma^\mu{}_{\nu\rho}$}

Writing $f=1-2M(v)/r$, the radial <Christoffel symbols> are $\Gamma^v{}_{vv}=M/r^2$, $\Gamma^r{}_{vv}=fM/r^2+M'/r$ and $\Gamma^r{}_{vr}=-M/r^2$. The angular couplings are $\Gamma^v{}_{\theta\theta}=-r$, $\Gamma^r{}_{\theta\theta}=-fr$, with $\sin^2\theta$ factors for the corresponding $\phi\phi$ terms, $\Gamma^\theta{}_{r\theta}=\Gamma^\phi{}_{r\phi}=1/r$, $\Gamma^\theta{}_{\phi\phi}=-\sin\theta\cos\theta$ and $\Gamma^\phi{}_{\theta\phi}=\cot\theta$. The lower indices are symmetric. The plus sign of $M'/r$ follows from the ingoing $+2dv\,dr$ cross term.

= Equivalence principle
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

The equivalence principle states that gravity couples universally to energy-momentum and that a freely falling local frame removes uniform gravitational acceleration at a point. Tidal curvature remains measurable over a finite region.

= Comma-to-semicolon prescription
{parent=Equivalence principle}
{title2=$\partial_a\ \longmapsto\ \nabla_a$}

= Comma goes to semicolon rule
{synonym}

The prescription replaces partial derivatives in flat-spacetime matter equations by the <Levi-Civita connection> and the <Minkowski metric> by the curved <metric tensor>. It implements minimal gravitational coupling and reproduces flat-spacetime physics at a point in a <local inertial frame>. It is not a unique rule for rearranged higher-derivative equations: commuting partial derivatives before replacement can conceal a <Riemann curvature tensor> term afterwards. The <derivative-order ambiguity in covariant Maxwell potential equations> is an explicit example. The <equivalence principle> removes uniform gravitational acceleration locally but does not erase tidal curvature over a finite experiment.

= Einstein equivalence principle
{c}
{parent=Equivalence principle}
{title2=EEP}

The <Einstein equivalence principle> combines the <weak equivalence principle> with local Lorentz invariance and local position invariance of nongravitational experiments. In a sufficiently small freely falling laboratory, their results agree with <special relativity> independently of its location and velocity. The <strong equivalence principle> extends this claim to local gravitational experiments.

= Local position invariance
{parent=Einstein equivalence principle}

Local position invariance requires identical local experiments to have the same outcome independently of where and when they are performed. For nongravitational experiments it is part of the <Einstein equivalence principle>. Comparisons of clocks through <gravitational redshift> test this principle once the gravitational potential difference is independently specified; they do not by themselves test the whole <strong equivalence principle>.

= Local Lorentz invariance
{parent=Einstein equivalence principle}

Local Lorentz invariance requires the outcome of a local experiment, expressed in its freely falling apparatus, to be independent of the apparatus's velocity and orientation. In the <Einstein equivalence principle> it applies to nongravitational experiments; the <strong equivalence principle> also includes local gravitational experiments. It is distinct from <local position invariance>.

= Weak equivalence principle
{parent=Equivalence principle}

Freely falling <test particles> with identical initial position and velocity follow the same trajectory independently of their material composition. The principle neglects appreciable self-gravity and nongravitational forces. The <Einstein equivalence principle> extends the local statement to nongravitational experiments; the <strong equivalence principle> also includes gravitational experiments and self-gravitating bodies.

= Eötvös experiment
{c}
{parent=Weak equivalence principle}
{wiki}

A torsion-balance comparison of the gravitational acceleration of bodies of different composition, testing equality of inertial and passive gravitational mass up to a universal proportionality factor. A universal acceleration law independent of test-body mass and composition agrees with this <weak equivalence principle> test. It does not by itself establish the <strong equivalence principle> for self-gravitating bodies.

= Strong equivalence principle
{parent=Equivalence principle}
{title2=SEP}

= Strong principle of equivalence
{synonym}

The strong <equivalence principle> extends universality of free fall to bodies with appreciable gravitational self-energy and requires local experiments, including gravitational ones, in a freely falling laboratory to be independent of the laboratory's location and velocity. This statement neglects external tidal variation across the laboratory; it does not remove <Riemann curvature tensor>. In <general relativity>, universal metric coupling, fixed local gravitational coupling and the absence of extra preferred-frame gravitational fields implement this principle.

= Nordtvedt effect
{parent=Strong equivalence principle}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$m_G/m_I=1+\eta_N E_G/(mc^2)$}

A violation of the <strong equivalence principle> can make the gravitational-to-inertial mass ratio depend on a body's negative gravitational self-energy $E_G$. Bodies with different self-binding fractions would then fall differently in the same external field, even after composition-dependent weak-equivalence effects are removed. For the Earth and Moon this produces a polarization of the lunar orbit toward the Sun, measurable by lunar laser ranging. <General relativity> predicts $\eta_N=0$.

= Absence of a local gravitational stress-energy tensor
{parent=Equivalence principle}

The <equivalence principle> allows <Riemann normal coordinates> in which the first derivatives of the <metric tensor> vanish at a chosen point. A purported local gravitational <stress-energy tensor> built from those first derivatives would vanish there in that chart and therefore, if tensorial, in all charts. Such an expression cannot encode the gravitational energy assigned by a coordinate-dependent field description. This rules out an ordinary universal local covariant gravitational energy density, not the construction of local curvature tensors or boundary and averaged notions of gravitational energy. The <Riemann curvature tensor> still describes invariant tidal gravity.

= Spacetime dimension
{parent=General relativity}

The spacetime dimension is the dimension of the <smooth manifold> used to model spacetime. Ordinary general relativity uses one temporal and three spatial dimensions.

= Killing vector field
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Killing vector
{c}
{synonym}

= Killing field
{c}
{synonym}

A Killing vector field $K$ generates a local one-parameter family of isometries. Equivalently,
$$
\mathcal L_Kg=0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\nabla_{(\mu}K_{\nu)}=0.
$$

= Two-dimensional Killing algebra with a periodic generator is abelian
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$[T,\Phi]=0$}

Suppose the full <Lie algebra> of <Killing fields> has basis $T,\Phi$ and the flow of $\Phi$ has a nonzero period $P$. Write $[T,\Phi]=aT+b\Phi$ with constant coefficients. The <adjoint representation> of the periodic flow satisfies $e^{P\operatorname{ad}\Phi}=1$. Its real eigenvalues are $-a$ and zero, so $a=0$. The remaining adjoint operator is nilpotent and its exponential is $1+P\operatorname{ad}\Phi$, forcing $b=0$. This proves commutation from genuine periodic axial symmetry, without differentiating mere asymptotic component limits.

= Bochner identity for Killing vector fields
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$\int_M|\nabla X|^2\,dV=\int_M\operatorname{Ric}(X,X)\,dV$}

= Integrated Bochner identity for Killing fields
{synonym}

On a closed <Riemannian manifold>, a <Killing vector field> $X$ satisfies $\nabla^*\nabla X=\operatorname{Ric}^{\sharp}X$ for the nonnegative <rough Laplacian>. This follows by differentiating $\nabla_iX_j+\nabla_jX_i=0$, contracting and commuting <covariant derivatives>; the contraction also gives $\operatorname{div}X=0$. Taking the <inner product> with $X$ and using <integration by parts> gives the displayed identity. If <Ricci curvature> is nonpositive, the nonnegative left side must vanish, so $X$ is a <parallel vector field>. In particular every <Killing vector field> on a closed <flat Riemannian manifold> is a <parallel vector field>.

= Killing fields on incomplete manifolds need not generate global isometries
{parent=Killing vector field}

On a geodesically complete <Riemannian manifold>, every <Killing vector field> is complete and integrates to a global one-parameter subgroup of its <isometry group>. On the incomplete interval $(0,1)$ with its Euclidean metric, $\partial_x$ satisfies the <Killing equation>, but translations leave the interval in finite time. The full isometry group is only the identity and reflection. Local solutions of the Killing equation therefore need not equal the Lie algebra of global isometries without completeness.

= Killing transport
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$\nabla_V\xi_a=L_{ab}V^b,\quad\nabla_VL_{ab}=-R_{abc}{}^dV^c\xi_d$}

= Killing transport equations
{c}
{synonym}

The <Killing equation> and the <second covariant derivative of a Killing vector> transport a Killing field and its antisymmetric derivative along any curve. Here $L_{ab}=\nabla_b\xi_a$ and the curvature convention is the one in <curvature-sign convention in Killing derivative identities>. In a parallel frame the equations form a homogeneous linear system of ordinary differential equations. Initial value zero has unique zero continuation. Thus on a connected <pseudo-Riemannian manifold>, not just on a Riemannian manifold, the field is determined by its value and first derivative at one point.

= Dimension bound for Killing vector fields
{parent=Killing transport}
{title2=$\dim\mathfrak{kill}(M,g)\leq n(n+1)/2$}

On a connected $n$-dimensional <pseudo-Riemannian manifold>, evaluation of a <Killing vector field> and its first derivative is injective by <Killing transport>. The value has $n$ components and the <Killing equation> makes the derivative an antisymmetric two-tensor with $n(n-1)/2$ components. Their sum gives the bound. Flat metrics attain it through $\xi_a=A_a+B_{ab}x^b$ with $B_{ab}=-B_{ba}$. Connectedness is essential for a uniform global bound in terms of $n$: independent fields can be supported on different connected components.

= Killing field is determined by its value and first covariant derivative
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$K(p),\quad(\nabla K)_p$}

On a <connected> <Riemannian manifold>, these data determine a <Killing field> uniquely, without a completeness assumption. The <second covariant derivative of a Killing vector> makes its restriction to every <geodesic> a <Jacobi field>. The difference of two fields with identical data has zero Jacobi initial values and vanishes in a <convex normal neighborhood>. The set on which both its value and <covariant derivative> vanish is closed by continuity and open by the same local argument, so connectedness makes it the whole manifold.

= Killing-flow four-acceleration
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$a_a=-\nabla_a\log\sqrt{K^bK_b}$}

With <metric signature> $(+---)$, normalize a timelike <Killing vector field> by $U^a=K^a/N$, $N^2=K^aK_a$. The <Killing equation> gives $K^b\nabla_bK_a=-\tfrac12\nabla_aN^2$, while $K^b\nabla_bN=0$. Thus the <four-acceleration> of its normalized flow is the displayed gradient. With <metric signature> $(-+++)$ the corresponding lower-index formula has the opposite sign.

= Papapetrou electromagnetic field
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$F=dK^\flat$}

= Killing-generated Maxwell field
{c}
{synonym}

For a <Killing vector field> $K$, its metric-dual <differential one-form> produces the <electromagnetic field> $F_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}K_{b]}=2\nabla_aK_b$. The <exterior derivative> gives $dF=0$, while the <second covariant derivative of a Killing vector> gives $\nabla^aF_{ab}=-2R_{bd}K^d$. Thus this construction solves the source-free <Maxwell equations> in a <vacuum spacetime> with zero <cosmological constant>. The field is a test field: adding its <stress-energy tensor> would generally change the original <metric tensor>.

= Kaluza-Klein decomposition along a Killing field
{parent=Killing vector field}
{c}
{title2=$h=V(dx+A)^2+\gamma$}

Where a <Killing vector field> is $\partial_x$ and its squared norm $V=h_{xx}$ is nonzero, an invariant <metric tensor> decomposes as $h=V(dx+A)^2+\gamma$. The quotient one-form has $A_i=h_{xi}/V$ and the quotient metric has $\gamma_{ij}=h_{ij}-h_{xi}h_{xj}/V$. All coefficients are independent of $x$. Changing the fibre coordinate to $x'=x+\lambda$ sends $A$ to $A-d\lambda$. A null Killing direction requires a different decomposition.

= Kaluza-Klein geodesic Lorentz force
{c}
{parent=Kaluza-Klein decomposition along a Killing field}
{title2=$\frac{D\dot x^i}{ds}=kF^i{}_j\dot x^j$}

For $ds_5^2=\gamma_{ij}dx^idx^j+(dz+A_idx^i)^2$ with $z$-independent coefficients, the <geodesic Lagrangian> makes $k=\dot z+A_i\dot x^i$ constant. Its remaining <Euler-Lagrange equations> give the displayed <Lorentz force>, where $F=dA$ and $D$ uses the <Levi-Civita connection> of $\gamma$. For a particle of five-dimensional mass $M$ with proper-time parameter $s$, the conserved momentum is $p_z=Mk$ and the four-dimensional rest mass is $m=M\sqrt{1+k^2}$. Reparametrizing by four-dimensional proper time gives $Du^i/d\tau=(p_z/m)F^i{}_ju^j$.

= Heisenberg metric Kaluza-Klein reduction
{parent=Kaluza-Klein decomposition along a Killing field}
{c}
{title2=$V=\alpha+\chi y^2$}

For $h=\alpha dx^2+\beta dy^2+\chi(dz-y\,dx)^2$, the <Kaluza-Klein decomposition along a Killing field> $\partial_x$ has $V=\alpha+\chi y^2$, $A=-\chi y\,dz/V$ and $\gamma=\beta dy^2+\alpha\chi\,dz^2/V$. For a <Riemannian metric>, positive constants make the fibre norm and quotient metric positive everywhere.

= Euclidean Killing vector field
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}
{title2=$K(x)=A+Bx,\quad B^T=-B$}

Every <Killing vector field> on <Euclidean space> is the sum of a constant translation and a constant infinitesimal rotation. The <second covariant derivative of a Killing vector> vanishes in flat space, so its components are affine; the <Killing equation> makes their linear coefficient an <antisymmetric matrix>.

= Helical orbit of a Euclidean Killing vector
{parent=Euclidean Killing vector field}

An infinitesimal rotation in one coordinate plane plus a constant translation has <integral curves of a vector field> that rotate about a shifted centre in that plane and move uniformly in the remaining directions. Nonzero transverse drift gives helices; zero drift gives circles, with zero radius allowing straight or stationary curves.

= Killing equation
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}

Killing's equation is $\nabla_{(a}K_{b)}=0$. It says that the infinitesimal flow of $K$ preserves the metric.

= Killing derivative contraction identity
{c}
{parent=Killing equation}
{title2=$K_aF^{bc}F_{bc}=3F^{bc}K_{[a}F_{bc]}-2F_{sa}a^s$}

For a <Killing vector field>, set $F_{ab}=\nabla_aK_b$ and $a^s=K^t\nabla_tK^s$. The <Killing equation> makes $F$ antisymmetric, and expansion of normalized three-index antisymmetrization gives the displayed identity. On a <Killing horizon>, <hypersurface orthogonality> removes its first right-hand term and relates $F^{ab}F_{ab}$ to <surface gravity>.

= Second covariant derivative of a Killing vector
{parent=Killing equation}

For a <Killing vector field> and the curvature convention $[\nabla_\mu,\nabla_\nu]V^\alpha=R^\alpha{}_{\beta\mu\nu}V^\beta$,
$$
\nabla_\nu\nabla_\mu K^\alpha=R^\alpha{}_{\mu\nu\beta}K^\beta,
\qquad
\nabla_\mu\nabla^\mu K^\alpha=-R^\alpha{}_{\beta}K^\beta.
$$
The identity follows by combining the <Ricci identity>, <Killing equation>, and <first Bianchi identity>.

= Curvature-sign convention in Killing derivative identities
{parent=Second covariant derivative of a Killing vector}
{title2=$k_{a;bc}=-C_{abc}{}^d k_d$}

Let $C^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_d\Gamma^a_{bc}-\partial_c\Gamma^a_{bd}-\Gamma^a_{ec}\Gamma^e_{bd}+\Gamma^a_{ed}\Gamma^e_{bc}$, with all other index positions obtained by the <metric tensor>. This convention is the negative of the convention $[\nabla_c,\nabla_d]V^a=R^a{}_{bcd}V^b$. The <Ricci identity> is $k_{a;bc}-k_{a;cb}=C_a{}^d{}_{bc}k_d$, with $k_{a;bc}=\nabla_c\nabla_b k_a$. For a <Killing vector>, the <Killing equation> makes this second derivative antisymmetric in $a,b$. Applying the commutator three times gives $2k_{a;bc}=(C_a{}^d{}_{bc}+C_c{}^d{}_{ba}-C_b{}^d{}_{ac})k_d=-2C_{abc}{}^d k_d$, where the last step uses the <first Bianchi identity>. Switching the sign of the <Riemann curvature tensor> switches the sign in this identity; moving a raised index by ordinary metric contraction does not switch it.

= Killing vector preserves scalar curvature
{parent=Second covariant derivative of a Killing vector}

Every <Killing vector field> $K$ preserves the <scalar curvature>: $\mathcal L_KR=K^\alpha\nabla_\alpha R=0$. One index proof rewrites this derivative as a doubly contracted curvature commutator acting on the antisymmetric tensor $\nabla_{[\mu}K_{\lambda]}$; both terms vanish by symmetry of the <Ricci tensor>.

= Geodesic conserved quantity from a Killing vector
{parent=Killing vector field}

= Geodesic conserved quantities from Killing vectors
{synonym}

If $U$ is the tangent to an affinely parametrized geodesic and $K$ is a Killing vector, then $g(K,U)$ is constant along the geodesic.

= Killing energy
{parent=Geodesic conserved quantity from a Killing vector}
{c}
{title2=$E=-K_a u^a$}

For a geodesic tangent $u$ and a stationary <Killing vector field> $K$, the <Killing energy> is $E=-g(K,u)$. It is conserved along the <geodesic>. When $K$ is future timelike and $u$ is future causal, $E>0$ for a nonzero tangent; it need not be positive where $K$ becomes spacelike.

= Charged-particle conserved quantity from a Killing symmetry
{parent=Geodesic conserved quantity from a Killing vector}
{title2=$mV_au^a-q\Phi$}

Suppose $V$ is a <Killing vector field>, $F$ is an <electromagnetic field tensor> satisfying $dF=0$ and $\mathcal L_VF=0$, and a particle of nonzero mass $m$ obeys the <Lorentz force> equation $\nabla_uu=(q/m)F^a{}_bu^b$. <Cartan's magic formula> gives $d(\iota_VF)=0$, so the <local potential of a closed differential one-form> proof gives $d\Phi=\iota_VF$ locally. The <Killing equation> eliminates $u^au^b\nabla_aV_b$, and the force term gives $d(V\cdot u)/d\tau=(q/m)d\Phi/d\tau$. Therefore $mV\cdot u-q\Phi$ is locally conserved. The sign assumes contraction in the first argument of $F$. A global potential additionally requires the closed one-form to have zero periods; invariance alone is insufficient without the homogeneous <Maxwell equations>.

= Stress-energy current from a Killing vector
{title2=$J^a=T^a{}_bK^b$}
{parent=Killing vector field}

If $T^{ab}$ is symmetric and covariantly conserved and $K$ is a <Killing vector field>, then $J^a=T^a{}_bK^b$ is conserved. Its flux through a spacelike hypersurface is the energy or momentum charge generated by the isometry.

= Komar charge
{c}
{parent=Killing vector field}

= Komar integral
{c}
{synonym}

A Komar integral constructs a conserved gravitational charge from the exterior derivative of the one-form dual to a Killing vector. In four dimensions, a stationary mass is conventionally normalized as
$$
M_K=-\frac1{8\pi}\int_S\star dk.
$$

= Komar current from trace-reversed stress-energy
{c}
{parent=Komar charge}
{title2=$J^\mu=R^\mu{}_\nu\xi^\nu$}

A <Killing vector field> gives an antisymmetric tensor $\nabla^\mu\xi^\nu$ whose divergence is this current, up to overall surface-orientation convention. The four-dimensional <Einstein field equations> express it as $8\pi G(T^\mu{}_\nu-\tfrac12T\delta^\mu{}_\nu)\xi^\nu$ at zero cosmological constant. The <contracted Bianchi identity> and Killing invariance of <scalar curvature> prove its divergence is zero. Stress conservation alone does not cancel the trace derivative without Killing invariance of the trace.

= Komar angular momentum
{c}
{parent=Komar charge}
{title2=$J=(16\pi)^{-1}\int_S\star dm$}

<Komar angular momentum> is the <Komar charge> associated with an axial <Killing vector field> in four-dimensional <general relativity>. In geometrized units its normalization is $(16\pi)^{-1}\int_S\star dm$, where $m$ is the metric-dual <differential one-form>. With zero <cosmological constant>, vacuum <Einstein field equations> make $d\star dm=0$, so homologous surrounding surfaces in the vacuum region give the same charge.

= Angular momentum conservation in an axisymmetric vacuum region
{parent=Komar angular momentum}
{title2=$J_{S_2}=J_{S_1}$}

For an axial <Killing vector> $\xi$ and zero <cosmological constant>, set $F^{ab}=\nabla^b\xi^a$. The <Killing equation> and <Ricci identity> give $\nabla_bF^{ab}=-R^a{}_b\xi^b$. In a vacuum region this vanishes, so <Stokes theorem> makes the <Komar angular momentum> equal on homologous enclosing surfaces. The region may contain <gravitational waves> with nonzero Weyl curvature: Ricci flatness, rather than complete flatness, is what removes the flux. This proves absence of axial angular-momentum transport by exactly axisymmetric gravitational radiation, without assuming that a radiating spacetime is stationary.

= Komar angular momentum with inner boundaries
{c}
{parent=Komar angular momentum}

With zero <cosmological constant> and the component <Hodge star operator> convention $(\star j)_{abc}=\epsilon_{abcd}j^d$, the <Einstein field equations> and <Killing equation> give $d\star dm=-16\pi\star(T_{ab}m^b-\tfrac12Tm_a)$. If $m$ is tangent to a spacelike <hypersurface>, the trace term pulls back to zero. For $\partial\Sigma=S_\infty-\bigcup_iS_i$, <Stokes theorem> yields
$$
J_\infty-\sum_iJ_i=-\int_\Sigma\star(T_{ab}m^b\,dx^a).
$$
The inner <Komar charges> cannot be dropped for a vacuum <Kerr black hole>, whose <angular momentum> is nonzero although its <stress-energy tensor> vanishes. A matter-only volume formula requires a regular filling with no inner boundaries.

= Komar mass
{c}
{parent=Komar charge}
{wiki}

Komar mass is the <Komar charge> associated with a stationary timelike Killing vector, using the normalization appropriate to gravitational mass.

= Komar mass normalization in higher dimensions
{c}
{parent=Komar mass}
{title2=$M=-\frac{D-2}{16\pi G_D(D-3)}\int\nabla^ak^b\,d\Sigma_{ab}$}

For future static-slice normal $u$ and outward radial normal $n$, take $d\Sigma_{ab}=(n_au_b-n_bu_a)dA$, with both antisymmetric components summed. For the <Schwarzschild-Tangherlini metric> the flux is $-\Omega_{D-2}r^{D-2}f'=-(D-3)\Omega_{D-2}\mu$. The displayed dimension-dependent coefficient then reproduces the <ADM mass>. Reversing orientation reverses the flux; including a factor of one-half in the binormal convention also changes its numerical value. A four-dimensional normalization cannot be carried unchanged into other dimensions.

= Static spherical Komar mass in reciprocal radial gauge
{parent=Komar mass}
{title2=$M_K(r)=R(r)^2f'(r)/(2G)$}

For $ds^2=-f(r)dt^2+dr^2/f(r)+R(r)^2d\Omega^2$ and $K=\partial_t$ normalized at infinity, the <Komar mass> on a round sphere is $R^2f'/(2G)$. The future unit normal to a static slice is $f^{-1/2}K$, and the outward unit radial normal is $f^{1/2}\partial_r$; their contraction with $\nabla_aK_b$ gives $f'/2$, whose integral over the sphere yields the formula. The asymptotic mass is its limit at infinity. In the presence of matter, the finite-radius integral need not be constant.

= Background-subtracted Komar energy
{c}
{parent=Komar charge}

For a spacetime with a non-flat reference background, replacing $k$ by $k-\bar k$ removes the background contribution and can make the Komar surface integral finite.

= Conformal Killing vector field
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

A conformal Killing vector field generates a local flow that preserves the metric up to a position-dependent scale factor.

= Conformal Killing equation
{c}
{parent=Conformal Killing vector field}
{wiki}

In $d$ dimensions, the conformal Killing equation is
$$
\nabla_aX_b+\nabla_bX_a=\frac2d g_{ab}\nabla_cX^c.
$$

= Flat conformal Killing integrability
{parent=Conformal Killing equation}
{title2=$\partial_\beta\partial_\gamma\omega_\alpha=(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\partial_\gamma\rho+\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_\beta\rho-\delta_{\beta\gamma}\partial_\alpha\rho)/d$}

For a flat <conformal Killing vector field>, let $\rho=\partial_\mu\omega^\mu$. Differentiate the <conformal Killing equation> in three index orders, add two equations and subtract the third to obtain the displayed identity. Tracing gives $\Delta\omega_\alpha=(2-d)\partial_\alpha\rho/d$, whose divergence implies $\Delta\rho=0$ for $d>1$. Taking the divergence of the original identity gives $(d-2)\partial_\beta\partial_\gamma\rho=-\delta_{\beta\gamma}\Delta\rho$. Thus $\rho$ is affine and $\omega$ is at most quadratic when $d>2$.

= Gravitational wave
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Gravitational radiation
{synonym}

A gravitational wave is a propagating disturbance of spacetime curvature produced by changing mass-energy multipole moments.

= Averaged stress-energy of transverse gravitational waves
{parent=Gravitational wave}
{title2=$t^{\rm GW}_{ab}=\frac1{32\pi G}\langle\partial_aH_{ij}\partial_bH_{ij}\rangle$}

In the weak-field, short-wavelength averaging regime, a <transverse-traceless tensor> strain $H_{ij}$ carries the displayed effective <stress-energy tensor> in units $c=1$. The average spans several wave periods while remaining small compared with the background <curvature> scale. The stress is second order in amplitude, so it is absent from a first-order vacuum <linearized Einstein equations> calculation. For a plane wave, summing the two equal transverse diagonal contributions gives flux $(16\pi G)^{-1}\langle\dot H_+^2+\dot H_\times^2\rangle$. This effective averaged stress is not an arbitrary pointwise gravitational energy <tensor>.

= Axisymmetric radiation carries no axial angular momentum
{parent=Gravitational wave}

An exact axial <Killing vector field> makes <Komar angular momentum> independent of the surrounding vacuum surface. Therefore <gravitational waves> in a configuration with exact <axisymmetry> carry no net <angular momentum> about that symmetry axis, although they can carry <energy>. This is specific to axial <angular momentum>; it is not an absence of radiation.

= Gravitational-wave frequency
{title2=$f_{\rm GW}$}
{parent=Gravitational wave}

The gravitational-wave frequency is the temporal oscillation frequency of a radiative metric perturbation. A rigid source rotating with angular frequency $\omega$ and invariant under a half-turn radiates dominantly at angular frequency $2\omega$.

= Mass quadrupole moment
{title2=$Q_{ij}$}
{parent=Gravitational wave}

The trace-free mass quadrupole moment of a mass distribution is
$$
Q_{ij}=\int \rho(\mathbf x)
\left(x_ix_j-\frac13\delta_{ij}|\mathbf x|^2\right)d^3x.
$$
It is the leading time-dependent mass multipole that can radiate in <general relativity>: mass conservation removes monopole radiation, while momentum conservation removes mass-dipole radiation.

= Stress-energy quadrupole identity
{parent=Mass quadrupole moment}
{title2=$\ddot I^{ij}=2\int T^{ij}\,d^3x$}

For a symmetric <stress-energy tensor> with <compact support> and $\partial_bT^{ab}=0$ in <Minkowski spacetime>, let $I^{ij}=\int T^{00}x^ix^j\,d^3x$. Two integrations by parts give $\dot I^{ij}=\int(T^{0i}x^j+T^{0j}x^i)\,d^3x$ and the displayed identity. Compact support removes the boundary fluxes. The support should stay bounded over the time interval used when differentiating the integrals.

= Second mass moment tensor
{parent=Mass quadrupole moment}
{title2=$I_{ij}=\int \rho(\mathbf x)x_ix_j\,d^3x$}

The second mass moment tensor records the quadratic spatial distribution of mass. Its trace-free part is the <mass quadrupole moment> $Q_{ij}=I_{ij}-\delta_{ij}I_{kk}/3$. The mechanical moment-of-inertia tensor instead equals $J_{ij}=\delta_{ij}I_{kk}-I_{ij}$. A rigid rotation $L$ transforms the second mass moment tensor as $I(t)=L(t)I(0)L(t)^T$.

= Scalar second mass moment
{parent=Second mass moment tensor}
{title2=$I=\int r^2\,dm$}

The scalar second mass moment is the <trace> of the <second mass moment tensor>. The <moment of inertia> about a specified axis instead integrates squared distance from that axis. For an isotropic distribution each axial <moment of inertia> is $2I/3$; the sum over three orthogonal axes is $2I$.

= Quadrupole formula
{parent=Mass quadrupole moment}
{wiki}

For a slowly moving isolated source, the leading gravitational-wave luminosity is
$$
P=\frac{G}{5c^5}\left\langle\dddot Q_{ij}\dddot Q_{ij}\right\rangle,
$$
where $Q_{ij}$ is the trace-free mass quadrupole moment.

= Far-zone quadrupole strain
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$H^{\mathrm{TT}}_{ij}=\frac{2G}{r}\Lambda_{ij,kl}(\mathbf n)\ddot Q^{kl}(t-r)$}

In units $c=1$, a slowly moving isolated source has trace-free <mass quadrupole moment> $Q^{ij}=\int T^{00}(x^ix^j-\delta^{ij}|\mathbf x|^2/3)\,d^3x$. The retarded <linearized gravity> solution and the <stress-energy quadrupole identity> give the displayed physical spatial strain, with $\mathbf n=\mathbf x/r$ and the <transverse-traceless projector> $\Lambda$. If the spatial metric is written $g_{ij}=-\delta_{ij}-2E_{ij}$ for <metric signature> $(+---)$, its trace-free Lorenz-gauge coefficient before the transverse projection is $E_{ij}=G\ddot Q_{ij}(t-r)/r$. Thus $H^{\mathrm{TT}}=2E^{\mathrm{TT}}$. Far distance alone does not permit dropping source-dependent retardation: the quadrupole approximation also requires the source size to be small compared with the radiation wavelength.

= Quadrupole strain scaling of a weak self-gravitating source
{parent=Far-zone quadrupole strain}
{title2=$E=O(\epsilon^2R/r),\quad\epsilon=GM/R$}

For a weakly self-gravitating bound source of size $R$, mass $M$ and nonspherical dynamical <mass quadrupole moment> $Q=O(MR^2)$, the <virial theorem> gives $v^2=O(GM/R)=O(\epsilon)$. Its time scale is $R/v$, so $\ddot Q=O(Mv^2)$. The <far-zone quadrupole strain> then has a spatial half-strain coefficient $E=O(GMv^2/r)=O(\epsilon^2R/r)$ in units $c=1$. The physical transverse-traceless strain is $2E^{\mathrm{TT}}$. Spherical or stationary sources can have zero radiation, so this is a characteristic scale for a changing nonspherical quadrupole, not a positive lower bound. The radiation zone also requires $r\gg R/v$.

= Circular-binary quadrupole radiation
{parent=Far-zone quadrupole strain}
{title2=$\omega^2=Gm/(4R^3),\quad \omega_{\mathrm{GW}}=2\omega$}

Two equal masses $m$ orbiting their <centre of mass> at radius $R$ have separation $2R$. <Newton's law of universal gravitation> gives the displayed orbital frequency. Their <mass quadrupole moment> varies at twice that frequency, producing <gravitational radiation> with two <gravitational wave polarizations>. For inclination $\iota$, its leading plus and cross amplitudes are proportional to $1+\cos^2\iota$ and $2\cos\iota$, respectively. Face-on radiation is circularly polarized; edge-on radiation is linearly polarized.

= Orbital compactness
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$GM/(Rc^2)$}

The dimensionless ratio of gravitational radius to orbital scale measures how relativistic a bound orbit is. In an equal-mass circular binary with individual orbital radius $R$, the leading <quadrupole formula> gives $P=(2/5)(c^5/G)[GM/(Rc^2)]^5$. Compact objects permit smaller noncontact orbits than extended stars and hence larger orbital compactness; near merger the Newtonian approximation must be replaced.

= Equal-mass circular-binary quadrupole luminosity
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$P=\frac{2G^4M^5}{5c^5R^5}$}

For two masses $M$ at opposite points of a circular orbit, each at radius $R$ from the centre of mass, $\Omega^2=GM/(4R^3)$. The trace-free <mass quadrupole moment> oscillates at twice the orbital frequency, and its third-derivative squared contraction is $128M^2R^4\Omega^6$. The <quadrupole formula> gives the displayed phase-independent luminosity. Using separation $2R$ rather than individual radius $R$ avoids a factor-of-32 error.

= Head-on quadrupole radiation from equal masses
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$P=m^5(1/z-1/z_0)/(15z^4)$}

For two equal <point masses> at $\pm z$ falling from rest at $\pm z_0$ under <Newtonian gravity>, in <geometrized units> one has $\ddot z=-m/(4z^2)$ and $\dot z=-\sqrt{m(1/z-1/z_0)/2}$. The only nonzero component of the <second mass moment tensor> is $I_{zz}=2mz^2$, and its third derivative is $-m^2\dot z/z^2$. The trace-free <mass quadrupole moment> and <quadrupole formula> give the displayed power. From infinity, integrating down to $z=z_c$ gives $E=2\sqrt2\,m^{9/2}/(105z_c^{7/2})$. Relative to the total initial mass $2m$ the fraction is $\sqrt2(m/z_c)^{7/2}/105$. The weak-field slow-motion approximation must be distinguished from its extrapolation to a compact endpoint.

= Quadrupole radiation from a harmonically oscillating point mass
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$\langle P\rangle=\frac{16Gm^2L^4\omega^6}{15c^5}$}

For a <point mass> moving as $z=L\cos\omega t$ along one fixed axis, the trace-free <mass quadrupole moment> is $mL^2\cos^2\omega t\,\operatorname{diag}(-1/3,-1/3,2/3)$. Its radiating component has twice the source <angular frequency>. The <quadrupole formula> gives $\langle P\rangle=16Gm^2L^4\omega^6/(15c^5)$ for this contribution in the leading slow-motion <weak-field approximation>. Any physical driver or support has its own quadrupole contribution; this expression is for the prescribed mass alone.

= Gravitational-wave energy and angular-momentum balance
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$\dot E=\Omega\dot J$}

For a slowly evolving circular <binary star> with separation $a$, <reduced mass> $\mu$ and orbital angular frequency $\Omega$, the leading <gravitational-wave> losses satisfy $\dot E=\Omega\dot J$. One direct check uses the standard quadrupole angular-momentum loss law
$$
\dot J_i=-\frac{2G}{5c^5}\epsilon_{ijk}\left\langle\ddot Q_{j\ell}\dddot Q_{k\ell}\right\rangle,
$$
where $Q$ is the <mass quadrupole moment> and $\epsilon_{ijk}$ is the <Levi-Civita symbol>. For $\mathbf r=a(\cos\Omega t,\sin\Omega t,0)$, put $A=\mu a^2$; then $Q_{xx}=A(1/6+\cos(2\Omega t)/2)$, $Q_{yy}=A(1/6-\cos(2\Omega t)/2)$, $Q_{xy}=A\sin(2\Omega t)/2$, and $Q_{zz}=-A/3$. Substitution gives $\dot J_z=-32GA^2\Omega^5/(5c^5)$ and the <quadrupole formula> gives $\dot E=-32GA^2\Omega^6/(5c^5)$. Their ratio proves the balance relation. The same relation follows from the rotating wave's angular harmonic number two and frequency $2\Omega$. The additional angular-momentum flux law, not the energy flux alone, is needed to justify preservation of secular circularity.

= Instantaneous quadrupole luminosity of a Kepler binary
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$L=32G^3M^2\mu^2(v^2-11\dot r^2/12)/(5c^5r^4)$}

For a Newtonian <Kepler orbit>, let $M=M_1+M_2$ be the total <mass> and $\mu=M_1M_2/M$ the <reduced mass>; put $k=GM$, $\mathbf n=\mathbf r/r$, $u=\dot r$ and $\mathbf v_\perp=\dot{\mathbf r}-u\mathbf n$, with $v^2=\dot{\mathbf r}\cdot\dot{\mathbf r}$. In the convention $q_{ij}=\mu(3r_ir_j-r^2\delta_{ij})/2$, differentiating the acceleration $\ddot{\mathbf r}=-k\mathbf r/r^3$ gives
$$
\dddot q_{ij}=\frac{\mu k}{r^2}\left[u(\delta_{ij}-3n_in_j)-6(n_iv_{\perp j}+v_{\perp i}n_j)\right].
$$
The two terms have zero cross <tensor contraction>, with squared norms $6u^2$ and $72v_\perp^2$. Therefore $\dddot q_{ij}\dddot q_{ij}=72\mu^2k^2(v^2-11u^2/12)/r^4$. Substitution into the <quadrupole formula> in this convention, $L=4G\dddot q_{ij}\dddot q_{ij}/(45c^5)$, proves the luminosity. It is nonnegative since $v^2-11u^2/12=v_\perp^2+u^2/12$. The formula uses a weak gravitational field, slow motion and leading radiation order; orbital averaging gives a secular luminosity.

= Gravitational radiation from a rotating triaxial body
{parent=Quadrupole formula}
{title2=$P=\frac{32G}{5c^5}\Omega^6(I_1-I_2)^2$}

For a rigid body rotating around its third principal axis, the anisotropic part of its <second mass moment tensor> oscillates at angular frequency $2\Omega$. The <quadrupole formula> gives the displayed luminosity. Equal transverse principal moments remove this rotating quadrupole and its leading <gravitational wave> emission.

= Gravitational-wave energy flux
{parent=Gravitational wave}

In the radiation zone, the time-averaged energy flux of a weak wave in <transverse-traceless gauge> is
$$
\frac{dP}{d\Omega}
=\frac{c^3r^2}{32\pi G}
\left\langle\dot h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\dot h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\right\rangle.
$$
Combining this with the far-zone quadrupole field and integrating over directions gives the <quadrupole formula>.

= Newman-Penrose formalism
{c}
{parent=Gravitational wave}
{wiki=Newman–Penrose_formalism}

The Newman--Penrose formalism resolves spacetime curvature into scalars by contracting the Weyl tensor with a complex null tetrad.

= Complex null tetrad
{parent=Newman-Penrose formalism}

A complex null tetrad consists of two real null vectors and a complex-conjugate transverse pair, normalized so that the metric is reconstructed from their pairwise products.

= Newman--Penrose scalar Psi4
{title2=$\Psi_4$}
{c}
{parent=Newman-Penrose formalism}

For a null tetrad $(\ell,k,m,\bar m)$, one common curvature-sign convention defines $\Psi_4=C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}k^\mu\bar m^\nu k^\rho\bar m^\sigma$. In an outgoing wave zone it encodes the two transverse <gravitational wave polarizations> as one complex scalar.

= Plane-fronted gravitational wave
{parent=Gravitational wave}

For
$$
ds^2=2\,du\,dv+dx^2+dy^2+H(u,x,y)\,du^2,
$$
the only potentially nonzero Ricci component is
$$
R_{uu}=-\frac12(H_{xx}+H_{yy}).
$$
The vacuum equation is therefore the transverse Laplace equation.

= Ricci tensor of a Brinkmann plane wave
{c}
{parent=Plane-fronted gravitational wave}
{title2=$R_{uu}=H_{xx}+H_{yy}$}

For $ds^2=-2du\,dv+dx^2+dy^2-2H(u,x,y)du^2$, the nonzero connections are $\Gamma^v_{uu}=H_u$, $\Gamma^v_{ux}=H_x$, $\Gamma^v_{uy}=H_y$, $\Gamma^x_{uu}=H_x$, and $\Gamma^y_{uu}=H_y$, with symmetric lower indices. Their traces and quadratic Ricci products vanish, leaving only the displayed Ricci component. Hence the vacuum equations require a harmonic transverse profile. The sign is tied to the metric's $-2Hdu^2$ convention.

= Homogeneous wavefront of a plane gravitational wave
{parent=Plane-fronted gravitational wave}

For a Brinkmann plane wave whose transverse profile is quadratic, Killing fields obtained from solutions of transverse oscillator equations act transitively on every constant-retarded-time wavefront. Each wavefront is therefore a homogeneous null hypersurface.

= Rosen coordinates for a plane gravitational wave
{c}
{parent=Plane-fronted gravitational wave}

Rosen coordinates put a plane wave into the transverse-metric form
$$
ds^2=-du\,dv+g_{AB}(u)dx^A dx^B.
$$
For diagonal $g_{AB}=\operatorname{diag}(f^2,g^2)$, the only potentially nonzero <Ricci tensor> component is $R_{uu}=-f''/f-g''/g$.

= Curvature of a diagonal plane wave in Rosen coordinates
{parent=Rosen coordinates for a plane gravitational wave}
{title2=$\operatorname{Ric}=-(A''/A+B''/B)du\otimes du$}

For $g=-dt^2+A(u)^2dx^2+B(u)^2dy^2+dz^2$, $u=t-z$, with nowhere-zero smooth $A,B$, take $e^0=dt$, $e^1=A\,dx$, $e^2=B\,dy$, $e^3=dz$, $\ell=du$. In signature $(-,+,+,+)$, <Cartan's first structure equation> and <metric compatibility> give the lower-index <connection 1-forms> $\omega_{01}=\omega_{13}=-(A'/A)e^1$ and $\omega_{02}=\omega_{23}=-(B'/B)e^2$. <Cartan's second structure equation> gives $\Theta_{01}=\Theta_{13}=-(A''/A)\ell\wedge e^1$ and $\Theta_{02}=\Theta_{23}=-(B''/B)\ell\wedge e^2$. All other independent <curvature 2-forms> vanish: in particular the $12$ component cancels between the time and longitudinal terms. Contracting $R^a{}_{bad}$ yields $\operatorname{Ric}=-(A''/A+B''/B)du\otimes du$. Thus the <vacuum Einstein equations> are $A''/A+B''/B=0$, while nonzero $A''/A=-B''/B$ gives opposite transverse tidal curvatures and <plus polarization> relative to these axes.

= Gravitational-wave pulse
{parent=Plane-fronted gravitational wave}

A gravitational-wave pulse has curvature supported in a bounded interval of <retarded time>. Spacetime can be flat both before and after the pulse even though freely falling bodies retain a displacement or relative velocity produced while the pulse passed.

= Gravitational-wave memory effect
{parent=Gravitational-wave pulse}
{wiki}

The gravitational-wave memory effect is a lasting change in the relative configuration of freely falling test bodies after a transient <gravitational wave> has passed.

= Displacement memory
{parent=Gravitational-wave memory effect}

Displacement memory is a persistent change in the separation of freely falling bodies after a <gravitational-wave pulse>.

= Velocity memory
{parent=Gravitational-wave memory effect}

Velocity memory is a persistent change in the relative velocity of freely falling bodies after a <gravitational-wave pulse>.

= Covariant derivative
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

A connection defines covariant differentiation by adding one connection term for each contravariant index and subtracting one for each covariant index.

= Covariant divergence formula
{parent=Covariant derivative}
{title2=$\nabla_\mu A^\mu=|g|^{-1/2}\partial_\mu(\sqrt{|g|}A^\mu)$}

The <Christoffel trace identity> converts the <covariant derivative> of a <vector field> into a coordinate divergence weighted by the metric volume density. This is valid for the <Levi-Civita connection>. Applied to the raised gradient of a <scalar field>, it gives $\Box\phi=|g|^{-1/2}\partial_\mu(\sqrt{|g|}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_\nu\phi)$ in Lorentzian signature, the curved <d'Alembert operator>.

= Covariant divergence
{parent=Covariant derivative}

The covariant divergence contracts a covariant-derivative index with a contravariant tensor index. For a mixed rank-two tensor it is $\nabla_\mu T^\mu{}_{\nu}$.

= Tensor type
{title2=$(r,s)$}
{parent=Covariant derivative}

A tensor of type $(r,s)$ has $r$ contravariant and $s$ covariant indices. Taking a covariant derivative adds one covariant index, while an outer tensor product adds the corresponding contravariant and covariant index counts.

= Tensor component transformation law
{parent=Tensor type}

Under a coordinate change $x\mapsto x'$, the components of a type-$(r,s)$ tensor acquire one forward Jacobian for each contravariant index and one inverse Jacobian for each covariant index. For a type-$(1,1)$ tensor,
$$
T^{\mu'}{}_{\nu'}
=\frac{\partial x^{\mu'}}{\partial x^\alpha}
\frac{\partial x^\beta}{\partial x^{\nu'}}
T^\alpha{}_{\beta}.
$$

= Tensor density
{parent=Tensor component transformation law}

A tensor density of weight $w$ transforms like a <tensor> with an additional factor $|\det(\partial x/\partial x')|^w$ under $x\mapsto x'$. A scalar density of weight one can be integrated against the coordinate volume element to give an invariant integral. On a spatial hypersurface, $\sqrt h$ is such a scalar density; hence $\pi^{ij}=\sqrt h p^{ij}$ is a tensor density when $p^{ij}$ is an ordinary tensor. The <Levi-Civita connection> extends to densities and satisfies $D_i\sqrt h=0$.

= Identity tensor
{title2=$\operatorname{id}_{TM}=\delta^\mu{}_{\nu}$}
{parent=Tensor type}

The identity tensor is the canonical type-$(1,1)$ tensor that maps each tangent vector to itself. It pairs a covector $\eta$ and vector $V$ as $\eta(V)$, and its components are the <Kronecker delta> $\delta^\mu{}_{\nu}$ in every coordinate system.

= Covariant derivative of a covariant tensor
{parent=Covariant derivative}

For $W_{\mu\nu}$,
$$
\nabla_\alpha W_{\mu\nu}
=\partial_\alpha W_{\mu\nu}
-\Gamma^\rho_{\alpha\mu}W_{\rho\nu}
-\Gamma^\rho_{\alpha\nu}W_{\mu\rho}.
$$

= Levi-Civita connection
{parent=Covariant derivative}
{c}
{wiki}

The Levi-Civita connection is the unique torsion-free connection compatible with the metric; its coefficients are the Christoffel symbols formed from first metric derivatives.

= Variation of Levi-Civita connection
{parent=Levi-Civita connection}
{title2=$\delta\Gamma^a{}_{bc}=\frac12g^{ad}(\nabla_b\delta g_{cd}+\nabla_c\delta g_{bd}-\nabla_d\delta g_{bc})$}

For a family of <Levi-Civita connections>, the <difference of affine connections is a tensor>. Dividing by the family parameter and taking the <limit> shows that its <variation> is <tensorial>. Vary the <metric compatibility> equation and retain the symmetry of the two lower indices. Adding the two cyclic identities and subtracting the third gives the displayed result. In a normal <coordinate frame> it is the direct <derivative> of the Christoffel formula; its <tensorial> form then holds in every frame.

= Parallel metrics with a common Levi-Civita connection
{parent=Levi-Civita connection}
{title2=$\widetilde g(u,v)=g(Au,v),\quad\nabla A=0$}

Two <Riemannian metrics> have the same <Levi-Civita connection> precisely when their relative positive <self-adjoint> <endomorphism> $A$ is parallel for that connection. <Parallel transport> preserves both metrics. Consequently agreement at one point implies agreement everywhere on a <connected> manifold. Without that agreement, constant scalar multiples always give examples; constant nonproportional Euclidean forms show that a common connection does not force scalar proportionality.

= Fundamental theorem of Riemannian geometry
{parent=Levi-Civita connection}

A <Riemannian metric> determines a unique <torsion-free connection> that is a <metric connection>. Metric compatibility and the torsion identity imply the <Koszul formula>, which proves uniqueness. Conversely the right-hand side of the <Koszul formula> is a smooth one-form in its last vector-field argument. The <musical isomorphism> defines $D_XY$ from it; expanding the <Lie bracket of vector fields> verifies the connection rules, metric compatibility and zero torsion. Thus it constructs the <Levi-Civita connection> intrinsically.

= Lie derivative of an affine connection
{parent=Levi-Civita connection}

The Lie derivative of a connection is the tensor
$$
(\mathcal L_X\nabla)_YZ
=\mathcal L_X(\nabla_YZ)-\nabla_{\mathcal L_XY}Z-\nabla_Y(\mathcal L_XZ).
$$
An isometry preserves the Levi-Civita connection, so $\mathcal L_K\nabla=0$ for a <Killing vector field>.

= Riemann curvature tensor
{parent=General relativity}
{c}
{wiki}

= Riemannian curvature tensor
{c}
{synonym}

= Riemann tensor
{c}
{synonym}

The Riemann tensor measures the failure of covariant derivatives to commute.

= Curvature sign convention
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$R_{\rm reversed}=-R$}

Definitions of the <Riemann curvature tensor> can differ by an overall sign, independent of the <metric signature>. Reversing that definition reverses the <Ricci tensor>, <Ricci scalar> and <Einstein tensor>, as well as the <curvature> side of the <geodesic deviation> identity and the <Palatini identity>. A derivation must choose the contraction and <derivative> ordering explicitly before combining formulas from different conventions. Changing a <metric signature> alone is a separate operation.

= Double divergence of a contravariant tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$T^{ab}{}_{;ab}=T^{ab}{}_{;ba}$}

For a <Levi-Civita connection>, a contravariant rank-two <tensor> satisfies $T^{ab}{}_{;ab}=T^{ab}{}_{;ba}$ without itself being symmetric. The <Ricci identity> gives their difference as $R_{pb}(T^{pb}-T^{bp})$, which vanishes because the <Ricci tensor> is symmetric. Zero torsion alone for a general affine connection does not ensure this symmetry.

= Pair symmetry from the normal-coordinate curvature formula
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$R_{ij,kl}=R_{kl,ij}$}

With convention $R(X,Y)=\nabla_{[X,Y]}-[\nabla_X,\nabla_Y]$, and $R_{ij,kl}=g(R(\partial_i,\partial_j)\partial_k,\partial_l)$, normal coordinates give
$$
R_{ij,kl}=\tfrac12(g_{jk,il}+g_{il,jk}-g_{jl,ik}-g_{ik,jl})
$$
at their center. Symmetry of the metric and commutation of second derivatives prove pair exchange. The opposite curvature convention changes the formula's overall sign but preserves all these algebraic symmetries.

= Bianchi identities from covariant derivative commutators
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

The <first Bianchi identity> follows by applying cyclic curvature commutators to the gradient of an arbitrary <scalar field>: its symmetric Hessian makes the third-derivative terms cancel, leaving $R_{abc}{}^d+R_{bca}{}^d+R_{cab}{}^d=0$. For the <second Bianchi identity>, write the vector-bundle derivative as $D_a=\partial_a+\Gamma_a$, so $[D_b,D_c]=\mathcal R_{bc}$. The operator <Jacobi identity> gives the cyclic sum of $\partial_a\mathcal R_{bc}+[\Gamma_a,\mathcal R_{bc}]$ equal to zero. Restoring the connection terms on the two curvature-form indices gives precisely $\nabla_aR_{bcd}{}^e+\nabla_bR_{cad}{}^e+\nabla_cR_{abd}{}^e=0$: the additional terms cancel because the connection is torsion-free and curvature is antisymmetric in its first pair.

= Curvature commutator is a tensor derivation
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$\Delta_{ab}(Q\otimes S)=(\Delta_{ab}Q)\otimes S+Q\otimes\Delta_{ab}S$}

For a torsion-free <linear connection on a manifold>, the commutator of two <covariant derivatives> obeys the tensor-product <Leibniz rule>: mixed first-derivative terms cancel after antisymmetrizing. It vanishes on <scalar fields>, so $\Delta_{ab}(fQ)=f\Delta_{ab}Q$; its action is therefore pointwise tensorial. Its vector action defines the <Riemann curvature tensor>. Applying the commutator to a scalar contraction proves that every covariant tensor index contributes minus the corresponding curvature action, while every contravariant index contributes plus. Torsion-free does not by itself imply <metric compatibility>.

= Three-dimensional curvature from the Ricci tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$\operatorname{Ric}=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{Riem}=0\quad(n=3)$}

In dimension three the <Riemann curvature tensor> is $R_{ijpq}=g_{ip}S_{jq}-g_{iq}S_{jp}-g_{jp}S_{iq}+g_{jq}S_{ip}$, where $S$ is the <Schouten tensor>. Consequently every three-dimensional <Ricci-flat Riemannian manifold> is a <flat Riemannian manifold>. The implication fails in higher dimensions.

= Kretschmann scalar
{c}
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{title2=$K=R_{abcd}R^{abcd}$}

The Kretschmann scalar is the scalar <tensor contraction> of two copies of the <Riemann curvature tensor>. In <Schwarzschild spacetime>, with metric coefficient $1-2M/r$ and $G=c=1$, it is $48M^2/r^6$. Divergence of this scalar along a curve approaching a proposed regular point proves that there is no twice continuously differentiable, nondegenerate metric extension through that point: such an extension would have continuous finite curvature components and hence a finite scalar contraction. This gives a coordinate-independent test of a <curvature singularity>. Boundedness of this single scalar does not prove regularity.

= Curvature commutator on a covariant tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

= Ricci identity
{c}
{synonym}

For a rank-two covariant tensor, the derivative commutator contributes one curvature action on each index:
$$
[\nabla_\alpha,\nabla_\beta]W_{\mu\nu}
=-R^\rho{}_{\mu\alpha\beta}W_{\rho\nu}
-R^\rho{}_{\nu\alpha\beta}W_{\mu\rho}.
$$

= Curvature commutator on a contravariant tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
