For a type-$(2,0)$ tensor, the <Ricci identity> acts once on each contravariant index:
$$
[\nabla_\alpha,\nabla_\beta]T^{\mu\nu}
=R^\mu{}_{\rho\alpha\beta}T^{\rho\nu}
+R^\nu{}_{\rho\alpha\beta}T^{\mu\rho}.
$$

= Geodesic deviation
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{wiki}

For a family of affinely parametrized geodesics with tangent $U$ and connecting field $J$, the Jacobi equation in the convention used here is
$$
\nabla_U\nabla_UJ=R(U,J)U.
$$
It describes the relative acceleration produced by spacetime curvature.

= Local tidal acceleration scaling
{parent=Geodesic deviation}
{title2=$a_{\mathrm{tidal}}=O(\mathcal R L)$}

The <geodesic deviation> equation makes the relative gravitational acceleration proportional to the separation $L$ and a typical <Riemann curvature tensor> component $\mathcal R$. Thus tidal acceleration vanishes as $L\to0$ at fixed bounded curvature. A uniform <electric field> can give different accelerations $qE/m$ at the same point, independently of separation. Relative tidal changes accumulated over a fixed long time need not vanish as a fraction of the initial separation; a local spacetime experiment must control its duration as well as its spatial size.

= Conserved deviation energy in constant curvature
{parent=Geodesic deviation}
{title2=$K=|DS|^2-\kappa|S|^2$}

In <constant sectional curvature> $\kappa$, a <deviation vector> orthogonal to a unit <timelike geodesic> obeys $D^2S=\kappa S$, where $D=\nabla_T$. Therefore $K=g(DS,DS)-\kappa g(S,S)$ is constant, since its derivative is $2g(DS,D^2S)-2\kappa g(S,DS)=0$. Its squared length $f=g(S,S)$ satisfies $\ddot f=2K+4\kappa f$. In four spacetime dimensions, $\kappa=R/12$.

= Exponential and oscillatory geodesic deviation
{parent=Conserved deviation energy in constant curvature}
{title2=$D^2S=\kappa S$}

In a <parallel-propagated orthonormal frame>, orthogonal <geodesic deviation> in <constant sectional curvature> reduces to three scalar equations $\ddot S^i=\kappa S^i$. For $\kappa>0$, $S=Ae^{\sqrt\kappa\tau}+Be^{-\sqrt\kappa\tau}$: generic initial data grow exponentially, but the pure decaying mode is a counterexample to universal growth. Initially comoving neighbors have $S=S(0)\cosh(\sqrt\kappa\tau)$. For $\kappa<0$, $S=A\cos(\sqrt{-\kappa}\tau)+B\sin(\sqrt{-\kappa}\tau)$, so the deviation is bounded and oscillatory. Simultaneous focusing requires compatible vector initial data and is not automatic for every oscillatory solution.

= Orthogonality of proper-time deviation vectors
{parent=Geodesic deviation}
{title2=$\frac{d}{d\tau}g(S,T)=0$}

For a <geodesic variation> of unit <timelike geodesics>, <proper time> normalization holds across the entire variation: $g(T,T)=-1$. <Metric compatibility> and $\nabla_TS=\nabla_ST$ then give $d g(S,T)/d\tau=\tfrac12S(g(T,T))=0$. Consequently an initially orthogonal <deviation vector> stays orthogonal. The common normalization is essential: an arbitrary family of affinely parametrized curves with different tangent norms does not imply this conclusion.

= Jacobi field
{parent=Geodesic deviation}
{wiki}

A Jacobi field is the infinitesimal connecting vector between neighboring members of a one-parameter family of geodesics. It satisfies the geodesic-deviation equation along the reference geodesic.

= Jacobi fields vanishing at their initial point
{c}
{parent=Jacobi field}
{title2=$J(t)=(d\exp_p)_{tV}(tD_tJ(0))$}

For $\gamma(t)=\exp_p(tV)$, every <Jacobi field> with $J(0)=0$ is uniquely of the form
$$
J(t)=(d\exp_p)_{tV}(tB),\qquad B=D_tJ(0)\in T_pM.
$$
Differentiate the <geodesic variation> $\exp_p(t(V+sB))$ in $s$ to obtain the formula. Uniqueness of the Jacobi equation with prescribed initial value and derivative shows that all such fields are obtained. A nontrivial field vanishing at $t=L>0$ therefore corresponds to a nonzero vector in the kernel of $(d\exp_p)_{LV}$, giving the <conjugate point> criterion.

= Jacobi curvature operator
{c}
{parent=Jacobi field}
{title2=$\mathcal R_X(v)=R(v,X)X$}

For the <Riemann curvature tensor> convention $R(X,Y)Z=\nabla_X\nabla_YZ-\nabla_Y\nabla_XZ-\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z$, define $\mathcal R_X(v)=R(v,X)X$. Curvature pair interchange makes this operator self-adjoint. If $v\perp X$, then $\langle\mathcal R_Xv,v\rangle=K(v,X)|v|^2|X|^2$. The <Jacobi field> equation is $D_t^2J+\mathcal R_{\dot\gamma}J=0$. The operator $v\mapsto R(X,v)X$ is $-\mathcal R_X$ and is likewise self-adjoint; this sign distinction matters when testing curvature inequalities.

= Jacobi fields from conjugation at a central endpoint
{c}
{parent=Jacobi field}
{title2=$J_X(t)=Xe^{tA}-e^{tA}X$}

For a <Lie group> with a <bi-invariant Riemannian metric>, vary $\gamma(t)=e^{tA}$ by conjugation: $F(s,t)=e^{sX}e^{tA}e^{-sX}$. Its <Jacobi field> is $J_X(t)=Xe^{tA}-e^{tA}X$. If $e^A$ is central, all these fields vanish at both endpoints. Their space has dimension $\dim\mathfrak g-\dim\mathfrak z(A)$, since the kernel is the <centralizer of an element of a Lie algebra>.

= Endpoint-vanishing normal Jacobi fields have dimension at most n-1
{parent=Jacobi field}
{title2=$\dim\{J\perp\dot\gamma:J(0)=J(1)=0\}\le n-1$}

In dimension $n\ge2$, along a nonconstant <geodesic>, such a <Jacobi field> is determined injectively by $D_tJ(0)$, which lies in the $(n-1)$-dimensional normal space. On the round unit sphere the geodesic from a point to its antipode, parametrized with speed $\pi$ on $[0,1]$, has fields $J(t)=\sin(\pi t)E(t)$ for all parallel normal fields $E$, attaining the bound. For a constant geodesic the endpoint-vanishing Jacobi field space is zero.

= Symmetric Hessian of a scalar field
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

For a torsion-free connection, $U_\mu=\partial_\mu\phi$ satisfies $\nabla_\alpha U_\beta=\nabla_\beta U_\alpha$.

= Third covariant derivatives of a scalar
{parent=Symmetric Hessian of a scalar field}

Use the sequential convention $\varphi_{;lmn}=\nabla_n\nabla_m\nabla_l\varphi$ and define $R$ by $X^a{}_{;mn}-X^a{}_{;nm}=R^a{}_{bmn}X^b$. The <symmetric Hessian of a scalar field> gives symmetry in $l,m$, but the <curvature commutator on a covariant tensor> gives $\varphi_{;l[mn]}=-R^p{}_{lmn}\varphi_{;p}/2$. Averaging the three distinct permutations yields $\varphi_{;(lm)n}=\varphi_{;(lmn)}-(R^p{}_{lmn}+R^p{}_{mln})\varphi_{;p}/3$. Thus a scalar's third covariant derivative is not generally totally symmetric.

= First Bianchi identity
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

For a torsion-free connection, $R^\mu{}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+R^\mu{}_{\beta\gamma\alpha}+R^\mu{}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}=0$.

= Second Bianchi identity
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

For the <Levi-Civita connection>, the cyclic covariant derivative of curvature vanishes: $\nabla_{[e}R^a{}_{|b|cd]}=0$. Contracting this identity gives the <contracted Bianchi identity>.

= Weyl tensor
{title2=$C_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}$}
{c}
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{wiki}

The Weyl tensor is the completely trace-free part of the <Riemann curvature tensor>. In four dimensions,
$$
\begin{aligned}
C_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
={}&R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
-\frac12(
g_{\alpha\gamma}R_{\beta\delta}
+g_{\beta\delta}R_{\alpha\gamma}\\
&\qquad
-g_{\alpha\delta}R_{\beta\gamma}
-g_{\beta\gamma}R_{\alpha\delta})
+\frac R6(
g_{\alpha\gamma}g_{\beta\delta}
-g_{\alpha\delta}g_{\beta\gamma}).
\end{aligned}
$$

= Magnetic part of the Weyl tensor
{parent=Weyl tensor}
{title2=$B_{ab}=\tfrac12\epsilon_{acde}C^{de}{}_{bf}n^cn^f$}

= Magnetic Weyl tensor
{synonym}

In four spacetime dimensions, relative to a timelike <unit normal> $n$, define $B_{ab}=\tfrac12\epsilon_{acde}C^{de}{}_{bf}n^cn^f$, with the chosen spacetime <metric volume tensor>. This is the dual tidal part of the <Weyl tensor>. Normal contractions vanish by curvature and volume-tensor antisymmetry. Its sign depends on the specified orientation and dualization convention.

= Electric part of the Weyl tensor
{parent=Weyl tensor}
{title2=$E_{ab}=C_{acbd}n^cn^d$}

= Electric Weyl tensor
{synonym}

Relative to a timelike <unit normal> $n$, the electric part is $E_{ab}=C_{acbd}n^cn^d$. It describes the tidal component of the <Weyl tensor> measured by that observer. Antisymmetry in each curvature pair makes it spatial in both slots; pair symmetry and vanishing traces make it symmetric and trace-free.

= Trace-free property of the Weyl tensor
{parent=Weyl tensor}
{c}

Every contraction of the Weyl tensor vanishes. Contracting its first and third indices directly cancels the Ricci and scalar-curvature terms; antisymmetry in each pair and symmetry under exchanging the two pairs reduce all other contractions to this one or make them vanish immediately.

= Weyl tensor of a conformally flat metric
{parent=Weyl tensor}
{c}

Every <conformally flat metric> in dimension at least four has identically zero Weyl tensor. The Weyl tensor is the obstruction to local conformal flatness in dimensions at least four.

= First-pair antisymmetry of the Riemann tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

Applying the curvature commutator to the parallel metric gives $R_{\mu\alpha\beta\gamma}=-R_{\alpha\mu\beta\gamma}$.

= Ricci tensor
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}
{c}

The Ricci tensor is the contraction $R_{\alpha\beta}=R^\mu{}_{\alpha\mu\beta}$ and is symmetric for the Levi-Civita connection.

= Schouten tensor
{c}
{parent=Ricci tensor}
{title2=$S_{ij}=\frac1{n-2}(R_{ij}-\frac{R}{2(n-1)}g_{ij})$}

For dimension $n\geq3$, the Schouten tensor packages the <Ricci tensor> and <scalar curvature> in the trace part of the <Riemann curvature tensor>. In dimension three it is $S_{ij}=R_{ij}-Rg_{ij}/4$, and the full <Riemann curvature tensor> is reconstructed from it.

= Ricci tensor in two dimensions
{parent=Ricci tensor}

The algebraic symmetries of the <Riemann curvature tensor> leave only one independent curvature component in two dimensions and force
$$
R_{\alpha\beta}=\frac12g_{\alpha\beta}R.
$$
Consequently the <Einstein tensor> vanishes identically.

= Two-dimensional general relativity
{parent=Ricci tensor in two dimensions}

With no cosmological term, the two-dimensional <Einstein field equations> reduce identically on the geometric side to $G_{\alpha\beta}=0$. They impose $T_{\alpha\beta}=0$ and supply no local metric dynamics; the Einstein-Hilbert action is topological.

= Contracted Bianchi identity
{parent=Ricci tensor}

The Ricci tensor and scalar satisfy $\nabla^\alpha R_{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac12\nabla_\beta R$.

= Double divergence of the Riemann tensor
{parent=Contracted Bianchi identity}
{title2=$R^{ik}{}_{mn;ik}=0$}

For the <Levi-Civita connection>, the <second Bianchi identity> gives $\nabla_iR^{ik}{}_{mn}=\nabla_mR^k{}_n-\nabla_nR^k{}_m$. Applying another <covariant derivative> and the <contracted Bianchi identity> reduces its divergence to a commuting scalar Hessian plus curvature commutators on the <Ricci tensor>. The latter are the difference of symmetric tensors: a square of the <Ricci tensor> and $R^{ab}R_{anbm}$, which is symmetric in $m,n$ by curvature pair exchange and Ricci symmetry. Both differences vanish. This identity does not require vacuum field equations and is independent of the overall curvature sign convention.

= Einstein tensor
{c}
{parent=Contracted Bianchi identity}
{wiki}

The Einstein tensor is
$$
G_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac12Rg_{\mu\nu}.
$$
The contracted Bianchi identity and metric compatibility give
$\nabla^\nu G_{\mu\nu}=0$, ensuring compatibility of the Einstein equations with covariant stress-energy conservation.

= Flat FLRW Einstein-tensor divergence
{parent=Einstein tensor}

For $ds^2=-dt^2+a(t)^2d\mathbf x^2$, the nonzero connection coefficients are
$$
\Gamma^t_{ij}=a\dot a\,\delta_{ij},
\qquad
\Gamma^i_{tj}=\Gamma^i_{jt}=H\delta^i_j.
$$
With $G_t^t=-3H^2$ and
$G_i^j=-(2\ddot a/a+H^2)\delta_i^j$, direct substitution into the mixed-tensor covariant derivative gives zero.

= de Sitter scale factor in flat slicing
{c}
{parent=Flat FLRW Einstein-tensor divergence}

The vacuum equation with positive cosmological constant gives
$H^2=\Lambda/3$. Its expanding flat-slicing solution is
$$
a(t)=a_0e^{\sqrt{\Lambda/3}\,t}.
$$

= Constant sectional curvature
{title2=$R_{abcd}=K(g_{ac}g_{bd}-g_{ad}g_{bc})$}
{parent=Ricci tensor}
{wiki}

A pseudo-Riemannian manifold has constant sectional curvature $K$ when $R_{abcd}=K(g_{ac}g_{bd}-g_{ad}g_{bc})$. In dimension $n$, its Ricci tensor and scalar are $R_{ab}=(n-1)Kg_{ab}$ and $R=n(n-1)K$.

= Schur theorem in pseudo-Riemannian geometry
{parent=Constant sectional curvature}
{c}

In dimension greater than two, if sectional curvature is pointwise independent of the two-plane, the contracted Bianchi identity forces that curvature to be constant on each connected component.

The positive-definite specialization is <Schur's lemma (Riemannian geometry)>.

= Penrose wave equation
{c}
{parent=Riemann curvature tensor}

In a Ricci-flat spacetime, the Penrose wave equation is a nonlinear covariant wave equation for the Riemann tensor. Its principal part is $\nabla^e\nabla_eR_{abcd}$ and its lower-order terms are quadratic contractions of curvature.

= Normal coordinates
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Riemann normal coordinates
{c}
{synonym}

= Geodesic coordinates
{synonym}

At any spacetime point, normal coordinates make the metric equal to the Minkowski metric and all Christoffel symbols vanish at that point.

= Fermi coordinates
{c}
{parent=Normal coordinates}
{wiki}

= Fermi normal coordinates
{c}
{synonym}

<Fermi coordinates> transport a local frame along a timelike <worldline>. Along a <timelike geodesic>, a parallel-transported orthonormal frame defines <Fermi normal coordinates> in which the <metric tensor> is Minkowskian and the <Christoffel symbols> vanish on the worldline. Curvature first appears in terms quadratic in the spatial distance from it, so finite laboratories retain <tidal forces>.

= Lorentz frame
{parent=Normal coordinates}
{c}

A <Lorentz frame> at a spacetime event is an orthonormal tangent basis with metric $\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,1,1)$. Future-oriented frames are related by proper orthochronous <Lorentz transformations>. Any such basis can be realized at the event by <normal coordinates>.

= Orthonormal frame in spacetime
{parent=General relativity}

= Orthonormal tetrad
{synonym}

= Orthonormal frame
{synonym}

An orthonormal frame $\{e_a\}$ satisfies $g(e_a,e_b)=\eta_{ab}$. Any two such frames differ by a local Lorentz transformation.

= Parallel-propagated orthonormal frame
{parent=Orthonormal frame in spacetime}

Along a curve with tangent $T$, a frame is parallel propagated when $\nabla_Te_a=0$. Metric compatibility makes every inner product $g(e_a,e_b)$ constant along the curve, so an initially orthonormal frame remains orthonormal.

= Orthonormal coframe in spacetime
{parent=Orthonormal frame in spacetime}

= Orthonormal coframe
{synonym}

= Vierbein
{synonym}

The dual coframe obeys $\theta^a(e_b)=\delta^a_b$ and reconstructs the metric as $g=\eta_{ab}\theta^a\otimes\theta^b$.

= Curved spacetime gamma matrices
{parent=Orthonormal coframe in spacetime}
{title2=$\gamma_a=e_a{}^I\Gamma_I,\quad\{\gamma_a,\gamma_b\}=2g_{ab}\mathbf1$}

= Curved gamma matrices
{synonym}

An orthonormal coframe converts fixed flat <gamma matrices> to coordinate-index Clifford multiplication. Its metric identity gives the curved anticommutation relation. The <spin connection> is chosen so that these matrices are covariantly constant under the combined spin and tensor connection, which is the tetrad postulate in Clifford form.

= Zweibein
{parent=Orthonormal coframe in spacetime}
{title2=$e^A{}_{\mu}$}

A zweibein is a two-dimensional <orthonormal coframe in spacetime>: its components obey $h_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{AB}e^A{}_{\mu}e^B{}_{\nu}$. It converts worldsheet coordinate indices into local Lorentz-frame indices and defines curved gamma matrices $\gamma^\mu=e_A{}^\mu\gamma^A$. The associated <spin connection> supplies the <covariant derivative> of worldsheet spinors. In flat <conformal gauge> the coframe can be constant.

\Include[connection-1-form]{parent=Orthonormal coframe in spacetime}

= Stress-energy tensor
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Energy-momentum tensor
{synonym}

The stress-energy tensor describes local energy density, momentum density, and stress. Compatibility with the <Einstein field equations> requires covariant conservation $\nabla^\mu T_{\mu\nu}=0$.

= Hilbert stress-energy tensor
{c}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T_{ab}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_m}{\delta g^{ab}}\quad(+---)$}

The metric variational stress tensor is defined by the matter action's response to a metric change. With signature $(+---)$ and the corresponding usual matter-action signs, the displayed convention means $\delta S_m=\tfrac12\int\sqrt{-g}\,T_{ab}\delta g^{ab}=-\tfrac12\int\sqrt{-g}\,T^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$. Fix the independent matter variables while varying the metric. Sign conventions must be matched to the action; copying a mostly-plus stress formula into this convention can reverse its energy density.

= Stress-energy tensor of a canonical scalar field
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T_{\mu\nu}=\partial_\mu\phi\partial_\nu\phi-g_{\mu\nu}[(\nabla\phi)^2/2-V]$}

For signature $(+---)$ and $S_\phi=\int\sqrt{-g}[(\nabla\phi)^2/2-V(\phi)]$, the convention $T_{\mu\nu}=2(\delta S_\phi/\delta g^{\mu\nu})/\sqrt{-g}$ gives the displayed tensor. Varying the inverse metric yields the derivative product, while varying the volume element yields the term proportional to $g_{\mu\nu}$. A homogeneous field has energy density $\dot\phi^2/2+V$ and pressure $\dot\phi^2/2-V$.

= Stress-energy tensor improvement
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T\prime{}^{\mu\nu}=T^{\mu\nu}+\partial_\rho B^{\rho\mu\nu}$}

Adding $\partial_\rho B^{\rho\mu\nu}$ with $B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}$ to a <stress-energy tensor> preserves its divergence identically, since commuting partial derivatives annihilates the antisymmetric pair. The total <four-momentum> changes only by a spatial boundary term. Improvements can make local densities symmetric or <gauge invariant>; equality of integrated charges requires the chosen <boundary conditions>. Equation-of-motion terms can also relate representatives <on shell>.

= Improved stress-energy tensor of a free massless scalar
{parent=Stress-energy tensor improvement}
{title2=$\Theta_{\mu\nu}=\partial_\mu\phi\partial_\nu\phi-\frac12\eta_{\mu\nu}(\partial\phi)^2+\frac{d-2}{4(d-1)}(\eta_{\mu\nu}\Box-\partial_\mu\partial_\nu)\phi^2$}

For $d>1$, the displayed <stress-energy tensor> is symmetric and conserved on the free massless <scalar field> equation $\Box\phi=0$. Its improvement is identically divergence-free. Since $\Box\phi^2=2(\partial\phi)^2$ on shell, its trace vanishes. Contracting it with a <conformal Killing vector field> therefore produces a conserved conformal current. In $d=2$ the improvement coefficient is zero: the canonical tensor already has zero trace.

= Stress-energy superpotential
{parent=Stress-energy tensor improvement}
{title2=$B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}$}

A <stress-energy superpotential> is an antisymmetric tensor $B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}$ whose divergence supplies a <stress-energy tensor improvement>. In the <Maxwell field>, the potential-dependent expression $B^{\rho\mu}{}_{\nu}=F^{\mu\rho}A_\nu$ produces the difference between canonical and gauge-invariant <stress-energy tensors> <on shell>. Antisymmetry ensures identically conserved improvement terms.

= Dust stress-energy tensor
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T^{\mu\nu}=mn_0u^\mu u^\nu$}

A pressureless fluid with proper number density $n_0$, rest mass $m$ and <four-velocity> $u$ has tensor $mn_0u^\mu u^\nu$. In its local rest frame there is energy density but no spatial stress. Particle-number conservation and the Lorentz force make its divergence the electromagnetic force density.

= Null dust
{parent=Dust stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T_{ab}=\varepsilon k_ak_b$}

Null dust is matter with a <stress-energy tensor> $T_{ab}=\varepsilon k_ak_b$ for a future-directed <null vector> $k$. For every future-directed <timelike vector> $t$, the energy current is $-T^a{}_bt^b=\varepsilon(-k\cdot t)k^a$. Therefore its <dominant energy condition> holds exactly when $\varepsilon\geq0$, allowing the zero current. Rescaling $k$ rescales $\varepsilon$ inversely by the square and leaves the <stress-energy tensor> unchanged. Ingoing <Vaidya metric> matter has $k=-\partial_r$, $k_a=-(dv)_a$, and $\varepsilon=\dot m/(4\pi r^2)$.

= Momentum density
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$q^{\hat i}=T^{\hat0\hat i}$}

<Momentum density> is <momentum> per unit physical volume. In a local <orthonormal tetrad> of the measuring observer it is $q^{\hat i}=T^{\hat0\hat i}$. A <perfect fluid in general relativity> moving at a small velocity relative to that observer has $q^{\hat i}=(\rho+P)v^{\hat i}$ to first order. For isotropic background <photons>, $P=\rho/3$, so the factor is $4\rho/3$ rather than $\rho$. Integrating the <kinetic stress-energy tensor> gives $q^{\hat i}=\int f p^{\hat i}\,d^3p$.

= Anisotropic stress
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$\pi^{ij}=T^{ij}-\tfrac13\delta^{ij}T^k{}_k$}

In a local <orthonormal tetrad>, <anisotropic stress> is the trace-free spatial part of the <stress-energy tensor>, conventionally denoted $\pi^{ij}$. Some <linear cosmological perturbation theory> conventions instead use $\Pi^{ij}=-\pi^{ij}$ and write $T^{ij}=P\delta^{ij}-\Pi^{ij}$. The sign must be specified before using a <photon Euler equation>. The <photon quadrupole> determines the linear <photon> <anisotropic stress>.

= Stress-energy tensor of a nonminimally coupled scalar field
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{title2=$T_{ab}=\nabla_a\Phi\nabla_b\Phi-\frac12g_{ab}(\nabla\Phi)^2+2\xi[\Phi^2G_{ab}+g_{ab}\Box(\Phi^2)-\nabla_a\nabla_b(\Phi^2)]$}

For action $S=\int\sqrt{-g}[-\frac12(\nabla\Phi)^2-\xi R\Phi^2]d^4x$, <metric variation> defines $\delta S=\frac12\int\sqrt{-g}T^{ab}\delta g_{ab}d^4x$ and gives the displayed tensor. The <contracted Bianchi identity> and the scalar derivative commutator yield $\nabla^aT_{ab}=(\Box\Phi-2\xi R\Phi)\nabla_b\Phi$, so the <stress-energy tensor> is conserved on the field equation.

= Stress-energy conservation
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}

= Covariant conservation of stress-energy
{synonym}

Local energy-momentum conservation is the covariant equation $\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0$. Its normal and spatial projections give energy and momentum evolution equations in a spacetime foliation.

= Noether identity
{c}
{parent=Stress-energy conservation}

A Noether identity is an off-shell differential relation among Euler-Lagrange expressions implied by a local gauge symmetry. Diffeomorphism invariance relates the divergence of the stress-energy tensor to the matter field equations.

= Diffeomorphism identity for a metric action
{parent=Noether identity}
{title2=$\nabla_aE^{ab}\equiv0$}

= Off-shell metric divergence identity
{synonym}

For a diffeomorphism-invariant pure metric action, write $\delta S=-\int\sqrt{-g}E^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$. Substitute $\delta g_{ab}=\nabla_a\xi_b+\nabla_b\xi_a$ for an arbitrary compactly supported <vector field> and integrate by parts. Symmetry of $E^{ab}$ gives $\nabla_aE^{ab}=0$ without using field equations. This <Noether identity> concerns the gravitational variational tensor alone.

= Diffeomorphism Noether identity for a scalar field
{parent=Noether identity}
{title2=$\nabla^aT_{ab}=\frac1{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Phi}\nabla_b\Phi$}

Simultaneously varying a <metric tensor> and a <scalar field> by the <Lie derivative> along a compactly supported <vector field> leaves a covariant matter action invariant. <Integration by parts> gives the displayed <Noether identity>. It implies <stress-energy conservation> when the scalar equation holds without needing to impose the metric field equation.

= Perfect fluid in general relativity
{title2=$T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b+pg_{ab}$}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}

A relativistic perfect fluid has energy density $\rho$, isotropic pressure $p$ and unit timelike four-velocity $u$. Projecting $\nabla_aT^{ab}=0$ along and orthogonally to $u$ gives its energy and Euler equations.

For a <perfect fluid>, the relativistic stress tensor is $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b+pg_{ab}$ in signature $(-,+,+,+)$ with $u^au_a=-1$.

= Canonical scalar stress as a perfect fluid
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$\rho=K+V,\quad p=K-V$}

For a canonical scalar with timelike gradient, its <stress-energy tensor> has perfect-fluid form. In a homogeneous background, $\rho=\dot\phi^2/2+V$ and $p=\dot\phi^2/2-V$. Potential domination gives negative pressure, while the scalar's physical rest-frame <sound speed> is one. A constant negative background $w$ therefore does not force its perturbations to obey the adiabatic barotropic closure $\delta p=w\delta\rho$.

// Target: cosmology.bigb

= Conformal density of a canonical scalar field
{parent=Canonical scalar stress as a perfect fluid}
{title2=$T_{00}=a^2\rho$}

For $ds^2=a^2(d\tau^2-d\mathbf x^2)$ and a homogeneous canonical scalar, $\rho=\phi'^2/(2a^2)+V$ is the physical density, whereas $T_{00}=a^2\rho$. At $w=-1/3$, the kinetic and potential energies are $K=\rho/3$ and $V=2\rho/3$, so $\phi'^2=2a^2\rho/3=2T_{00}/3$. A single power of $\phi'$ cannot replace the square in this energy identity.

// Target: cosmology.bigb

= Canonical vorticity of a relativistic fluid
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$\omega=d(\epsilon u^\flat)$}

The canonical vorticity two-form is $\omega_{ab}=\nabla_a(\epsilon u_b)-\nabla_b(\epsilon u_a)$, with <specific enthalpy per particle> $\epsilon$. Potential flow $\epsilon u_a=\nabla_a\chi$ has $\omega=0$ for a <torsion-free connection>. Contracting this identity with $u^a$ yields $\epsilon u^a\nabla_au_b=\nabla_b\epsilon-u_bu^a\nabla_a\epsilon$, the isentropic <relativistic Euler equation> in signature $(+---)$. This canonical condition does not mean the unweighted four-velocity one-form is closed.

= Variational action for relativistic potential flow
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}

In signature $(+---)$, the matter action $S=\int\sqrt{-g}[p(\epsilon)+n((\nabla\chi)^2-\epsilon^2)/(2\epsilon)]$ uses independent $n,\epsilon,\chi$. Variations of $n,\epsilon,\chi$ give $(\nabla\chi)^2=\epsilon^2$, $p'(\epsilon)=n$, and <particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid> for $u_a=\nabla_a\chi/\epsilon$. Its <Hilbert stress-energy tensor> is $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b-pg_{ab}$. The action describes isentropic potential flow, with vanishing <canonical vorticity of a relativistic fluid>, rather than every vortical perfect-fluid solution.

= Particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$\nabla_a(nu^a)=0$}

The number current is $J^a=nu^a$, where $n$ is the rest-frame <number density> and $u^a$ the unit <four-velocity>. Its covariant continuity equation is $\nabla_aJ^a=0$, equivalently $u^a\nabla_an+n\nabla_au^a=0$. Integration over a spacetime volume and the <divergence theorem> equate the particle numbers crossing its boundary. This conservation law is separate from <stress-energy conservation>.

= Specific enthalpy per particle
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$\epsilon=(\rho+p)/n$}

For <number density> $n$, rest-frame <energy density> $\rho$ and <pressure> $p$, the enthalpy per particle is $\epsilon=(\rho+p)/n$. In an isentropic <barotropic fluid>, $d\rho=\epsilon\,dn$ and $dp=n\,d\epsilon$. It differs from enthalpy per unit mass by the particle mass when that mass is fixed. Using $\epsilon$ as thermodynamic variable gives $n=p'(\epsilon)$ and $\rho=\epsilon p'(\epsilon)-p(\epsilon)$.

= Barotropic relativistic sound speed
{parent=Specific enthalpy per particle}
{title2=$c_s^2=dp/d\rho$}

In units $c=1$, an isentropic <perfect fluid in general relativity> has sound speed $c_s^2=dp/d\rho$. Writing $n=p'(\epsilon)$ gives $d\rho=\epsilon\,dn$ and $c_s^2=n/(\epsilon p''(\epsilon))$. Linearized <particle number conservation in a relativistic fluid> and the <relativistic Euler equation> then yield acoustic waves with this speed. Positive $c_s^2$ is needed for ordinary acoustic stability; a causal fluid model additionally requires $c_s^2\le1$.

= Perfect-fluid alignment in a static spacetime
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$u^a=k^a/\sqrt{k^bk_b}$}

For <metric signature> $(+---)$, a <perfect fluid> has $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b-pg_{ab}$. The <Einstein field equations> and <static Killing field is a Ricci eigenvector> imply $(\rho+p)(k\cdot u)u_{[a}k_{b]}=0$. Two nonzero timelike vectors cannot be orthogonal, so $k\cdot u\ne0$. If $\rho+p\ne0$, the <four-velocity> is parallel to the static <Killing vector>. Choosing both future-directed gives the displayed normalization. For $\rho+p=0$, the <stress-energy tensor> is proportional to the <metric tensor> and does not determine a preferred fluid velocity; the strict enthalpy hypothesis is therefore material.

= Stiff fluid
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{title2=$P=\rho$}

A <perfect fluid> with <equation of state> $P=\rho$ is called a stiff fluid. For positive <energy density>, its constant equation-of-state parameter is $w=1$. The <cosmological continuity equation> gives $\rho\propto a^{-6}$. In a flat universe dominated by this fluid, the <Friedmann equation> then gives $a(t)\propto(t-t_B)^{1/3}$. This statement assumes a nonzero fluid density and negligible other components.

= Homogeneous free scalar as a stiff fluid
{parent=Stiff fluid}
{title2=$a^3\dot\phi=\mathrm{constant}$}

For a spatially homogeneous <minimally coupled scalar field> with zero <scalar potential>, the <stress-energy tensor> has $\rho=P=\dot\phi^2/2$. Its field equation is $\ddot\phi+3H\dot\phi=0$, so $a^3\dot\phi$ is constant and its <energy density> decreases as $a^{-6}$. Thus its homogeneous stress is precisely that of a <stiff fluid>. Spatial gradients would introduce directional stresses, so this equivalence requires homogeneity.

= Cosmological contraction with a free homogeneous scalar
{parent=Homogeneous free scalar as a stiff fluid}
{title2=$a(\tau)=(-\tau)^{1/2},\quad\phi_0=\sqrt{3/2}\log(-\tau)$}

In units $8\pi G=1$, a free homogeneous scalar has $\rho=P=\dot\phi_0^2/2$ and therefore a stiff equation of state. Its equation gives $a^2\phi_0'=\text{constant}$, while the <Friedmann equation> gives $3\mathcal H^2=\phi_0'^2/2$. The displayed contracting branch satisfies both equations for negative <conformal time>. Constant normalizations and an additive scalar shift can be restored.

= Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction
{parent=Cosmological contraction with a free homogeneous scalar}
{title2=$\Phi_k=N_k(-\tau)^{-1}H_1^{(1)}(-k\tau)$}

In <conformal Newtonian gauge>, a free scalar has no linear anisotropic stress. Eliminating its perturbation with the momentum constraint gives $\Phi_k''+3\Phi_k'/\tau+k^2\Phi_k=0$. Substitution of $\Phi_k=(-\tau)^{-1}y(-k\tau)$ gives the order-one <Bessel equation>. The displayed <Hankel function> has positive incoming frequency $e^{-ik\tau}$ at early times.

= Vacuum-normalized Newtonian spectrum in stiff contraction
{parent=Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction}
{title2=$|\Phi_k|^2\sim3\hbar/(8\pi k^4\tau^4)$}

The momentum constraint relates the incoming scalar mode $a^{-1}\sqrt{\hbar/(2k)}e^{-ik\tau}$ to the <Newtonian-potential Hankel modes in stiff contraction>. Large-argument Hankel asymptotics give $|N_k|^2=3\pi\hbar/(32k^2)$. The small-argument behavior $H_1^{(1)}(z)\sim-2i/(\pi z)$ then gives the displayed spectrum. Its dimensional power is $k^{-4}$ and its power per logarithmic interval is proportional to $k^{-1}$. These are Newtonian-potential spectra; a curvature-perturbation spectrum need not have the same exponent.

= Relativistic perfect-fluid energy equation
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}

The timelike projection of stress-energy conservation is $u^a\nabla_a\rho+(\rho+p)\nabla_au^a=0$. It is the local adiabatic first law for a perfect fluid.

= Relativistic Euler equation
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}

The spatial projection of stress-energy conservation is $(\rho+p)u^b\nabla_bu^a=-(g^{ab}+u^au^b)\nabla_bp$.

= Weak-field slow-motion equations for a relativistic fluid
{parent=Relativistic Euler equation}

For $ds^2=(1+2\phi)dt^2-d\mathbf x^2$ with static weak $\phi$, normalization gives $u^0=1-\phi+O(\phi^2,v^2)$ and $\Gamma^i{}_{00}=\partial_i\phi$. Around a homogeneous state, the first-order equations are $\partial_t\delta n+n_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf v=0$, $\partial_t\delta\rho+(\rho_0+p_0)\nabla\cdot\mathbf v=0$, and $(\rho_0+p_0)\partial_t\mathbf v=-\nabla\delta p-(\rho_0+p_0)\nabla\phi$. The inertial density is enthalpy density even though the flow is slow. Only a further nonrelativistic equation of state, $p\ll\rho\simeq mn$, replaces it by mass density.

= Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector
{parent=Relativistic Euler equation}
{title2=$dp=-(\rho+p)d\log N,\quad N=\sqrt{K^aK_a}$}

For <metric signature> $(+---)$, a stationary <perfect fluid> follows a timelike <Killing vector field> $K$ with <four-velocity> $U=K/N$. Its <Killing-flow four-acceleration> is $U^b\nabla_bU_a=-\nabla_a\log N$. The <relativistic Euler equation> and stationarity of the <pressure> give the displayed <hydrostatic equilibrium> equation. This uses stationarity along $K$ and does not require hypersurface orthogonality.

= Stationary radiation density
{parent=Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector}
{title2=$\rho N^4=C$}

The <equation of state> of isotropic <blackbody radiation> is $p=\rho/3$. The <barotropic hydrostatic first integral> gives $\rho N^4=C$. The <Stefan–Boltzmann law> $\rho\propto T^4$ then yields the <Tolman–Ehrenfest relation> $TN=\mathrm{constant}$. Here $N=\sqrt{|K^aK_a|}$ for the stationary timelike <Killing vector field>; its normalization only changes the constants.

= Radiation fluid has no regular free surface
{parent=Stationary radiation density}
{title2=$\rho=C/N^4>0$}

A connected nonvacuum <perfect fluid> with <equation of state> $p=\rho/3$ obeys the <stationary radiation density> law $\rho=C/N^4$ with $C>0$. On a compact region of a regular <spacetime>, a smooth <Killing vector field> has bounded norm. Consequently $\rho$ cannot tend to zero at a finite regular <free surface>. A vanishing timelike norm instead makes the density diverge. A radiation-filled cavity can have a material wall with nonzero boundary <pressure>; this is a different boundary condition.

= Barotropic hydrostatic first integral
{parent=Hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector}
{title2=$\int^p\frac{d\widetilde p}{\rho(\widetilde p)+\widetilde p}+\log N=C$}

For a <barotropic fluid> in <hydrostatic equilibrium along a Killing vector>, assume $\rho+p>0$. Integrating $dp/(\rho+p)=-d\log N$ gives the displayed <first integral>, constant on each connected fluid region. For the <equation of state> $p=w\rho$ with constant $w>0$, it gives $\rho N^{(1+w)/w}=C_1$.

= Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation
{title2=TOV equation}
{c}
{parent=Perfect fluid in general relativity}
{wiki}

The Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation governs a static, spherically symmetric <perfect fluid in general relativity>. In geometrized units, if $m(r)$ is the mass inside areal radius $r$, then
$$
\frac{dm}{dr}=4\pi r^2\rho,
\qquad
\frac{dp}{dr}=-\frac{(\rho+p)(m+4\pi r^3p)}{r(r-2m)}.
$$

= Stellar compactness
{parent=Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation}
{title2=$C(r)=\frac{m(r)}r$}

The ratio of enclosed gravitational mass to areal radius in units $G=c=1$. In a spherical star the local value is $m(r)/r$, and the surface value is $M/R$. Bounds on this ratio constrain relativistic stellar models; it is unrelated to topological compactness.

= Regular central expansion of the TOV equations
{parent=Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation}
{title2=$m=\frac{4\pi}3\rho_cr^3+O(r^5)$}

For a smooth centre with density $\rho_c$ and pressure $p_c$, the <Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation> gives $m(r)=4\pi\rho_cr^3/3+O(r^5)$ and $p(r)=p_c-2\pi(\rho_c+p_c)(\rho_c/3+p_c)r^2+O(r^4)$. A fixed <barotropic stellar equation of state> leaves one physical central parameter; the additive lapse constant fixes time normalization.

= Interior Schwarzschild metric
{c}
{parent=Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation}
{wiki}

= Interior Schwarzschild solution
{c}
{synonym}

The interior Schwarzschild metric is the exact static solution for a spherical fluid of constant density. If the star has mass $M$ and radius $R$, its pressure is
$$
p(r)=\rho
\frac{\sqrt{1-2Mr^2/R^3}-\sqrt{1-2M/R}}
{3\sqrt{1-2M/R}-\sqrt{1-2Mr^2/R^3}}.
$$

= Central pressure of a constant-density relativistic star
{parent=Interior Schwarzschild metric}

The <interior Schwarzschild metric> has central pressure
$$
p(0)=\rho
\frac{1-\sqrt{1-2M/R}}
{3\sqrt{1-2M/R}-1}.
$$
It diverges when $M/R=4/9$, the limiting compactness in <Buchdahl's theorem>.

= Dominant-energy compactness bound for an incompressible star
{parent=Central pressure of a constant-density relativistic star}
{title2=$\frac MR\leq\frac38$}

The <dominant energy condition> for the <interior Schwarzschild metric> reduces to its largest pressure satisfying $p_c\leq\rho$. Substitution of the exact central pressure gives $\sqrt{1-2M/R}\geq1/2$, hence $M/R\leq3/8$. Equality has finite central pressure and is allowed by the non-strict condition.

= Buchdahl's theorem
{c}
{parent=Tolman–Oppenheimer–Volkoff equation}
{wiki}

Buchdahl's theorem gives $2M/R\leq8/9$ for a static spherical perfect-fluid star whose density is nonincreasing outwards, subject to its regularity assumptions. Equality would require divergent central pressure, so every regular such star satisfies $M/R<4/9$.

= High-density core bound from TOV compactness
{parent=Buchdahl's theorem}
{title2=$a<\frac1{\sqrt{3\pi\rho_0}}$}

Let $\rho_0>0$ be a density threshold and let $a,\mu$ be the radius and enclosed mass where a monotonically decreasing stellar density reaches that threshold. Then $\mu\geq4\pi\rho_0a^3/3$. A local compactness bound $\mu/a<4/9$ implies $a<(3\pi\rho_0)^{-1/2}$ and $\mu<4/(9\sqrt{3\pi\rho_0})$, independently of the <equation of state> above the threshold. A total-mass bound also requires suitable control of the known low-density envelope.

= Null energy condition
{title2=NEC}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{wiki}

The null energy condition requires $T_{\mu\nu}k^\mu k^\nu\geq0$ for every null vector $k$. Through the Einstein field equations this is equivalent to $R_{\mu\nu}k^\mu k^\nu\geq0$ when the cosmological term is absent.

= Perfect-fluid null energy condition
{parent=Null energy condition}
{title2=$\rho+p\geq0$}

For $T_{ab}=(\rho+p)u_au_b+pg_{ab}$ and nonzero null $k$, contraction gives $T_{ab}k^ak^b=(\rho+p)(u\cdot k)^2$. A timelike fluid velocity has nonzero scalar product with every nonzero null vector, so the <null energy condition> is exactly the displayed inequality. Individual nonnegativity of pressure or density is unnecessary for null focusing. A <cosmological constant> term has zero null contraction.

= Null energy condition for a canonical scalar field
{parent=Null energy condition}
{title2=$T_{ab}\ell^a\ell^b=(\ell^a\partial_a\phi)^2\geq0$}

The classical <stress-energy tensor of a canonical scalar field> has a gradient-product term and a metric-proportional term. Contracting with a real <null vector> removes the latter, leaving the displayed square for a real field. This proves the <null energy condition> for a minimally coupled canonical scalar, including the massless case. It is not a statement about arbitrary curvature couplings, wrong-sign kinetic terms or a renormalized quantum <stress-energy tensor>.

= Null energy condition for a planar warped spacetime
{parent=Null energy condition}
{title2=$(\log A)''\le0$}

In a <planar warped spacetime>, contraction of the <Ricci tensor> with a <null vector> in an <orthonormal frame> gives $R_{ab}k^ak^b=-2(\log A)''(k^3)^2$. The <Einstein field equations> identify this with $8\pi T_{ab}k^ak^b$; metric terms, including a cosmological constant, vanish in a null contraction. Thus the <null energy condition> is equivalent to concavity of $\log A$ in the proper transverse coordinate $z$. Null directions confined to a planar slice saturate the condition.

= Null convergence condition
{parent=Null energy condition}
{title2=$R_{ab}k^ak^b\geq0$}

The <null convergence condition> requires $R_{ab}k^ak^b\geq0$ for every <null vector>. Through the <Einstein field equations>, it follows from the <null energy condition>, since all terms proportional to the <metric tensor> vanish on a <null vector>.

= Cosmological constant preserves null convergence
{parent=Null convergence condition}
{title2=$R_{ab}\ell^a\ell^b=8\pi G T_{ab}\ell^a\ell^b$}

Contract the <Einstein field equations> with a <null vector>. Both the trace term and the <cosmological constant> term multiply $g_{ab}\ell^a\ell^b=0$ and disappear. The <null energy condition> therefore still implies the <null convergence condition>, independently of the sign of the <cosmological constant>. A <black-hole area theorem> still needs the appropriate global horizon assumptions as well as this local focusing condition.

= Averaged null energy condition
{title2=ANEC}
{parent=Null energy condition}

The averaged null energy condition requires
$$
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}T_{ab}k^ak^b\,d\lambda\geq0
$$
along every complete affinely parametrized null geodesic. It is weaker than the pointwise null energy condition.

= Averaged null energy condition for a nonminimally coupled scalar
{parent=Averaged null energy condition}

For the curvature-coupled scalar equation considered here, contraction with an affine null tangent and integration by parts gives, for $\xi<0$,
$$
\int T_{ab}k^ak^b\,d\lambda
=\int\frac{1+\xi(4\xi-1)\Phi^2}{(1-\xi\Phi^2)^2}(\Phi')^2\,d\lambda\geq0
$$
when $\Phi\Phi'/(1-\xi\Phi^2)$ vanishes at the endpoints.

= Conserved Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy ansatz
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{c}

For $\nabla_\alpha\nabla^\alpha\phi=m^2\phi$, consider
$$
T_{\mu\nu}=\frac12\left[
\nabla_\mu\phi\nabla_\nu\phi
+g_{\mu\nu}\left(A\nabla_\rho\phi\nabla^\rho\phi+B\phi^2\right)
\right].
$$
Covariant conservation for all solutions holds exactly when
$$
A=-\frac12,
\qquad
B=-\frac{m^2}{2}.
$$

= Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy tensor
{c}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}

For a real scalar satisfying $\nabla_a\nabla^a\psi-\mu^2\psi=0$,
$$
T_{ab}=\nabla_a\psi\nabla_b\psi-\frac12g_{ab}\left(\nabla_c\psi\nabla^c\psi+\mu^2\psi^2\right)
$$
is symmetric and covariantly conserved.

= Weak energy condition
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{wiki}

The weak energy condition requires
$$
T_{\mu\nu}X^\mu X^\nu\geq0
$$
for every timelike vector $X$, so every observer measures nonnegative energy density.

= Weak energy condition for a quadratic scalar stress-energy ansatz
{parent=Weak energy condition}

For the general scalar ansatz in <conserved Klein-Gordon scalar stress-energy ansatz>, work in a unit timelike frame. Then
$$
2T_{00}=(1+A)(\partial_0\phi)^2
-A|\nabla_{\rm s}\phi|^2-B\phi^2.
$$
The weak energy condition for arbitrary local field data is therefore equivalent to
$$
-1\leq A\leq0,
\qquad B\leq0.
$$

= Strong energy condition
{title2=SEC}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{wiki}

The strong energy condition requires $(T_{ab}-Tg_{ab}/2)u^au^b\geq0$ for every timelike vector $u$ in four spacetime dimensions. Through the <Einstein field equations>, it implies the timelike convergence condition $R_{ab}u^au^b\geq0$ when the cosmological constant vanishes.

= Dominant energy condition
{title2=DEC}
{parent=Stress-energy tensor}
{wiki}

The dominant energy condition requires the energy flux measured by every future-directed timelike observer to be future-directed and causal. Equivalently, $T_{ab}u^av^b\geq0$ for every pair of future-directed causal vectors $u$ and $v$ under the $(-,+,+,+)$ convention.

= Dominant energy condition for a kinetic scalar field
{parent=Dominant energy condition}

For a <scalar field> with $X=-q^2/2$, Lagrangian $F(X)$ and <stress-energy tensor> $T_{ab}=F\prime q_aq_b+Fg_{ab}$, the inequalities $A=F\prime\ge0$ and $Y=XF\prime-F\ge0$ imply the <dominant energy condition> for any gradient type. In a local <Lorentz frame>, $\rho=A(q_0^2+\sum_iq_i^2)/2+Y\ge0$ and $\rho^2-\sum_iT_{0i}^2=A^2X^2+A(q_0^2+\sum_iq_i^2)Y+Y^2\ge0$.

= Einstein field equations
{parent=General relativity}
{c}
{wiki}

= Einstein equation
{c}
{synonym}

With signature $(-,+,+,+)$ and vanishing cosmological constant, the Einstein field equations are
$$
R_{\mu\nu}-\frac12 g_{\mu\nu}R=\kappa T_{\mu\nu}.
$$
In four spacetime dimensions, contraction with $g^{\mu\nu}$ gives the useful trace relation $R=-\kappa T$.

= Einstein equation in the sequential-derivative curvature convention
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{c}

With signature $(+---)$, let $R^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_d\Gamma^a{}_{bc}-\partial_c\Gamma^a{}_{bd}-\Gamma^a{}_{pc}\Gamma^p{}_{bd}+\Gamma^a{}_{pd}\Gamma^p{}_{bc}$ and $R_{ab}=R^c{}_{acb}$. This curvature is the negative of the convention in which $[\nabla_c,\nabla_d]X^a=R^a{}_{bcd}X^b$. The physical <Einstein field equations> are $G_{ab}+\Lambda g_{ab}=-8\pi G T_{ab}/c^4$. In a static weak field $R_{00}=-\nabla^2\Phi/c^2$; the trace-reversed equation then reproduces the <Poisson equation> $\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G\rho_{\rm mass}$. Metric variation of $\int\sqrt{-g}(R-2\Lambda)/(2\kappa)+S_m$ gives the same sign when $\delta S_m=\int\sqrt{-g}T_{ab}\delta g^{ab}/2$.

= Newtonian normalization of the Einstein field equations
{c}
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{title2=$G_{ab}+\Lambda g_{ab}=\kappa T_{ab}$}

Assume a metric theory in four dimensions, a <Levi-Civita connection>, covariant <stress-energy conservation> and field equations linear in second metric derivatives. The <contracted Bianchi identity> selects $G_{ab}=R_{ab}-g_{ab}R/2$. Its trace-reversed equation is $R_{ab}=\kappa(T_{ab}-g_{ab}T/2)+\Lambda g_{ab}$. In the convention $R_{00}\simeq-\nabla^2\varphi$ and signature $(+---)$, nonrelativistic matter gives $R_{00}\simeq\kappa\rho/2$. The <Poisson equation> $\nabla^2\varphi=4\pi G\rho$ fixes $\kappa=-8\pi G$. Reversing the curvature convention reverses this displayed coupling sign without changing the physical theory. The <strong equivalence principle> alone does not establish uniqueness without the stated additional assumptions.

= Einstein-equation coupling under reversed curvature
{c}
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{title2=$R^a{}_{bcd}\mapsto-R^a{}_{bcd}\implies\kappa\mapsto-\kappa$}

With the metric and <stress-energy tensor> fixed and zero <cosmological constant>, changing the sign convention for the <Riemann curvature tensor> changes the signs of the <Ricci tensor>, <scalar curvature> and <Einstein tensor>. The same physical field equation is therefore written with the opposite coupling sign. For signature $(+---)$, the convention $R^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_d\Gamma^a_{bc}-\partial_c\Gamma^a_{bd}-\Gamma^a_{ec}\Gamma^e_{bd}+\Gamma^a_{ed}\Gamma^e_{bc}$ and $R_{ab}=R^c{}_{acb}$ gives $R_{00}\simeq-\Delta\Phi$ in a static <Newtonian limit>. The trace-reversed equation and $\Delta\Phi=4\pi G\rho$ then require $G_{ab}=-8\pi GT_{ab}$.

= Planar Einstein metric with cubic radial function
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{title2=$ds^2=z^{-2}[-Fdt^2+dx^2+dy^2+F^{-1}dz^2],\quad F=1-\alpha z^3$}

This metric has $R_{ab}=-3g_{ab}$ and <cosmological constant> $\Lambda=-3$ in unit curvature-radius normalization. Its regular static coframe is real when $F>0$ and $z\ne0$. Setting $q=\alpha z^3$, its six independent <curvature 2-form> coefficients are $-(1-q)$ in the $03$ and $12$ planes and $-(1+q/2)$ in the other four planes. Their Ricci contractions cancel $q$. The <Weyl tensor> squared invariant is $12\alpha^2z^6$, so nonzero $\alpha$ does not give constant sectional curvature despite the Einstein equation.

= Lovelock's theorem
{c}
{parent=Einstein field equations}

In four dimensions, a local natural symmetric divergence-free metric tensor involving the metric and its derivatives of at most second order has the Lovelock form $aG_{ab}+bg_{ab}$ with constant coefficients. Generic nonlinear metric <f(R) gravity> instead has fourth-order terms and does not meet that derivative-order hypothesis. For affine $f$, the metric field equation is precisely of Lovelock form.

= Vacuum Einstein equations
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{c}

In a region with vanishing <stress-energy tensor>, the <Einstein field equations> reduce to $R_{\mu\nu}=0$ when the <cosmological constant> vanishes.

= Vacuum spacetime
{parent=Vacuum Einstein equations}
{title2=$T_{ab}=0$}

A <vacuum spacetime> has vanishing matter <stress-energy tensor>. In $D>2$ dimensions, tracing the <vacuum Einstein equations> gives $R=2D\Lambda/(D-2)$ and hence $R_{ab}=2\Lambda g_{ab}/(D-2)$; in four dimensions this reduces to $R_{ab}=\Lambda g_{ab}$. With zero <cosmological constant> it is <Ricci flat>, although its full <Riemann curvature tensor> can be nonzero: a vacuum <Kerr black hole> and vacuum <gravitational waves> illustrate that distinction.

= Einstein-Hilbert action
{c}
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{wiki}

The Einstein-Hilbert action is the integral of the <Ricci scalar> against the metric volume form. Varying it with respect to the metric gives the vacuum <Einstein field equations>, up to a boundary term.

= Einstein-Cartan theory
{parent=Einstein-Hilbert action}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$(e^a,\omega^{ab})$}

An independent <vierbein> and metric-compatible <spin connection> allow <spin> density to source a <torsion tensor>. The connection equation from the <Einstein-Hilbert action> is algebraic in torsion. In the absence of connection-dependent matter it fixes the <Levi-Civita connection>. For a minimally coupled <Dirac field>, eliminating the connection generates a local four-fermion interaction. Thus its equivalent torsion-free second-order <action> includes this contact term; simply dropping it defines a different matter theory.

= 1.5-order formalism
{parent=Einstein-Cartan theory}
{title2=$\delta S/\delta\omega=0$}

Solve the algebraic connection equation for $\widehat\omega(e,\chi)$, but perform the remaining variations as though the connection were independent. The chain-rule contribution $(\delta S/\delta\omega)\delta\widehat\omega$ vanishes on that solution. The resulting equations are those of the connection-eliminated second-order <action>. This is especially useful for local <supersymmetry> calculations, where the gravitino-induced contorsion is quadratic in <fermions> and the omitted induced connection variation would otherwise be lengthy.

= Algebraic torsion of a Dirac field
{parent=Einstein-Cartan theory}
{title2=$K_{abc}=-\kappa^2\bar\chi\gamma_{abc}\chi/4$}

Use $K_{abc}=e_a{}^\mu(\omega-\omega(e))_{\mu bc}$ and $T_{abc}=K_{bac}-K_{cab}$. For the symmetrized Dirac <action> $-e\bar\chi\gamma^\mu\overleftrightarrow\nabla_\mu\chi/2-em\bar\chi\chi$, varying the <spin connection> gives $-e\bar\chi\gamma^{\mu ab}\chi\,\delta\omega_{\mu ab}/4$. Only totally antisymmetric contorsion is sourced. Its <action> is $-eK^2/(2\kappa^2)-eK_{abc}\bar\chi\gamma^{abc}\chi/4$, hence the stated solution. Substitution generates $e\kappa^2(\bar\chi\gamma^{abc}\chi)(\bar\chi\gamma_{abc}\chi)/32$. The torsion is an <auxiliary field>, not an independent propagating degree of freedom.

= First-order three-dimensional gravity
{parent=Einstein-Hilbert action}
{title2=$T^a=0,\quad R^{ab}=-3\lambda E^a\wedge E^b$}

For a nondegenerate <orthonormal coframe in spacetime> $E^a$ and independent antisymmetric Lorentz <connection 1-form>, the action $\int\epsilon_{abc}(R^{ab}\wedge E^c+\lambda E^a\wedge E^b\wedge E^c)$ has independent coframe and connection variations. Since $\delta R^{ab}=D\delta\omega^{ab}$, integration by parts yields a connection equation $\epsilon_{abc}T^c=0$. The three variations of the cubic coframe term give the coframe equation $\epsilon_{abc}(R^{ab}+3\lambda E^a\wedge E^b)=0$. In three dimensions these are exactly the equations displayed above. The solution has <constant sectional curvature> $K=-3\lambda$. Omitting the factor of three changes the cosmological normalization.

= Einstein-Proca theory
{c}
{parent=Einstein-Hilbert action}

Einstein-Proca theory couples <general relativity> to a massive vector field governed by the <Proca action>. Its matter stress-energy tensor is
$$
T_{ab}=F_{ac}F_b{}^c-\frac14g_{ab}F_{cd}F^{cd}
+m^2\left(A_aA_b-\frac12g_{ab}A_cA^c\right).
$$
The <contracted Bianchi identity> makes the Einstein equation compatible with the covariant <Proca equation>.

= Proca stress-energy tensor
{parent=Einstein-Proca theory}

The Proca stress-energy tensor is the metric variation of the matter part of the <Proca action>. It consists of the <electromagnetic stress-energy tensor> plus the quadratic mass contribution $m^2(A_aA_b-g_{ab}A^2/2)$.

= Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory
{c}
{parent=Einstein-Hilbert action}

Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory couples the metric to a scalar field and a Maxwell field with scalar-dependent gauge coupling, typically through $e^{\alpha\Phi}F_{ab}F^{ab}$. Its Maxwell equation is $\nabla_a(e^{\alpha\Phi}F^{ab})=0$.

= Magnetically charged dilaton black hole
{parent=Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory}
{title2=$V=1-r_+/r,\quad R^2=r(r-r_-)$}

The unit-coupling magnetic solution of <Einstein-Maxwell-dilaton theory> has $ds^2=-Vdt^2+V^{-1}dr^2+R^2d\Omega_2^2$, magnetic potential $A=P\cos\theta\,d\phi$, and dilaton $\phi=-\tfrac12\log(1-r_-/r)$, where $r_-=2P^2/r_+$ and $r_+>0$. Its physical asymptotic component has $r>r_-$. For $0\le r_-<r_+$ it has a regular <Killing horizon> at $r_+$, area $4\pi r_+(r_+-r_-)$ and <surface gravity> $1/(2r_+)$. Its asymptotic mass is $r_+/2$. For $r_->r_+$ the zero-area singularity is naked. The limiting case $r_-=r_+$ is singular and must not be treated as a regular nondegenerate horizon.

= Causal structure of the magnetic dilaton black hole
{parent=Magnetically charged dilaton black hole}

For $r_-<r_+$, an ingoing chart with $v=t+r_*$ has metric $-Vdv^2+2dv\,dr+R^2d\Omega_2^2$. In its future interior, $\nabla r=\partial_v+V\partial_r$ is future timelike, so every future causal curve decreases $r$ and cannot reach <future null infinity>. The singularity $r=r_-$ is spacelike. A <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates> extension has the same qualitative <Penrose diagram> as <Schwarzschild spacetime>, with two exterior regions, a future black-hole interior and a past <white hole> interior. The past interior has the same radial interval but is not a <black hole> region; specifying the radial interval alone does not identify the future component. For $r_->r_+$, the physical exterior $r>r_-$ has $V>0$ everywhere and outgoing null rays escape from arbitrarily near a timelike <naked singularity>.

= Geodesic potential of the magnetic dilaton black hole
{parent=Magnetically charged dilaton black hole}
{title2=$\widetilde V=\tfrac12[V(\epsilon+L^2/R^2)-E^2]$}

Rotate any <geodesic> into an equatorial plane of the <magnetically charged dilaton black hole>. Conserved energy and angular momentum are $E=V\dot t$ and $L=R^2\dot\phi$. Normalizing its tangent to $g(\dot x,\dot x)=-\epsilon$, with $\epsilon=1,0,-1$ for timelike, null and spacelike geodesics, gives $\dot r^2=E^2-V(\epsilon+L^2/R^2)$. Thus its radial equation is $\dot r^2/2+\widetilde V=0$. Radial null rays have $\dot r=\pm E$ and use the same <Schwarzschild tortoise coordinate> with $2M$ replaced by $r_+$.

= Extremal dilaton metric photon capture
{parent=Geodesic potential of the magnetic dilaton black hole}
{title2=$B_c=2M,\quad \sigma_{\rm capture}=4\pi M^2$}

For $f=1-2M/r$ and squared areal radius $C=r(r-b)$, an equatorial <null geodesic> obeys $\dot r^2=E^2[1-B^2f/C]$, with <impact parameter> $B=L/E$. At $b=2M$, $f/C=1/r^2$ in the exterior. Its supremum is $1/(4M^2)$ at the null singular boundary, rather than at a regular exterior <photon sphere>. Rays with $B>2M$ turn at $r=B$; rays with $B<2M$ reach the boundary. Assuming those rays are absorbed gives the displayed geometric-optics cross-section despite zero limiting horizon area.

= Komar mass of the magnetic dilaton black hole
{c}
{parent=Magnetically charged dilaton black hole}
{title2=$M_K(r)=r_+/2-P^2/r$}

For the <magnetically charged dilaton black hole>, the metric-dual one-form of $k=\partial_t$ is $k^\flat=-Vdt$. With orientation $dt\wedge dr\wedge d\theta\wedge d\phi$, its <Hodge star operator> gives $\star dk^\flat=-V'R^2\sin\theta\,d\theta\wedge d\phi$. Therefore the <Komar mass> is $R^2V'/2=r_+/2-P^2/r$. Its radial dependence is allowed because the <Ricci tensor> is nonzero; the divergence of $dk^\flat$ is sourced by $R_{ab}k^b$. At the horizon $M_K(r_+)=(r_+-r_-)/2=\kappa A_H/(4\pi)$.

= Trace-reversed Einstein field equations in D dimensions
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{c}

For $D>2$, tracing and substituting back gives
$$
R_{\mu\nu}
=\kappa\left(T_{\mu\nu}-\frac1{D-2}g_{\mu\nu}T\right).
$$

= Ricci curvature sourced by a Klein-Gordon scalar field
{parent=Trace-reversed Einstein field equations in D dimensions}
{c}

For the conserved scalar stress tensor above,
$$
R_{\mu\nu}
=\frac\kappa2\left[
\nabla_\mu\phi\nabla_\nu\phi
+\frac{m^2}{D-2}g_{\mu\nu}\phi^2
\right].
$$

= Metric variation
{parent=Einstein field equations}

For $h_{ab}=\delta g_{ab}$, variation of the inverse metric and metric volume form gives
$$
\delta g^{ab}=-h^{ab},
\qquad
\delta(d\operatorname{vol}_g)=\frac12g^{ab}h_{ab}\,d\operatorname{vol}_g.
$$
The <Levi-Civita connection> varies by
$$
\delta\Gamma^a{}_{bc}=\frac12g^{ad}
(\nabla_bh_{cd}+\nabla_ch_{bd}-\nabla_dh_{bc}).
$$

= Palatini identity
{c}
{parent=Metric variation}
{wiki}
{title2=$\delta R_{ab}=\nabla_c\delta\Gamma^c{}_{ab}-\nabla_b\delta\Gamma^c{}_{ac}$}

For the <Levi-Civita connection>, the difference of two connections is a tensor. Varying the coordinate expression for the <Ricci tensor> then gives the displayed covariant identity. It is the starting point for the <metric variation of scalar curvature>.

= Metric variation of scalar curvature
{parent=Metric variation}

For $h_{ab}=\delta g_{ab}$, the <Ricci scalar> varies as
$$
\delta R=-R^{ab}h_{ab}+\nabla_a\nabla_bh^{ab}-\nabla^a\nabla_ah,
\qquad h=g^{ab}h_{ab}.
$$
The last two terms form a <covariant divergence> and hence contribute only a boundary term to a compactly supported variation of the <Einstein-Hilbert action>.

= Modified gravity
{parent=Einstein field equations}
{wiki}

Modified gravity changes the Einstein-Hilbert action or its field equations in order to produce gravitational dynamics different from general relativity.

= Scalar-tensor theory
{parent=Modified gravity}

A <scalar-tensor theory> uses a <metric tensor> and an additional <scalar field> to mediate gravity. A <scalar> multiplier of the <Ricci scalar> makes the <curvature> coupling depend on that field. The <Brans-Dicke theory> is a standard example; the choice of <scalar> potential and kinetic coupling distinguishes different members of the class.

= Metric variation of a scalar-curvature coupling
{parent=Scalar-tensor theory}
{title2=$E_{ab}=\Phi G_{ab}+\nabla_a\nabla_b\Phi-g_{ab}\Box\Phi$}

With the <curvature sign convention> in which $\delta R_{bc}=\nabla_c\delta\Gamma^a{}_{ba}-\nabla_a\delta\Gamma^a{}_{bc}$, the inverse-metric <variation> of $\int\sqrt{-g}\,\Phi R$ is $\int\sqrt{-g}\,E_{ab}\delta g^{ab}$ plus a <boundary term>, for a <scalar> multiplier held fixed in that variation. The <Palatini identity> expresses the <curvature> variation as divergences of $\delta\Gamma$. Two <integrations by parts> move the <derivatives> onto $\Phi$, yielding the displayed terms. For constant $\Phi$ they vanish. The opposite <curvature> convention instead has $E_{ab}=\Phi G_{ab}+g_{ab}\Box\Phi-\nabla_a\nabla_b\Phi$.

= Brans-Dicke theory
{c}
{parent=Scalar-tensor theory}
{wiki=Brans–Dicke_theory}
{title2=$\mathcal L_g=\Phi R+\omega\Phi^{-1}(\nabla\Phi)^2$}

The <Brans-Dicke theory> adds a dynamical <scalar field> $\Phi$ to a <metric tensor> theory of gravity, with constant dimensionless coupling $\omega$. In a $(+---)$ signature and the reversed-curvature convention used here, the displayed Lagrangian is supplemented by matter and integrated against the <metric tensor> volume density with prefactor $1/(16\pi G)$. Its <scalar> multiplier of the <Ricci scalar> lets the <curvature> coupling vary in spacetime. Both the <metric tensor> and <scalar> are varied; the <scalar equation of Brans-Dicke theory> follows by eliminating <curvature> between the <scalar> equation and the <trace> of the <metric tensor> equation. The original construction is described in \a[https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRev.124.925][Brans and Dicke's 1961 article].

= Scalar equation of Brans-Dicke theory
{parent=Brans-Dicke theory}
{title2=$(2\omega+3)\Box\Phi=8\pi G T$}

Assume four spacetime dimensions, $\Phi\ne0$, constant $\omega$, no <scalar> potential and matter independent of $\Phi$. In the stated action convention, <scalar> <variation> gives $\Phi R+\omega\Phi^{-1}(\nabla\Phi)^2-2\omega\Box\Phi=0$. The <trace> of the <metric tensor> equation gives $\Phi R+\omega\Phi^{-1}(\nabla\Phi)^2+3\Box\Phi=8\pi G T$. Their subtraction proves the displayed <wave equation> with $T=g^{ab}T_{ab}$. The <degenerate coupling of Brans-Dicke theory> needs separate treatment before dividing by its coefficient.

= Degenerate coupling of Brans-Dicke theory
{parent=Scalar equation of Brans-Dicke theory}
{title2=$\omega=-3/2,\qquad T=0$}

At $\omega=-3/2$, the combined <scalar equation of Brans-Dicke theory> becomes the matter consistency condition $T=0$. It does not determine $\Box\Phi$ by division, since the wave coefficient has vanished. The original <metric tensor> and <scalar> <Euler-Lagrange equations> must be retained. Introducing scalar-dependent matter or a <scalar> potential changes the consistency condition.

= f(R) gravity
{c}
{parent=Modified gravity}

An f(R) theory replaces the Ricci scalar in the gravitational action by a smooth function $f(R)$. Its metric field equation is
$$
f'(R)R_{ab}-\frac12f(R)g_{ab}
+(g_{ab}\Box-\nabla_a\nabla_b)f'(R)=8\pi T_{ab}.
$$

= Einstein-frame representation of metric f(R) gravity
{c}
{parent=f(R) gravity}
{title2=$\widetilde g_{\mu\nu}=-f'g_{\mu\nu},\quad\phi=\sqrt{3/2}\log(-f')$}

In the mostly-minus convention with vacuum gravitational action proportional to $\int\sqrt{-g}f(R)$ and $f'<0$, the displayed <conformal transformation> turns its vacuum equation into $\widetilde G_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}(\phi)$, with potential $V=(Rf'-f)/(2f'^2)$ and the <stress-energy tensor of a canonical scalar field>. The conformal Ricci formula cancels the second derivatives of $f'$ against the original field equation. Its remaining derivative tensor is $\partial_\mu\phi\partial_\nu\phi-\widetilde g_{\mu\nu}(\widetilde\nabla\phi)^2/2$. Expressing $V$ as a function of $\phi$ requires a locally invertible $R\mapsto f'(R)$. When $f''=0$, this scalar-tensor parametrization degenerates and introduces no new propagating scalar degree of freedom.

= Einstein metric condition in f(R) gravity
{c}
{parent=f(R) gravity}
{title2=$f(4\Lambda)=2\Lambda f'(4\Lambda)$}

Every four-dimensional Einstein metric with $R_{ab}=\Lambda g_{ab}$ has constant <Ricci scalar> $R=4\Lambda$. The vacuum metric <f(R) gravity> tensor reduces to $(\Lambda f'(4\Lambda)-f(4\Lambda)/2)g_{ab}$, giving the displayed necessary and sufficient condition for a fixed <cosmological constant>. Both function and derivative may vanish, so division by $f'$ is unnecessary. Requiring this for all real $\Lambda$ and a smooth $f$ instead yields $f(R)=CR^2$.

= Normalization of the metric f(R) field equation
{parent=f(R) gravity}
{title2=$A E_{ab}=\frac12T_{ab}$}

For a metric action $S_g=A\int\sqrt{-g}f(R)\,d^4x$ and the usual <stress-energy tensor> convention, covariant metric variation gives $A E_{ab}=T_{ab}/2$, where $E_{ab}=f'R_{ab}-fg_{ab}/2+(g_{ab}\Box-\nabla_a\nabla_b)f'$. With $A=1/(16\pi G)$ this becomes $E_{ab}=8\pi G T_{ab}$; with $A=1$ it becomes $E_{ab}=T_{ab}/2$. The chosen normalization does not alter the vacuum equation.

= Linearized f(R) gravity about Minkowski spacetime
{c}
{parent=f(R) gravity}

If $f(0)=0$, $f'(0)=1$, and $f''(0)=0$, then $f(R)=R+O(R^3)$ near zero. The field equations linearized about <Minkowski spacetime> are therefore the <linearized Einstein equations>; deviations first appear beyond linear order.

= Linearized gravity
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Metric perturbation
{synonym}

Linearized gravity writes $g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}$ with $|h_{\mu\nu}|\ll1$ and discards terms quadratic in $h$ and its derivatives.

= Gauge-invariant scalar-vector-tensor perturbations of Minkowski spacetime
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$\Phi=A-\dot B+\ddot E,\quad\Psi=C-\Delta E/3,\quad V_i=B_i-\dot E_i$}

= Gauge-invariant Minkowski perturbation
{synonym}

For signature $(+---)$, decompose a <metric perturbation> into $h_{00}=2A$, $h_{0i}=\partial_iB+B_i$ and $h_{ij}=2C\delta_{ij}+2(\partial_i\partial_j-\delta_{ij}\Delta/3)E+\partial_iE_j+\partial_jE_i+h_{ij}^{\rm TT}$, where the spatial vectors are divergence-free and $h^{\rm TT}$ is a <transverse-traceless tensor>. Under a passive coordinate shift $\xi^0=\tau$, $\xi^i=\partial_iL+L_i$, with $\partial_iL_i=0$, the changes are $\delta A=-\dot\tau$, $\delta B=\dot L-\tau$, $\delta C=\Delta L/3$, $\delta E=L$, $\delta B_i=\dot L_i$, and $\delta E_i=L_i$. The displayed combinations and $h^{\rm TT}$ are therefore gauge invariant. The decomposition is unique after appropriate spatial decay or mode restrictions; homogeneous and boundary modes require separate conventions.

= Vacuum constraints for gauge-invariant Minkowski perturbations
{parent=Gauge-invariant scalar-vector-tensor perturbations of Minkowski spacetime}
{title2=$\Delta\Psi=0,\quad\Delta V_i=0,\quad\Delta\Phi=0,\quad\Box h_{ij}^{\rm TT}=0$}

With the reversed convention in <curvature-sign convention in Killing derivative identities>, choose the representative $h_{00}=2\Phi$, $h_{0i}=V_i$, $h_{ij}=2\Psi\delta_{ij}+h_{ij}^{\rm TT}$. The linearized Einstein components are $G_{00}^{(1)}=-2\Delta\Psi$, $G_{0i}^{(1)}=-2\partial_i\dot\Psi-\Delta V_i/2$ and $G_{ij}^{(1)}=(\partial_i\partial_j-\delta_{ij}\Delta)(\Phi-\Psi)-2\delta_{ij}\ddot\Psi-(\partial_i\dot V_j+\partial_j\dot V_i)/2+\Box h_{ij}^{\rm TT}/2$. On all space with regular decaying fields, or for nonzero spatial Fourier modes, the vacuum constraints remove the scalar and vector parts, leaving two radiative <gravitational wave polarizations>. Singular Coulomb fields, spatial boundaries and homogeneous modes are not covered by that removal argument.

= Static scalar perturbation of Minkowski spacetime
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$ds^2=(1+2A)dt^2-(1+2C)\delta_{ij}dx^idx^j$}

For static scalar perturbations with no mixed time-space or spatial shear terms, the displayed <metric tensor> uses signature $(+---)$. With the reversed convention in <curvature-sign convention in Killing derivative identities>, its first-order <Einstein tensor> is $\delta G_{00}=2\Delta C$, $\delta G_{0i}=0$, and $\delta G_{ij}=\partial_i\partial_j(A+C)-\delta_{ij}\Delta(A+C)$. These follow by substituting the metric into the first-order <Christoffel symbols> and <Ricci tensor>, discarding products of perturbations. A static source with the <dust stress-energy tensor> obeys $\Delta C=-4\pi G\rho$ and the decaying spatial equation makes $A=-C$. For a point mass the <Newtonian potential of a point mass> gives $A=-GM/r$, $C=GM/r$. The spatial potential is necessary to determine light propagation; a time potential alone is insufficient.

= Linearized Schwarzschild metric
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$ds^2=(1-r_S/r)dt^2-(1+r_S/r)dr^2-r^2d\Omega^2$}

For a static spherical point source, the weak-field solution in isotropic radius $\rho$ has $g_{tt}=1-2GM/\rho$ and spatial metric $-(1+2GM/\rho)(d\rho^2+\rho^2d\Omega^2)$. The <isotropic-to-areal weak-field radius change> gives the displayed metric with $r_S=2GM$. Its validity requires $r_S/r\ll1$. Treating it as an exact metric near $r\sim r_S$ can turn omitted terms into large corrections; it does not establish horizons or strong-field photon dynamics.

= Weak-field photon-orbit validity
{parent=Linearized Schwarzschild metric}
{title2=$u=r_S/r\ll1$}

The first-order null-orbit equation $(u')^2=r_S^2E^2/h^2-u^2(1-u)$ formally has a circular solution at $u=2/3$, with $|h/E|=3\sqrt3r_S/2$. Its second-order equation gives a perturbation $\delta u''=\delta u$, hence instability. However, $u=2/3$ is outside the weak-field expansion. The exact <Schwarzschild spacetime> has a corresponding unstable <photon sphere>, but that physical result needs the exact geometry; extrapolating the linearized metric is not a controlled proof.

= Isotropic-to-areal weak-field radius change
{parent=Linearized Schwarzschild metric}
{title2=$r=\rho+GM+O(G^2M^2/\rho)$}

The area of a symmetry sphere in the weak-field isotropic metric is $4\pi\rho^2(1+2GM/\rho)$. Equating it to $4\pi r^2$ defines the <areal radius> and gives the displayed change. To first order $dr=d\rho$, and replacing $1/\rho$ by $1/r$ changes the perturbation only at second order. This explains why the isotropic angular correction disappears in the <linearized Schwarzschild metric> without discarding spatial curvature.

= Quadratic gravitational effective stress-energy tensor
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$t_{\mu\nu}=-G^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}[h,h]/(8\pi)$}

= Quadratic gravitational stress-energy
{synonym}

Expand the <Einstein tensor> about the <Minkowski metric>. The quadratic term in the <metric perturbation> defines $t_{\mu\nu}=-G^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}[h,h]/(8\pi)$, a symmetric tensor relative to the background. For a first-order vacuum solution, the expanded <contracted Bianchi identity> gives $\partial^\mu t_{\mu\nu}=0$. Equivalently, a second-order correction $j$ obeys $G^{(1)}[j]=8\pi t$, whose flat divergence vanishes identically. This conservation is an on-shell statement. The local $t$ depends on perturbative gauge and the background split; conservation alone does not make it a gauge-independent local gravitational <stress-energy tensor>.

= Massless Fierz-Pauli action
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$\delta S=-\int d^4x\,G^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}\delta h^{\alpha\beta}$}

A <massless Fierz-Pauli action> is the quadratic flat-background action for a symmetric metric perturbation that yields the vacuum <linearized Einstein equations>. One normalization, up to a total derivative, is
$$
\mathcal L=-\frac14\partial_\mu h^{\rho\sigma}\partial^\mu h_{\rho\sigma}
+\frac12A_\mu A^\mu-\frac12A^\mu\partial_\mu h
+\frac14\partial_\mu h\partial^\mu h,\qquad A_\mu=\partial_\nu h^\nu{}_\mu.
$$
Varying the symmetric field with compact support gives the displayed linearized <Einstein tensor> coefficient. Its linearized coordinate-gauge symmetry is consistent with the contracted <Bianchi identity>. It is the massless gravitational action, distinct from imposing the massive <Fierz-Pauli equations> with five polarizations.

= Linearized coordinate gauge transformation
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{title2=$h_{\mu\nu}\mapsto h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_\mu\xi_\nu-\partial_\nu\xi_\mu$}

An infinitesimal coordinate change generated by $\xi$ changes a metric perturbation about <Minkowski spacetime> by $h_{\mu\nu}\mapsto h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_\mu\xi_\nu-\partial_\nu\xi_\mu$, in the passive sign convention. For a passive coordinate change $x^\mu\mapsto x^\mu+\xi^\mu$, the first-order change is minus the <Lie derivative of a tensor field> of the background metric, equivalently the pullback by the inverse flow. The <gauge invariance of the linearized Riemann tensor> follows because the added terms have commuting third derivatives, which cancel in the curvature antisymmetrizations. Gauge choices alter the metric components while preserving linearized tidal effects.

= Affine transverse trace in a diagonal linearized plane wave is pure gauge
{parent=Linearized coordinate gauge transformation}

For a perturbation $h_{xx}=2a(t-z)$, $h_{yy}=2b(t-z)$ with all other components zero, the linearized <vacuum Einstein equations> give $(a+b)''=0$. Put $s=a+b$ and choose $\xi_x=sx/2$, $\xi_y=sy/2$, $\xi_t=-s'(x^2+y^2)/4$, $\xi_z=s'(x^2+y^2)/4$. Under the <linearized coordinate gauge transformation>, the mixed time/transverse and longitudinal/transverse components cancel, while the other time/longitudinal changes are proportional to $s''=0$. The remaining components are $h_{xx}=a-b$, $h_{yy}=-(a-b)$, $h_{xy}=0$, a <transverse-traceless gauge> perturbation of <plus polarization>. This explicitly removes a nonzero affine trace without assuming it vanishes by boundary conditions. If $(a-b)''=0$ too, all linearized curvature vanishes.

= Weak-field approximation
{parent=Linearized gravity}

The weak-field approximation requires every dimensionless component of the <metric perturbation> to have magnitude much smaller than one, so terms quadratic in the perturbation may be neglected.

= Linearized Einstein equations
{c}
{parent=Linearized gravity}

In terms of the <trace-reversed metric perturbation> and in the <Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity>, the linearized Einstein equations on Minkowski spacetime are
$$
\Box\bar h_{\mu\nu}=-16\pi T_{\mu\nu}.
$$

= Parallel photon beams in linearized gravity
{parent=Linearized Einstein equations}
{title2=$h_{ik}=H(u,y,z)\ell_i\ell_k,\quad\Delta_\perp H=16\pi G\rho(u)$}

For signature $(+---)$ and the curvature convention giving $\Box\bar h_{ik}=-16\pi G\epsilon^{-1}T_{ik}$, take $u=t-x$, $\ell_i=(1,-1,0,0)$ and $T_{ik}=\epsilon\rho(u)\ell_i\ell_k$. A trace-free Lorenz-gauge particular solution has the displayed form. The vector $\ell^i=(1,1,0,0)$ is null, and $\Gamma^i{}_{jk}\ell^j\ell^k=0$, so co-propagating test photons are undeflected by this beam field. For a transversely uniform source, $H=4\pi G\rho(u)(y^2+z^2)$ is one particular solution. A function of $u$ alone has vanishing wave operator and cannot source nonzero $\rho$; homogeneous gravitational perturbations and boundary conditions remain separate data.

= Radiation boundary condition for linearized gravity
{parent=Linearized Einstein equations}

The retarded source solution of the <linearized Einstein equations> is selected by excluding added incoming or freely specified homogeneous <gravitational waves>. A homogeneous wave can satisfy <Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity> while having no source <mass quadrupole moment>. Thus a formula for the retarded source field is not an identity for every solution of the sourced wave equation.

= Retarded quadrupole field
{parent=Linearized Einstein equations}
{title2=$\bar h_{ij}(t,\mathbf x)\simeq 2\ddot I_{ij}(t-r)/r$}

For a compact slowly moving source in the <weak-field approximation>, with no incoming homogeneous wave and $G=c=1$, the retarded <linearized Einstein equations> give this leading far-zone <trace-reversed metric perturbation>. The key consequence of <stress-energy conservation> is $\ddot I_{ij}=2\int T_{ij}\,d^3x$. The physical <gravitational wave> is obtained by the <transverse-traceless projector>.

= Long-wavelength source approximation
{parent=Retarded quadrupole field}
{title2=$d/\tau\ll1$}

Dropping the position-dependent source delay in the leading far-zone retarded field requires source size $d$ to be small compared with its variation time $\tau$, in units $c=1$. The <retarded quadrupole field> also requires $d/r\ll1$. For a typical nonrelativistic bound source, $\tau\sim d/v$ implies this long-wavelength condition; an arbitrarily rapidly oscillating small displacement is not controlled merely by its instantaneous speed.

= Linearized inverse metric
{parent=Linearized gravity}

If $g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}$, then $g^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}-h^{\mu\nu}+O(h^2)$, with indices on $h$ raised using the background metric.

= Linearized Levi-Civita connection
{parent=Linearized gravity}

About a Cartesian flat background,
$$
\Gamma^\alpha_{\beta\gamma}
=\frac12\eta^{\alpha\rho}
(\partial_\beta h_{\gamma\rho}+\partial_\gamma h_{\beta\rho}-\partial_\rho h_{\beta\gamma})+O(h^2).
$$

= Linearized Riemann curvature operator
{parent=Linearized gravity}

The linearized Riemann tensor defines an operator on two-forms by
$$
(\mathcal R\omega)_{ab}=\frac12R^{(1)}_{ab}{}^{cd}\omega_{cd}.
$$
Unlike the metric perturbation itself, this operator is invariant under linearized gauge transformations.

= Gauge invariance of the linearized Riemann tensor
{parent=Linearized Riemann curvature operator}

Under $h_{\mu\nu}\mapsto h_{\mu\nu}+\partial_\mu\xi_\nu+\partial_\nu\xi_\mu$, every contribution to the linearized Riemann tensor contains three commuting derivatives of $\xi$ and cancels pairwise. Linearized curvature is therefore gauge invariant about flat spacetime.

= Flat linearized metric perturbations are locally pure gauge
{parent=Gauge invariance of the linearized Riemann tensor}
{title2=$R^{(1)}[h]=0\ \Longrightarrow\ h_{ab}=2\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}$}

On a <contractible> Cartesian patch of a flat background, zero <linearized Riemann curvature operator> says that the <connection 1-forms> $\Gamma^{(1)a}{}_{bc}\,dx^c$ are closed. The <Poincaré lemma> writes them as <derivatives> of a matrix-valued potential. Symmetry of the two lower <affine connection> indices then supplies a second potential, giving $\Gamma^{(1)a}{}_{bc}=\partial_b\partial_c\xi^a$. Linearized <metric compatibility> implies that $h_{ab}-2\partial_{(a}\xi_{b)}$ is constant. Its symmetric constant part is absorbed by an affine <vector field>. Thus the <metric perturbation> is locally a coordinate change. Global <topology> and imposed <boundary conditions> can obstruct the corresponding global gauge choice.

= Trace-reversed metric perturbation
{title2=$\bar h_{\mu\nu}$}
{parent=Linearized gravity}

In four dimensions, the trace-reversed perturbation is
$$
\bar h_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}-\frac12\eta_{\mu\nu}h,
\qquad h=\eta^{\rho\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}.
$$
Trace reversal simplifies the linearized Einstein equations.

= Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity
{c}
{parent=Trace-reversed metric perturbation}

The Lorenz condition $\partial^\mu\bar h_{\mu\nu}=0$ reduces the vacuum field equation to $\Box\bar h_{\mu\nu}=0$. A plane-wave amplitude therefore satisfies $k^\mu H_{\mu\nu}=0$ and $k^\mu k_\mu=0$.

= Residual gauge symmetry of linearized gravity
{parent=Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity}

An infinitesimal coordinate change generated by $\xi_\mu$ changes
$$
\bar h_{\mu\nu}\mapsto
\bar h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_\mu\xi_\nu-\partial_\nu\xi_\mu
+\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial_\rho\xi^\rho.
$$
It preserves Lorenz gauge exactly when $\Box\xi_\mu=0$.

= Plane gravitational wave in linearized gravity
{parent=Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity}

= Plane gravitational waves in linearized gravity
{synonym}

A vacuum mode $\bar h_{\mu\nu}=H_{\mu\nu}e^{ik\cdot x}$ has a null wavevector and a transverse symmetric amplitude. Residual gauge freedom can further reduce it to two physical transverse-traceless polarizations.

= Transverse-traceless gauge
{title2=TT gauge}
{parent=Plane gravitational wave in linearized gravity}
{wiki}

Transverse-traceless gauge sets the time and longitudinal components and the spatial trace of a vacuum gravitational-wave perturbation to zero.

= Explicit plane-wave reduction to transverse-traceless gauge
{parent=Transverse-traceless gauge}
{title2=$H_+=(H_{11}-H_{22})/2,\quad H_\times=H_{12}$}

For a <plane gravitational wave in linearized gravity> with $k_\mu=(-\omega,0,0,\omega)$, <Lorenz gauge in linearized gravity> gives $H_{3\nu}=-H_{0\nu}$.
