Under $\delta H_{\mu\nu}=i(k_\mu X_\nu+k_\nu X_\mu-\eta_{\mu\nu}k^\rho X_\rho)$, choose $X_i=H_{0i}/(i\omega)$ for $i=1,2$, $X_3-X_0=iH_{00}/\omega$, and $X_0+X_3=(H_{11}+H_{22})/(2i\omega)$. This <residual gauge symmetry of linearized gravity> removes all time and longitudinal entries and the transverse trace. The remaining amplitudes $H_+,H_\times$ are the two <gravitational wave polarizations>. The formula uses the plus-sign convention for $\delta h=2\partial_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}$; reversing the definition of $\xi$ reverses the gauge change.

= TT coordinate separation and measured separation
{c}
{parent=Transverse-traceless gauge}
{title2=$\xi^{\hat A}=\xi_{\rm coord}^A+\tfrac12h_{AB}L^B$}

In <transverse-traceless gauge>, particles initially at rest have fixed spatial coordinates because $\Gamma^\mu{}_{00}=0$. Their separation measured in a <parallel-propagated orthonormal frame> changes: to first order $S^{\hat A}=S^A+h_{AB}S^B/2$. Hence the physical <geodesic deviation> is $\ddot\xi^{\hat A}=\ddot h_{AB}L^B/2$, whereas the coordinate displacement obeys $\ddot\xi^A_{\rm coord}=0$. The extra $\partial_0\Gamma^A{}_{0B}L^B$ term in the coordinate expansion of the second <covariant derivative> reconciles the two results. This applies to infinitesimal separations and linear <metric perturbations>.

= Sliding-bead gravitational wave detector
{parent=TT coordinate separation and measured separation}
{title2=$\ddot L=\tfrac12\epsilon\ddot h_{nn}L_0+O(\epsilon^2)$}

A short rigid nonrotating guide constrains two freely sliding beads to one axis $n$. In its <parallel-propagated orthonormal frame>, the axial <geodesic deviation> is $\ddot L=-R_{n0n0}L$. For a transverse <gravitational wave>, $R_{n0n0}=-\epsilon\ddot h_{nn}/2$, so a wave incident after the beads are at rest changes the measured separation by $\epsilon h_{nn}L_0/2$ to first order. Transverse reaction forces do not supply an axial restoring force. Alignment with the propagation direction gives no first-order length change.

= Gravitational wave polarization
{parent=Transverse-traceless gauge}
{wiki}

A gravitational wave in four-dimensional general relativity has two independent linear polarizations, conventionally called plus and cross. A rotation by angle $\vartheta$ in the transverse plane rotates their pair by $2\vartheta$.

= Transverse-traceless tensor
{parent=Gravitational wave polarization}
{title2=$k_ih_{ij}=0,\quad h_{ii}=0$}

A transverse-traceless spatial tensor is orthogonal to its <Fourier mode> wavevector in each index and has zero trace. The <transverse-traceless projector> extracts this part. Trace terms and symmetrized coordinate-displacement gradients have no such part, explaining the linear gauge invariance of <tensor cosmological perturbations>.

= Plus polarization
{title2=$+$ polarization}
{parent=Gravitational wave polarization}

A <gravitational wave> of plus polarization has opposite transverse strain or <geodesic deviation> <eigenvalues> along two fixed perpendicular axes. For a sinusoidal <periodic function> profile, the stretching and compressing roles alternate every half-period.

= Cross polarization
{title2=$\times$ polarization}
{parent=Gravitational wave polarization}

The cross polarization is the plus pattern rotated through $45$ degrees in the transverse plane.

= Transverse-traceless projector
{title2=$\Pi_{ij,mn}^{\rm TT}$}
{parent=Gravitational wave polarization}

The transverse-traceless projector maps a symmetric tensor to the part orthogonal to a chosen wavevector and with zero trace. It is the identity on physical gravitational-wave polarizations.

= Polarization completeness relation
{parent=Transverse-traceless projector}

A polarization completeness relation expresses a projector as a sum of outer products of an orthonormal polarization basis. For transverse-traceless tensors, $\sum_s\epsilon_{ij}^s\epsilon_{mn}^{s*}$ is the transverse-traceless projector up to the chosen normalization.

= Linearized Ricci tensor and scalar
{parent=Linearized gravity}

To first order around flat spacetime,
$$
R_{\mu\nu}
=\frac12\left(
\partial_\rho\partial_\mu h^\rho{}_{\nu}
+\partial_\rho\partial_\nu h^\rho{}_{\mu}
-\mathop{\Box}h_{\mu\nu}
-\partial_\mu\partial_\nu h
\right),
$$
and
$$
R=\partial_\mu\partial_\nu h^{\mu\nu}-\mathop{\Box}h,
\qquad h=\eta^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}.
$$

= Newtonian limit
{parent=Linearized gravity}
{c}
{wiki}

= Newtonian limit of general relativity
{c}
{synonym}

For a weak, static field produced by nonrelativistic matter, $T_{00}=\rho$ is the dominant stress-energy component. The field equations and the slow-particle <geodesic equation> imply
$$
h_{00}=-2\Phi,
\qquad
\kappa=8\pi G,
$$
where the <Poisson equation> is $\nabla^2\Phi=4\pi G\rho$.

= Newtonian connection from an exponential lapse
{c}
{parent=Newtonian limit}
{title2=$\Gamma^i{}_{tt}=\partial_i\phi$}

For smooth spatial $\phi$ and $g(c)=-c^2e^{2\phi/c^2}dt^2+\delta_{ij}dx^idx^j$, the only nonzero <Christoffel symbols> are $\Gamma^t{}_{ti}=\Gamma^t{}_{it}=c^{-2}\partial_i\phi$ and $\Gamma^i{}_{tt}=e^{2\phi/c^2}\partial_i\phi$. Consequently the <Levi-Civita connection> converges smoothly on compact sets as $c\to\infty$ although the metric itself diverges in these coordinates. The limiting <torsion-free connection> has only $\Gamma^i{}_{tt}=\partial_i\phi$ and gives $d^2x^i/dt^2=-\partial_i\phi$ when $t$ is an <affine parameter>. With $R^a{}_{bcd}=\partial_c\Gamma^a{}_{db}-\partial_d\Gamma^a{}_{cb}+\cdots$, its <Ricci tensor> is $(\Delta\phi)dt\otimes dt$: quadratic connection terms vanish and $R^i{}_{tjt}=\partial_j\partial_i\phi$. Thus zero Ricci tensor is the <Laplace equation>, while full flatness would require the entire Hessian to vanish.

= Static radius
{title2=$r_{\rm s}=(3GM/\Lambda)^{1/3}$}
{parent=Newtonian limit}

For a point mass in a positive <cosmological constant>, the inward Newtonian acceleration $GM/r^2$ balances the outward cosmological acceleration $\Lambda r/3$ at the static radius $r_{\rm s}=(3GM/\Lambda)^{1/3}$.

= Linearized point-mass metric in radial Cartesian form
{parent=Newtonian limit}

For a point mass and $r>0$, write
$$
h_{00}=\frac{2GM}{r},
\qquad
h_{ij}=f(r)x_i x_j.
$$
The vacuum scalar-curvature equation gives $rf'+3f=0$, so $f=C/r^3$. The remaining vacuum equations fix $C=2GM$ in the corresponding Schwarzschild gauge.

= Slowly rotating weak-field metric
{parent=Linearized gravity}

Far from a slowly rotating isolated body of mass $m$ and angular momentum $\mathbf J$, the scalar part of the metric is the linearized point-mass field while $g_{0i}$ is the dipole field of $\mathbf J$. For rotation about the z-axis, the cross term is $4Jr^{-3}(y\,dx-x\,dy)dt$ in a common convention.

= Curvature singularity
{parent=General relativity}

A curvature singularity is an incomplete spacetime boundary approached by curves along which a scalar curvature invariant or a component of the <Riemann curvature tensor> in a parallel-propagated frame becomes unbounded. Such divergence cannot be removed by changing coordinates.

= Schwarzschild spacetime
{parent=General relativity}
{c}

= Schwarzschild metric
{synonym}
{c}

= Schwarzschild black hole
{synonym}
{c}

In units $G=c=1$, the Schwarzschild exterior metric is
$$
ds^2=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)dt^2
+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}dr^2+r^2d\Omega^2.
$$

= Schwarzschild radial fall from rest at a finite radius
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{title2=$r=\tfrac{r_0}2(1+\cos\eta),\quad s=\sqrt{r_0^3/(8M)}(\eta+\sin\eta)$}

For radial timelike <geodesic> fall released from rest at $r_0>2M$, the <Killing energy> is $E=\sqrt{1-2M/r_0}$ and the <proper time> radial equation is $(dr/ds)^2=2M/r-2M/r_0$. The displayed parameterization integrates its inward branch, with $s=0$ at release. The <Schwarzschild event horizon> is reached at $\eta_H=\arccos(4M/r_0-1)$ in finite proper time, even though <Schwarzschild time> diverges.

// Target: general-relativity.bigb

= Finite pulse count for a falling Schwarzschild beacon
{parent=Schwarzschild radial fall from rest at a finite radius}
{title2=$N=\left\lceil(s_H-\delta)/P\right\rceil$}

A freely falling source emits <optical pulses> at proper times $\delta+nP$, for integers $n\ge0$. If its horizon-crossing <proper time> is finite, only pulses beginning before $s_H$ can escape. Their number is the displayed ceiling, for $0\le\delta<P<s_H$. Infinite <Schwarzschild time> at crossing does not give infinitely many emitted pulses. The pulse phase $\delta$ fixes both the count and the redshift of the last pulse. If a finite pulse straddles the horizon, its exterior portion has an arbitrarily late, increasingly redshifted received tail.

= Proper-time bound inside a Schwarzschild black hole
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{title2=$\Delta s\leq2M\arcsin\sqrt{r_0/(2M)}-\sqrt{r_0(2M-r_0)}<\pi M$}

Inside the future black-hole region, the radius decreases along every future-directed timelike path. Its unit tangent obeys $|dr/ds|\geq\sqrt{2M/r-1}$ from the metric normalization, independently of radial acceleration or angular motion. Integrating from $r_0<2M$ to zero gives the displayed finite upper bound. The statement concerns future continuation within the black-hole region; reversing time in the white-hole region reverses the radial direction.

= Horizon redshift of a Schwarzschild infaller
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

For smooth radial infall through a future <Schwarzschild event horizon>, outgoing retarded time obeys $u\sim-4M\log(\tau_H-\tau)$. A distant observer sees the <redshift> grow as $e^{t_{\rm rec}/(4M)}$. Its exponent in emission <Schwarzschild time> is instead $1/(2M)$, because $t_{\rm rec}\sim2t_{\rm em}$. At finite fixed observer radius the wavelength ratio includes $\sqrt{1-2M/r_B}$.

= Schwarzschild time
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}
{title2=$t$}

<Schwarzschild time> $t$ is the exterior time coordinate normalized to the <proper time> of static observers at infinity. At fixed exterior radius, $d\tau=\sqrt{1-2M/r}\,dt$. A received signal and its emission occur at different values of this coordinate.

= Kruskal spacetime
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}

The <Kruskal spacetime> is the maximal analytic extension of the positive-mass vacuum <Schwarzschild spacetime>. It contains two exterior regions, a <black hole>, and a <white hole>. <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates> remove both horizon coordinate singularities, while $r=0$ remains a <curvature singularity>.

= Non-traversability of the Schwarzschild bridge
{parent=Kruskal spacetime}

In <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates>, every future-directed radial causal curve has nondecreasing $U$ and $V$. The right exterior has $U<0,V>0$, while the left has $U>0,V<0$. No future-directed causal curve can travel between the exteriors because it would have to decrease one null coordinate. The spatial bridge on a time-symmetric slice is therefore not a traversable passage.

= Kruskal diagram
{parent=Kruskal spacetime}
{c}
{title2=$T_K=(V_K+U_K)/2,\quad X_K=(V_K-U_K)/2$}

This radial spacetime diagram uses <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates> to display both exteriors, the black hole and the white hole of maximally extended <Schwarzschild spacetime>. Each point represents a symmetry two-sphere. Radial <null geodesics> have slopes $\pm1$ in the displayed coordinates; the horizons are $T_K=\pm X_K$ and the singularities satisfy $T_K^2-X_K^2=1$ in the usual normalization. A further compactification gives the corresponding <Penrose diagram>.

= Radial timelike incompleteness in Kruskal spacetime
{parent=Kruskal spacetime}

Every maximal radial <timelike geodesic> in positive-mass <Kruskal spacetime> is incomplete in at least one direction. Its equations are $\dot r^2=E^2-1+2M/r$ and $\ddot r=-M/r^2$. A finite turning point is a maximum, and each trajectory reaches $r=0$ at one or both ends. The remaining <proper time> is finite because $d\tau\sim\sqrt{r/(2M)}\,dr$. Complete circular timelike geodesics exist, but they have nonzero <angular momentum>.

= Negative heat capacity of a Schwarzschild black hole
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{title2=$C=-8\pi M^2$}

In units $G=\hbar=c=k_B=1$, a <Schwarzschild black hole> has <Hawking temperature> $T=1/(8\pi M)$, so its <heat capacity> is $dM/dT=-8\pi M^2$. It heats up as it loses <mass>, preventing stable canonical equilibrium with an unlimited thermal reservoir.

= Ultrarelativistic limit of the boosted Schwarzschild metric away from the null plane
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

Boost a Schwarzschild mass with speed $v$ and hold $p=m\gamma$ fixed as $v\to1$. Away from the null plane $x=t$, the isotropic-coordinate expression tends to
$$
ds^2=ds_{\rm Minkowski}^2+\frac{4p}{|x-t|}(dt-dx)^2.
$$
The complete distributional limit on the null plane requires the additional coordinate treatment that produces the Aichelburg-Sexl shock wave.

= Schwarzschild-de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{wiki}

= Kottler spacetime
{c}
{synonym}

The Schwarzschild-de Sitter spacetime is the spherically symmetric vacuum solution with positive <cosmological constant>. In static coordinates its metric function is $f(r)=1-2m/r-H^2r^2$, with a black-hole horizon and a cosmological horizon when $mH<1/\sqrt{27}$.

= Geodetic effect
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{wiki}

= Geodetic precession
{synonym}

Geodetic precession is the rotation of a parallel-transported gyroscope relative to the orbital radial direction in curved spacetime. For a circular Schwarzschild orbit its exact precession per revolution is $2\pi[1-\sqrt{1-3M/R}]$.

= Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{wiki}

Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates write the Schwarzschild metric as
$$
ds^2=-dt^2+\left(dr+\sqrt{\frac{2M}{r}}\,dt\right)^2+r^2d\Omega^2.
$$
The constant-$t$ spatial slices are flat, and the coordinates remain regular at the <Schwarzschild radius>.

= Domain of the ingoing Painleve-Gullstrand chart
{parent=Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates}
{title2=$V>0,\quad UV<1$}

Choose <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates> with the right exterior at $U<0,V>0$ and $UV=(1-r/(2M))e^{r/(2M)}$. The ingoing <Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates> cover that exterior and the future black-hole region $U>0,V>0$, together with their common future horizon. They exclude the other exterior, the white hole, the bifurcation sphere and the singularity. Indeed $\tau=v+F(r)-r_*(r)$, with $v=4M\log V$ and $F'=\sqrt{2M/r}/f$; the difference $F-r_*$ is finite at $r=2M$, whereas $V=0$ requires infinite coordinate time.

= Free-fall proper time in Schwarzschild spacetime
{parent=Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates}
{title2=$d\tau=dt+\frac{\sqrt{2M/r}}{1-2M/r}dr$}

The inward radial <geodesic congruence> with unit <Killing energy> has $U=f^{-1}\partial_t-\sqrt{2M/r}\,\partial_r$, where $f=1-2M/r$. Its metric dual is $U^\flat=-d\tau$, so $\tau$ is a synchronized <proper time> for the congruence. Locally $\tau=t+2\sqrt{2Mr}+2M\log| (\sqrt r-\sqrt{2M})/(\sqrt r+\sqrt{2M}) |$. This yields <Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates> with flat spatial slices and a regular future horizon.

= Schwarzschild horizon-to-singularity proper time for fall from infinity
{parent=Free-fall proper time in Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}
{title2=$\Delta\tau=4M/3$}

A radial observer released from rest at infinity has $dr/d\tau=-\sqrt{2M/r}$. Integration from $r=2M$ to $r=0$ gives $\Delta\tau=\int_0^{2M}\sqrt{r/(2M)}dr=4M/3$ in geometric units. In ordinary units it is $4GM/(3c^3)$. The endpoint is the curvature singularity, not a regular spacetime centre; this value belongs to that specified energy, not every possible infalling worldline.

= Orthonormal coframe in Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates
{parent=Painlevé–Gullstrand coordinates}

An orthonormal coframe is
$$
e^0=dt,
\qquad e^1=dr+\sqrt{\frac{2M}{r}}\,dt,
\qquad e^2=r\,d\theta,
\qquad e^3=r\sin\theta\,d\phi.
$$

= Radial Weyl curvature of Schwarzschild spacetime
{title2=$C_{trtr}=-2M/r^3$}
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

With the curvature convention
$$
R^\alpha{}_{\beta\gamma\delta}
=\partial_\gamma\Gamma^\alpha_{\beta\delta}
-\partial_\delta\Gamma^\alpha_{\beta\gamma}+\cdots,
$$
the vacuum Schwarzschild metric satisfies
$$
C_{trtr}=R_{trtr}=-\frac{2M}{r^3}.
$$
The opposite Riemann-sign convention reverses this sign.

= Schwarzschild tidal force
{parent=Radial Weyl curvature of Schwarzschild spacetime}

The orthonormal-frame components of Schwarzschild curvature scale as $M/r^3$. The <geodesic deviation> equation therefore gives radial stretching and transverse compression whose magnitude diverges as $r\to0$.

= Tidal gravity and the equivalence principle
{parent=Radial Weyl curvature of Schwarzschild spacetime}

Weyl curvature produces relative acceleration between neighbouring freely falling trajectories and therefore tidal deformation. A freely falling frame removes gravity at one event, as required by the equivalence principle, but cannot remove curvature over an extended region.

= Equatorial null orbit in Schwarzschild spacetime
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

For inverse radius $y=1/r$ and azimuth $\phi$, a Schwarzschild null geodesic obeys
$$
(y')^2+y^2-2My^3=\frac1{b^2},
\qquad
y''+y=3My^2,
$$
where $b=L/E$ is the <impact parameter>.

= Critical photon orbits of Schwarzschild spacetime
{parent=Equatorial null orbit in Schwarzschild spacetime}
{title2=$u-\tfrac13=-4Ae^\phi/(1+2Ae^\phi)^2$}

At the critical <impact parameter> $b=3\sqrt3M$, the null orbit equation factors as $(u')^2=(u-1/3)^2(2u+1/3)$ for $u=M/r$. One oriented family has the displayed solution. As $\phi\to-\infty$, positive $A$ approaches the <photon sphere> from larger radii and negative $A$ from smaller radii; $A=0$ is the circular orbit. The physical domain must retain $r>0$ and the positive square root in its implicit parametrization.

= Circular null geodesic
{parent=Equatorial null orbit in Schwarzschild spacetime}

A circular null geodesic remains at constant areal radius. It lies at a stationary point of the null radial effective potential and may be stable or unstable according to the sign of the second derivative there.

= Photon sphere
{parent=Circular null geodesic}
{wiki}

A photon sphere is a spherical set of circular <null geodesics>. In <Schwarzschild spacetime> it occurs at $r=3M$ and is unstable.

= Photon capture cross-section of a Reissner-Nordstrom black hole
{parent=Photon sphere}
{title2=$\sigma=2\pi r_{\mathrm{ph}}^3/(r_{\mathrm{ph}}-M)$}

For a <Reissner-Nordstrom black hole> with $|Q|\le M$, the outer unstable <circular null geodesic> has radius $r_{\mathrm{ph}}=(3M+\sqrt{9M^2-8Q^2})/2$. Maximizing $f/r^2$ for $f=1-2M/r+Q^2/r^2$ gives the critical <impact parameter> $b_c^2=r_{\mathrm{ph}}^2/f(r_{\mathrm{ph}})=2r_{\mathrm{ph}}^3/(r_{\mathrm{ph}}-M)$. The <geometrical optics> capture cross-section is $\pi b_c^2$, reducing to $27\pi M^2$ in <Schwarzschild spacetime> and $16\pi M^2$ in the extremal charged case. Finite-wavelength absorption need not equal this geometric limit.

// Target: general-relativity.bigb

= Schwarzschild radius
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}
{wiki}

The Schwarzschild radius is $r_s=2GM/c^2$, or $2M$ in geometrized units.

= Schwarzschild tortoise coordinate
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}

The tortoise coordinate satisfies
$$
\frac{dr_*}{dr}=\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1},
\qquad
r_*=r+2M\log\left|\frac{r}{2M}-1\right|.
$$

= Outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
{parent=Schwarzschild tortoise coordinate}
{c}
{title2=$u=t-r_*$}

The retarded coordinate $u=t-r_*$ puts the radial <Schwarzschild metric> into $ds^2=-f\,du^2-2\,du\,dr+r^2d\Omega^2$, with $f=1-2M/r$. Outgoing radial <null geodesics> have constant $u$. These coordinates are regular at the past horizon; an infaller crossing the future horizon has $u\to+\infty$.

= Ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
{parent=Schwarzschild tortoise coordinate}
{c}

The advanced coordinate $v=t+r_*$ puts the radial Schwarzschild metric into the form
$$
ds^2=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)dv^2+2\,dv\,dr+r^2d\Omega^2,
$$
which is regular at the future horizon.

These are the advanced, ingoing member of the <Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates>.

= Finkelstein diagram
{parent=Ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates}
{c}

A <Finkelstein diagram> plots <areal radius> $r$ against $T=v-r$ in <ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates>. Its radial <null directions> show explicitly that both future light-ray families decrease $r$ inside a <Schwarzschild black hole>. It is not a compactified <Penrose diagram>.

= Radial null trajectories in ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
{parent=Ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates}

Radial null curves comprise the ingoing family $v=\text{constant}$ and the outgoing family
$$
\frac{dr}{dv}=\frac12\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right).
$$
Outside the horizon the outgoing family moves to larger $r$; inside it both future-directed families move to smaller $r$.

= Schwarzschild event horizon
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{c}

The surface $r=2M$ is the future event horizon of Schwarzschild spacetime. Its singularity in Schwarzschild coordinates is removable, whereas $r=0$ is a curvature singularity.

= Schwarzschild horizon collision energy
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild event horizon}
{title2=$E_{\rm com}^2=m^2[4+(h_1-h_2)^2/(4M^2)]$}

For two equal-mass inward <timelike geodesics> of unit specific <Killing energy>, their invariant <center-of-mass energy> has this finite horizon limit. If both are captured from infinity, <Schwarzschild marginally bound capture> gives $E_{\rm com}<m\sqrt{20}$, with $m\sqrt{20}$ a supremum. No such universal bound follows for particles prepared inside the angular-momentum barrier.

= Schwarzschild singularity
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

The Schwarzschild singularity is the spacelike curvature singularity at $r=0$. Curvature invariants and orthonormal tidal components diverge there.

= Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{wiki}

Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates extend the Schwarzschild solution smoothly through its future and past horizons. Constant Schwarzschild radius curves are hyperbolae, while the horizons are null lines through the bifurcation sphere.

= Kruskal extension across a simple static horizon
{c}
{parent=Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates}
{title2=$r_*=(2\kappa)^{-1}\log|r-r_H|+h(r)$}

For $ds^2=-fdt^2+dr^2/f+R^2d\Omega^2$, suppose $f$ and $R$ are analytic at $r_H$, $f(r_H)=0$, $f'(r_H)=2\kappa>0$ and $R(r_H)>0$. The <tortoise coordinate> obeys the displayed expansion with analytic $h$. For $U=-e^{-\kappa(t-r_*)}$ and $V=e^{\kappa(t+r_*)}$ in the right exterior, $-UV=(r-r_H)e^{2\kappa h(r)}$ after fixing an additive constant. The inverse function theorem makes $r$ analytic in $UV$ through zero, while $ds^2=f\,dU\,dV/(\kappa^2UV)+R^2d\Omega^2$ has a finite nonzero radial coefficient there. This supplies the local analytic extension and its <bifurcate Killing horizon>.

= Schwarzschild geodesic from the bifurcation sphere to the singularity
{parent=Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates}

A radial geodesic orthogonal to the time-symmetric Kruskal slice starts at $r=2M$ with zero Schwarzschild energy and reaches $r=0$ after proper time $\pi M$.

= Gravitational redshift between static Schwarzschild observers
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

For static observers at radii $r_A$ and $r_B$, equal Schwarzschild-coordinate time intervals correspond to proper times $d\tau=\sqrt{1-2M/r}\,dt$. Hence
$$
\frac{\Delta\tau_B}{\Delta\tau_A}
=\sqrt{\frac{1-2M/r_B}{1-2M/r_A}}.
$$

This static-observer relation is a special case of <gravitational redshift>, not a definition of every gravitational frequency shift.

= Proper acceleration of a static Schwarzschild observer
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

A static observer at Schwarzschild radius $r>2M$ must maintain the outward <proper acceleration>
$$
a=\frac{M}{r^2\sqrt{1-2M/r}}.
$$
It approaches the Newtonian value $M/r^2$ at large radius and diverges at the horizon.

= Birkhoff's theorem
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}
{wiki=Birkhoff's_theorem_(relativity)}

= Birkhoff theorem
{c}
{synonym}

Every spherically symmetric vacuum solution of the Einstein field equation is locally a portion of Schwarzschild spacetime. In particular, a spherically symmetric body's exterior field depends only on its total mass.

= Redshifted gravitational mass of a static thin shell
{parent=Birkhoff's theorem}

A thin spherical shell of proper surface mass density $\rho$ held at Schwarzschild radius $r_{\rm st}$ around mass $M$ contributes, to first order in its mass,
$$
\Delta M_\infty
=4\pi r_{\rm st}^2\rho\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r_{\rm st}}}
$$
to the exterior Schwarzschild mass parameter.

= Schwarzschild light deflection
{c}
{parent=Schwarzschild spacetime}

A null Schwarzschild geodesic with $b\gg r_s$ is deflected through $2r_s/b=4GM/(bc^2)$ to first order.

This is the leading spherical-mass deflection underlying a <gravitational lens>.

= Perturbative derivation of Schwarzschild light deflection
{parent=Schwarzschild light deflection}

Writing $y=b^{-1}\sin\phi+\Delta y$ in $y''+y=3My^2$ and retaining first order in $M$ gives
$$
\Delta y''+\Delta y=\frac{3M}{2b^2}(1-\cos2\phi).
$$
The displacement of the outgoing zero is $4M/b$.

= Nordström's theory of gravitation
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= Nordstrom theory of gravitation
{c}
{synonym}

Nordstrom's scalar theory restricts the spacetime metric to the conformally flat form $g_{\mu\nu}=\varphi^2\eta_{\mu\nu}$. Its null geodesics have the same unparametrized paths as those of the <Minkowski metric>, so the theory predicts no gravitational bending of light.

= Newtonian limit of Nordstrom gravity
{parent=Nordström's theory of gravitation}
{c}
{title2=$\phi=1+\Phi,\quad\Delta\Phi=4\pi\rho$}

For a weak, nearly static <conformal factor> in <Nordstrom theory of gravitation>, the trace of the <dust stress-energy tensor> is negative in signature $(-+++)$, so its scalar field equation reduces to the <Poisson equation> for the <Newtonian gravitational potential>. The <geodesic equation for a conformally flat metric> gives $\ddot{\boldsymbol x}=-\boldsymbol\nabla\Phi$ at small speeds. The constant one in $\phi$ has no effect on the force.

= Conformally flat metric
{parent=Nordström's theory of gravitation}

= Conformally flat
{synonym}

A metric is conformally flat when it is locally a positive scalar multiple of a flat metric. Multiplication by the conformal factor preserves null cones and unparametrized null geodesics.

A conformally flat metric is locally a positive scalar multiple of a flat metric. It makes its domain a <conformally flat manifold>.

= Tolman wormhole
{c}
{parent=Conformally flat metric}
{title2=$ds^2=(1+\lambda/r^2)^2\delta_{ij}dx^idx^j$}

For $\lambda>0$, this four-dimensional Euclidean metric has two asymptotically flat ends. Inversion $\rho=\lambda/r$ puts the metric near the $r=0$ end into the same form with $\rho\to\infty$. On $r>0$, its <Ricci scalar> vanishes because $\Delta(r^{-2})=0$, but its <Ricci tensor> is $R_{ij}=4\lambda(\delta_{ij}-4x_ix_j/r^2)/(r^2+\lambda)^2$. It therefore does not satisfy the <vacuum Einstein equations> unless $\lambda=0$. For negative $\lambda$ the conformal factor vanishes at a finite radius, so that metric is not a smooth wormhole across that sphere.

= Curvature of a conformally Euclidean metric
{parent=Conformally flat metric}
{title2=$g_{ij}=e^{2u}\delta_{ij}$}

For a positive conformal factor in dimension $n\geq3$, set $S_{ij}=-u_{ij}+u_i u_j-\tfrac12|\nabla u|^2\delta_{ij}$. These are the coordinate components of the <Schouten tensor>. In the convention $R_{ijkl}=g(e_i,R(e_k,e_l)e_j)$, the <Riemann curvature tensor> is $R_{ijkl}=e^{2u}(\delta_{ik}S_{jl}+\delta_{jl}S_{ik}-\delta_{il}S_{jk}-\delta_{jk}S_{il})$. The <orthonormal coframe> $e^u dx^i$ has <connection 1-forms> $\omega^i{}_j=u_jdx^i-u_idx^j$; substituting into <Cartan's second structure equation> proves this expression directly. Its contractions give $R_{ij}=(n-2)S_{ij}+\delta_{ij}\sum_kS_{kk}$ and $R=e^{-2u}[-2(n-1)\Delta u-(n-1)(n-2)|\nabla u|^2]$.

= Conformal factor
{parent=Conformally flat metric}
{wiki}

A conformal factor is the positive scalar field multiplying a metric under a conformal rescaling. In numerical relativity one often writes a spatial metric as $\gamma_{ij}=\psi^4\widetilde\gamma_{ij}$.

= Conformal preservation of null geodesic paths
{parent=Conformal factor}
{title2=$\widetilde g=\Omega^2g$}

The difference of the two <Levi-Civita connections> is $\widetilde\Gamma^a{}_{bc}-\Gamma^a{}_{bc}=\delta^a_b\partial_c\log\Omega+\delta^a_c\partial_b\log\Omega-g_{bc}g^{ad}\partial_d\log\Omega$. Along a <null geodesic> with tangent $k$, the last term vanishes and the remaining acceleration is $2k(\log\Omega)k$. It is parallel to the tangent, so changing the <affine parameter> removes it. Thus a positive <conformal factor> preserves unparametrized <null geodesics>, although it generally does not preserve timelike or spacelike geodesics.

= Geodesic equation for a conformally flat metric
{parent=Nordström's theory of gravitation}

For $g_{\mu\nu}=\varphi^2\eta_{\mu\nu}$,
$$
\Gamma^\mu_{\alpha\beta}
=\delta^\mu_\alpha\partial_\beta\log\varphi
+\delta^\mu_\beta\partial_\alpha\log\varphi
-\eta_{\alpha\beta}\partial^\mu\log\varphi.
$$
For a null curve, the last term drops from the <geodesic equation>; the remaining acceleration is parallel to the tangent and can be removed by reparametrization.

= de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

= de Sitter
{c}
{synonym}

De Sitter spacetime is the maximally symmetric Lorentzian spacetime of constant positive curvature. Its expanding flat slicing has scale factor $a(\tau)=-1/(H\tau)$ in conformal time $\tau<0$.

= Open slicing of de Sitter spacetime
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$a(t)=\dfrac{\sqrt{-k}}{H_\Lambda}\sinh(H_\Lambda t)$}

With no matter or radiation, $k<0$ and $H_\Lambda=\sqrt{\Lambda/3}>0$, the <Friedmann equation> in units $c=1$ is $\dot a^2=H_\Lambda^2a^2-k$. Separating variables on the expanding branch and choosing its coordinate origin at $a=0$ gives the displayed <scale factor>. Normalization $a(t_0)=1$ imposes $\sinh(H_\Lambda t_0)=H_\Lambda/\sqrt{-k}$ and $H_0^2=H_\Lambda^2-k$. The zero of the <scale factor> is the boundary of this open coordinate patch, rather than a curvature singularity of <de Sitter spacetime>.

= Two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=dt^2-\cosh^2t\,d\chi^2$}

Unit-radius <two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime> is the <hyperboloid> $(X^0)^2-(X^1)^2-(X^2)^2=-1$ in three-dimensional <Minkowski spacetime>. Its closed coordinates are $X^0=\sinh t$, $X^1=\cosh t\cos\chi$, $X^2=\cosh t\sin\chi$, with $t\in\mathbb R$ and $\chi$ periodic modulo $2\pi$. Unwrapping $\chi$ gives its universal covering spacetime instead.

= Universal cover of two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=\sec^2\eta(-d\eta^2+d\xi^2),\quad\xi\in\mathbb R$}

The unrestricted spatial coordinate unwraps the spatial circle of <two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime>. The <conformal time> $\eta=\arcsin(\tanh t)$ lies in $(-\pi/2,\pi/2)$, and <null geodesics> have slopes $d\xi/d\eta=\pm1$. Constant-$\eta$ lines are <Cauchy hypersurfaces>: along any causal curve $|d\xi/d\eta|\leq1$, so a curve cannot escape to spatial infinity in a bounded conformal-time interval. The covering spacetime is therefore <globally hyperbolic>, despite its observer-dependent cosmological horizons. Identifying $\xi$ modulo $2\pi$ gives the de Sitter cylinder, also globally hyperbolic.

= Conformal cylinder of two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=\sec^2T(dT^2-d\chi^2),\quad -\pi/2<T<\pi/2$}

The <conformal time> $T=2\arctan(e^t)-\pi/2$ satisfies $\sin T=\tanh t$ and $\cos T=\operatorname{sech}t$. Multiplying the physical metric by $\cos^2T$ gives a finite <conformal compactification> with periodic spatial coordinate. Its past and future conformal boundaries are spacelike circles. <Null geodesics> have slopes $d\chi/dT=\pm1$.

= Observer horizons in two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Conformal cylinder of two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$d(\chi,0)<\pi/2-T\quad\text{or}\quad d(\chi,0)<T+\pi/2$}

For the complete observer $\chi=0$ in <two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime>, let $d(\chi,0)\in[0,\pi]$ be shortest angular distance. An event can signal to the observer exactly when $d(\chi,0)<\pi/2-T$, and can receive its signals exactly when $d(\chi,0)<T+\pi/2$. Equality describes its future and past <observer event horizons>. Their intersection is the <static patch of de Sitter spacetime> containing the observer. These are observer-dependent cosmological horizons; constant-$T$ circles are <Cauchy hypersurfaces>, so there are no <Cauchy horizons> in the full spacetime.

= Geodesics of two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$X''=\varepsilon X,\quad \varepsilon=X'^2\in\{1,0,-1\}$}

A <geodesic> on the embedded <two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime> is the intersection with a two-plane through the ambient origin. For initial point $P$ and tangent $V$ satisfying $P^2=-1$, $P\cdot V=0$, $V^2=\varepsilon$, its affine parametrization is $P\cosh s+V\sinh s$ when timelike, $P+sV$ when null and $P\cos s+V\sin s$ when spacelike. These solve $X''=\varepsilon X$. The <spacelike geodesics> are closed; <timelike geodesics> and <null geodesics> extend to both conformal infinities with infinite affine length.

= Closed slicing of de Sitter spacetime
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=\ell^2[-d\tau^2+\cosh^2\tau\,d\Omega_3^2],\quad\ell^2=3/\Lambda$}

The vacuum <Einstein field equations> with positive <cosmological constant> admit a closed spherical slicing with scale factor $a(t)=\ell\cosh[(t-t_0)/\ell]$. The time-symmetric slice is at $t=t_0$. The substitution $T=2\arctan(e^\tau)-\pi/2$ gives $dT/d\tau=\operatorname{sech}\tau$ and $\cos T=\operatorname{sech}\tau$, so the metric is $\ell^2\sec^2T[-dT^2+d\Omega_3^2]$. The conformal metric is a finite-time slab of the <Einstein static universe>, with spacelike conformal infinities $T=\pm\pi/2$.

= Cosmological horizons in global de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Closed slicing of de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$\chi_p=T+\pi/2,\quad\chi_e=\pi/2-T$}

For a complete comoving observer at the north pole of the <closed slicing of de Sitter spacetime>, radial null rays have $d\chi=\pm dT$. The finite past conformal interval gives the displayed <particle horizon>; the finite remaining future interval gives the displayed <observer event horizon>. The angular distances range strictly between zero and $\pi$ at every finite proper time. Complete constant-$T$ three-spheres are <Cauchy hypersurfaces>, so their domains of dependence cover the global spacetime and they have no <Cauchy horizons>. Cosmological horizons are observer-dependent and are not failures of global hyperbolicity.

= Static patch of de Sitter spacetime
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=-(1-r^2/R^2)dt^2+dr^2/(1-r^2/R^2)+r^2d\Omega_2^2$}

This region $0\leq r<R$ of <de Sitter spacetime> is static for the central observer, with a cosmological <Killing horizon> at $r=R$. The central <proper time> fixes the normalization of $\partial_t$. Static observers have acceleration $r/(R^2\sqrt{1-r^2/R^2})$. Horizon-crossing null coordinates remove the coordinate singularity, while the global extension has compact spherical spatial slices and spacelike past/future conformal infinity.

= Euclidean de Sitter action and entropy
{c}
{parent=Static patch of de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$I_E=-3\pi/\Lambda,\quad S=3\pi/\Lambda$}

In units $G=\hbar=c=k_B=1$, a smooth <Wick rotation> of the four-dimensional <static patch of de Sitter spacetime> has Euclidean time period $2\pi\sqrt{3/\Lambda}$. Its compact geometry is a round four-sphere of radius $\sqrt{3/\Lambda}$. The <Einstein field equations> give $R=4\Lambda$, and direct integration gives volume $24\pi^2/\Lambda^2$, hence gravitational <Euclidean action> $I_E=-3\pi/\Lambda$. With zero assigned internal energy, $I_E=\beta E-S$ gives <Bekenstein-Hawking entropy> $S=3\pi/\Lambda=A/4$.

= de Sitter horizon temperature
{c}
{parent=Static patch of de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$T(r)=1/(2\pi R\sqrt{1-r^2/R^2})$}

In units $\hbar=c=k_B=1$, the central inertial observer has temperature $1/(2\pi R)$. The <Tolman temperature law> redshifts it to the displayed static-observer value. Its acceleration satisfies $4\pi^2T^2=a^2+R^{-2}$. Near the horizon the temperature approaches $a/(2\pi)$, as predicted by the <Unruh effect>; at the center temperature remains nonzero despite vanishing acceleration.

= de Sitter isometry
{c}
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}

Four-dimensional <de Sitter spacetime> has ten independent isometries. In the expanding flat slicing they act on the spatial boundary as three translations, three rotations, one dilation, and three special conformal transformations.

= Spatial translation
{parent=de Sitter isometry}

A spatial translation in de Sitter flat slicing sends $\mathbf x\mapsto\mathbf x+\mathbf a$ at fixed conformal time. Translation invariance of a momentum-space correlator produces its momentum-conserving <Dirac delta distribution>.

= Spatial rotation
{parent=de Sitter isometry}

A spatial rotation acts by $\mathbf x\mapsto R\mathbf x$ with $R$ orthogonal. A rotationally invariant scalar two-point function depends only on the magnitude of its momentum.

= de Sitter dilation
{c}
{parent=de Sitter isometry}

In flat conformal coordinates, a de Sitter dilation acts as $(\tau,\mathbf x)\mapsto(s\tau,s\mathbf x)$. It is an isometry because the common coordinate rescaling cancels the inverse-square scaling of the conformal factor.

= Special conformal transformation
{parent=de Sitter isometry}
{wiki}

The three special conformal transformations complete translations, rotations, and dilations to the ten-dimensional de Sitter isometry group in flat slicing. A propagation speed different from the metric light speed generally breaks these transformations.

= Expanding flat slicing of de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=de Sitter spacetime}

The expanding flat slicing has metric
$$
ds^2=-dt^2+e^{2Ht}d\mathbf x^2,
\qquad H>0.
$$
It covers one half of global <de Sitter spacetime> and has a past coordinate boundary at $t\to-\infty$.

= Timelike geodesic in the expanding flat slicing of de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=Expanding flat slicing of de Sitter spacetime}

For a timelike geodesic moving in the $x$ direction with $t(0)=x(0)=0$ and $\dot x(0)=v_0>0$, spatial translation symmetry gives $e^{2Ht}\dot x=v_0$. Proper-time normalization then gives
$$
\dot t=\sqrt{1+v_0^2e^{-2Ht}}.
$$

= Past incompleteness of the expanding flat slicing of de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=Timelike geodesic in the expanding flat slicing of de Sitter spacetime}

The geodesic with nonzero conserved spatial momentum reaches $t=-\infty$ after the finite past proper time
$$
\tau_-=-\frac1{2H}\log\frac{\sqrt{1+v_0^2}+1}{\sqrt{1+v_0^2}-1}.
$$
This is incompleteness of the expanding coordinate patch, whose geodesics extend smoothly in global <de Sitter spacetime>; it is not a curvature singularity.

= Einstein static universe
{c}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

The Einstein static universe is a product of time with a round three-sphere supported by matter and a positive cosmological constant. For pressureless matter its cosmological repulsion balances the spatial curvature exactly.

= Anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=General relativity}
{c}
{wiki}

= AdS
{c}
{synonym}

= Anti-de Sitter
{c}
{synonym}

Anti-de Sitter spacetime is the maximally symmetric Lorentzian spacetime of constant negative curvature.

= Anti-de Sitter timelike geodesic refocusing
{c}
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$X(s)=X_P\cos(s/L)+LV_P\sin(s/L)$}

Embed radius-$L$ <anti-de Sitter spacetime> as $X\cdot X=-L^2$ in signature with two timelike directions. Initial tangent $V_P$ satisfies $X_P\cdot V_P=0$ and $V_P\cdot V_P=-1$. The displayed curve remains on the hyperboloid and has unit timelike tangent; its ambient acceleration is normal, proving it is a <geodesic>. Every such curve reaches $-X_P$ after proper time $\pi L$. On the <universal cover>, this is a later antipodal event, not a closed causal loop. Global time advances by $2\pi$ over a complete ambient period.

= Matrix model of anti-de Sitter three-space
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$\operatorname{AdS}_3\cong SL(2,\mathbb R),\quad g=B/8$}

For ambient <quadratic form> $Q=-x_0^2-x_4^2+x_1^2+x_2^2$, associate $M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_4+x_1&x_2+x_0\\x_2-x_0&x_4-x_1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Its <determinant> is $-Q$, so the unit <anti-de Sitter spacetime> hyperboloid is the <special linear group> of real two-by-two matrices. On its tangent space at the identity, the induced <bilinear form> is $\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(UV)$. The <Killing form> is $4\operatorname{tr}(UV)$, and left and right matrix multiplication preserve the ambient form. Consequently the induced <bi-invariant pseudo-Riemannian metric> is $B/8$. This model has periodic time; its causally unwrapped version is the <universal covering Lie group>.

= Left-right double cover of SO0(2,2)
{parent=Matrix model of anti-de Sitter three-space}
{title2=$SL(2,\mathbb R)^2/\{(I,I),(-I,-I)\}\cong SO_0(2,2)$}

The action $(A,B):M\mapsto AMB^{-1}$ preserves the <quadratic form> $-\det M$ on real two-by-two matrices. Its <kernel> consists of the simultaneous scalar pairs $(I,I)$ and $(-I,-I)$. The differential $(a,b):M\mapsto aM-Mb$ is injective on the two traceless <Lie algebras>, and both source and target have dimension six. Its image is therefore open and, since the source is connected, equals the <identity component of a Lie group> $SO_0(2,2)$. This gives a two-sheeted <covering map> onto the identity component, not onto the disconnected full <special orthogonal group> $SO(2,2)$.

= Poincare coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime
{c}
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=z^{-2}(dz^2+\eta_{ab}du^a du^b)$}

For Lorentzian boundary metric $\eta$ and $z>0$, this <anti-de Sitter spacetime> patch has unit curvature radius. Its <orthonormal coframe> has connection $\omega^a{}_3=-\theta^a$ and curvature $\Omega^a{}_b=-\theta^a\wedge\theta_b$, giving <sectional curvature> $-1$. The full local <isometry> algebra is $\mathfrak{so}(d-1,2)$ in spacetime dimension $d$. A patch need not support every local Killing flow for all parameter values.

= Timelike geodesics in a warped Poincaré patch
{parent=Poincare coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$e^{-y}=K\cos(s-s_0),\quad x^\mu=x_0^\mu+\frac{p^\mu}{K^2}\tan(s-s_0)$}

For unit-radius metric $dy^2+e^{-2y}\eta_{\mu\nu}dx^\mu dx^\nu$, translations conserve $p^\mu=e^{-2y}\dot x^\mu$. Timelike normalization gives $K^2=-\eta_{\mu\nu}p^\mu p^\nu>0$ and $\dot y^2=K^2e^{2y}-1$. Setting $w=e^{-y}$ yields $\dot w^2+w^2=K^2$, hence the displayed proper-time solution where the cosine is positive. Its two patch-horizon crossings are separated by proper time $\pi$, although the globally extended <geodesic> is complete.

= Poincaré horizon
{c}
{parent=Poincare coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$X_{-1}+X_d=0$}

The boundary of a chosen <Poincare coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime> patch inside the full spacetime is a null coordinate horizon. It is not the timelike <conformal boundary>, and not a curvature singularity. Timelike <geodesics> can cross it in finite proper time while the patch time diverges. In a signed-diameter conformal strip, the patch containing $T=\rho=0$ satisfies $|T|<\pi/2+\rho$.

= Two-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=\cosh^2r\,dt^2-dr^2$}

The unit hyperboloid $(X^0)^2+(X^1)^2-(X^2)^2=1$ in ambient signature $(++-)$ has $X^0=\cosh r\cos t$, $X^1=\cosh r\sin t$, $X^2=\sinh r$. It gives the displayed metric with $r\in\mathbb R$ and periodic $t$ modulo $2\pi$. Its time circles are timelike, so the <universal cover>, with $t\in\mathbb R$, removes these <closed timelike curves>. The metric alone does not prescribe the global identification. Both versions have constant negative curvature and two spatial ends.

= Geodesics of two-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Two-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$X''=-\kappa X,\quad\kappa=X'^2\in\{1,0,-1\}$}

A geodesic of the unit embedded hyperboloid lies in the plane spanned by its initial point and tangent. The constraint $X^2=1$ forces the displayed ambient acceleration. Timelike geodesics are $X=P\cos s+V\sin s$, null ones are $X=P+sV$, and spacelike ones are $X=P\cosh s+V\sinh s$. Timelike geodesics are closed on the hyperboloid but lift to complete nonclosed curves on its <universal cover>. In static coordinates, conserved $E=\cosh^2r\,dt/ds$ gives $(dr/ds)^2=E^2/\cosh^2r-1$ and $\sinh r=\sqrt{E^2-1}\sin(s-s_0)$. Null affine parameters obey $\lambda=\pm\sinh r/E+\text{constant}$, proving completeness at both spatial ends.

= Conformal strip of two-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Two-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$ds^2=\sec^2\psi(dt^2-d\psi^2),\quad\psi=\arctan(\sinh r)$}

On the universal cover, $-\pi/2<\psi<\pi/2$ and $t\in\mathbb R$. The two <conformal boundaries> are timelike. <Null geodesics> are $t\pm\psi=\text{constant}$ and can travel from either boundary to the center in finite coordinate time, despite taking infinite physical affine parameter to reach the boundary. There is no event horizon for the complete central static observer. The spacetime is not <globally hyperbolic>, unlike the global conformal cylinder of <two-dimensional de Sitter spacetime>; well-posed field evolution requires boundary conditions as well as initial data.

= Group model of three-dimensional anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}
{title2=$\mathrm{AdS}_3\cong SL(2,\mathbb R)$}

Associate $(u,v,x,y)$ with $X=\begin{pmatrix}u+x&y+v\\y-v&u-x\end{pmatrix}$. The ambient quadratic form is $-\det X$; hence the radius-$\ell$ anti-de Sitter quadric is $X=\ell g$ with $g\in SL(2,\mathbb R)$. The action $X\mapsto AXB^{-1}$ gives $SO_0(2,2)\cong(SL(2,\mathbb R)\times SL(2,\mathbb R))/\{(I,I),(-I,-I)\}$. Its induced <bi-invariant pseudo-Riemannian metric> is $g=\ell^2K/8$, giving $\operatorname{Ric}=-2g/\ell^2$ and cosmological constant $-1/\ell^2$. The antipodal quotient is $PSL(2,\mathbb R)$; unwrapping global time gives the universal covering group instead. The connected component is essential in the displayed orthogonal-group isomorphism.

= Static coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Anti-de Sitter spacetime}

In four dimensions, global static coordinates have
$$ds^2=-(1+r^2/a^2)dt^2+(1+r^2/a^2)^{-1}dr^2+r^2d\Omega^2.$$

= Radial timelike geodesic in anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Static coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime}

A radial timelike geodesic through the origin obeys a harmonic radial equation and returns to the origin after proper time $\pi a$, independently of its energy.

= Radial null geodesic in anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Static coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime}

In global static coordinates, an outward radial null geodesic takes infinite affine parameter to approach the anti-de Sitter conformal boundary but only finite static coordinate time $\pi a/2$.

= Stable circular timelike geodesic in anti-de Sitter spacetime
{parent=Static coordinates on anti-de Sitter spacetime}

At every radius $r_0>0$, global anti-de Sitter spacetime has stable circular timelike geodesics with $|\dot\phi|=1/a$ and $\dot t=1$.

= Planarity of geodesics in spherical symmetry
{parent=General relativity}

Rotational symmetry conserves the angular-momentum direction, so every nonradial geodesic lies in a plane through the symmetry centre; coordinates may place that plane at $\theta=\pi/2$.

= Timelike geodesic effective potential
{parent=General relativity}

After using Killing constants and four-velocity normalization, radial timelike geodesic motion can often be written as kinetic energy plus an effective potential equal to a constant.

= Effective-potential stability of a circular orbit
{parent=Timelike geodesic effective potential}

A circular orbit at a strict local minimum of the radial effective potential is stable under small radial perturbations.

= Effective potential for timelike Schwarzschild geodesics
{parent=Timelike geodesic effective potential}

For equatorial timelike motion with specific angular momentum $h$,
$$
\frac12\dot r^2+V_{\rm eff}(r)=\frac{E^2-1}{2},
\qquad
V_{\rm eff}=-\frac mr+\frac{h^2}{2r^2}-\frac{mh^2}{r^3}.
$$
A circular orbit has $h^2=mr^2/(r-3m)$ and therefore requires $r>3m$.

= Schwarzschild marginally bound capture
{c}
{parent=Effective potential for timelike Schwarzschild geodesics}
{title2=$|h|<4M$}

= Marginally bound Schwarzschild capture
{synonym}

A <timelike geodesic> with unit specific <Killing energy> can plunge from infinity into a <Schwarzschild black hole> only if its specific <angular momentum> satisfies $|h|<4M$. The limiting case $|h|=4M$ asymptotes to the unstable orbit at $r=4M$. A geodesic already inside the angular-momentum barrier can cross the <event horizon> with larger $|h|$, so the origin-at-infinity condition matters.

= Angular velocity of a circular Schwarzschild geodesic
{parent=Effective potential for timelike Schwarzschild geodesics}
{c}

A circular timelike geodesic of Schwarzschild radius $r>3M$ has coordinate angular velocity
$$
\frac{d\phi}{dt}=\sqrt{\frac{M}{r^3}}.
$$
Its coordinate period is $2\pi\sqrt{r^3/M}$.

= Proper period of a circular Schwarzschild geodesic
{parent=Angular velocity of a circular Schwarzschild geodesic}
{c}

Over one circular orbit at radius $r>3M$, the orbiting clock measures
$$
\Delta\tau=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{r^2(r-3M)}M}.
$$
The factor $r-3M$ combines gravitational and orbital kinematic time dilation.

= Schwarzschild perihelion precession
{parent=Effective potential for timelike Schwarzschild geodesics}
{c}

The nearly circular orbit equation
$$
u''+u=\frac m{h^2}+3mu^2
$$
has radial angular frequency $1-3m^2/h^2$ to leading order. The perihelion advances by $6\pi m^2/h^2$ per orbit.

= Metric tensor
{title2=$g_{\mu\nu}$}
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

A metric tensor is a <nondegenerate bilinear form>[nondegenerate] symmetric <covariant tensor> that defines squared line elements by $ds^2=g_{\mu\nu}dx^\mu dx^\nu$.

= Variation of metric volume density
{parent=Metric tensor}
{title2=$\delta\sqrt{|\det g|}=\frac12\sqrt{|\det g|}\,g^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$}

For a <nondegenerate> <metric tensor> whose signature is fixed during the <variation>, the <Jacobi formula> gives $\delta\det g=(\det g)g^{ab}\delta g_{ab}$. Differentiating the square root of the absolute <determinant> proves the displayed identity. Expressed through the <variation of inverse metric>, the formula is $-\sqrt{|\det g|}\,g_{ab}\delta g^{ab}/2$. Absolute values accommodate either Riemannian or Lorentzian signature.

= Variation of inverse metric
{parent=Metric tensor}
{title2=$\delta g^{ab}=-g^{ac}g^{bd}\delta g_{cd}$}

Differentiating $g^{ac}g_{cb}=\delta^a_b$ and multiplying by the inverse gives the displayed <variation>. It is the <matrix inverse> <derivative>, expressed with the covariant and contravariant components of a <metric tensor>.

= Inverse metric volume variation
{parent=Metric tensor}
{title2=$\delta\sqrt{|g|}=-\tfrac12\sqrt{|g|}g_{ab}\delta g^{ab}$}

For a nondegenerate <metric tensor>, differentiating $g_{ac}g^{cb}=\delta_a{}^b$ gives $\delta g_{ab}=-g_{ac}g_{bd}\delta g^{cd}$. Combining this with the <Jacobi determinant derivative formula> proves the displayed volume-density variation. It is the determinant contribution when defining a <stress-energy tensor> by varying the inverse <metric tensor>.

= Prolate spheroidal coordinates
{parent=Metric tensor}
{title2=$(\lambda,\mu,\phi)$}

For $0<\alpha<\beta$, define $-\beta\leq\mu\leq-\alpha\leq\lambda$ by $R^2/(\tau+\alpha)+z^2/(\tau+\beta)=1$. Then $R^2=(\lambda+\alpha)(\mu+\alpha)/(\alpha-\beta)$ and $z^2=(\lambda+\beta)(\mu+\beta)/(\beta-\alpha)$. Constant $\lambda$ surfaces are confocal <prolate spheroids> and constant interior $\mu$ surfaces are two-sheeted hyperboloids, with foci $z=\pm\sqrt{\beta-\alpha}$. The coordinates are orthogonal away from their degeneracies. The squared scale factors are $h_\lambda^2=(\lambda-\mu)/[4(\lambda+\alpha)(\lambda+\beta)]$, $h_\mu^2=(\mu-\lambda)/[4(\mu+\alpha)(\mu+\beta)]$ and $h_\phi^2=R^2$. The two signs of $z$ are coordinate branches, joined at the equatorial fold $\mu=-\beta$.

= Lorentzian metric
{c}
{parent=Metric tensor}
{title2=$\operatorname{signature}(g)=(-,+,\ldots,+)$}

A Lorentzian metric is a smooth nondegenerate symmetric <metric tensor> with one negative and all other positive directions, or its opposite-sign convention. At each point, a nonzero tangent is timelike, null or spacelike according as $g(v,v)$ is negative, zero or positive in the first convention. Unlike a <Riemannian metric>, it gives a light cone rather than a positive norm. <Minkowski spacetime> is its constant flat model, and <proper time> along a timelike curve is $\int\sqrt{-g(\dot\gamma,\dot\gamma)}\,du$.

= Spacetime metric
{parent=Lorentzian metric}
{title2=$g_{\mu\nu}$}

A spacetime metric is the <Lorentzian metric> on a <spacetime>. It determines <proper time> along timelike paths and the null directions that describe local light propagation.

= Null coordinate
{parent=Lorentzian metric}

A <null coordinate> is a coordinate function $u$ whose gradient is null, $g^{ab}\partial_au\partial_bu=0$. Its level sets are <null hypersurfaces>. Pairs of such coordinates straighten radial <null directions> in two-dimensional orbit metrics; a monotone reparametrization of either function preserves its null level sets. Examples include the <retarded and advanced null coordinates> and the radial <Kruskal–Szekeres coordinates>.

= Schwarzschild retarded null coordinate
{c}
{parent=Null coordinate}
{title2=$u=t-r_*,\quad r_*=r+2M\log(r/(2M)-1)$}

In the exterior of <Schwarzschild spacetime>, $dr_*/dr=(1-2M/r)^{-1}$. Outgoing <radial null geodesics> therefore have constant $u=t-r_*$. At fixed receiver radius $R$, their arrival Schwarzschild time is $u+r_*(R)$, so differences in $u$ give arrival-time differences. A static receiver's <proper time> includes the factor $\sqrt{1-2M/R}$.

// Target: optics.bigb

= Spacelike vector
{parent=Lorentzian metric}
{title2=$g(v,v)>0$}

With <metric signature> $(-,+,+,+)$, a nonzero <vector> $v$ is spacelike when its <Lorentzian inner product> with itself satisfies $g(v,v)>0$. In <Minkowski spacetime>, this means its spatial components have larger squared Euclidean length than its time component. The classification is preserved by <Lorentz transformations>. A <timelike vector> has negative squared <Lorentzian inner product>, and a <null vector> has zero squared <Lorentzian inner product>.

= Lorentzian inner product
{parent=Lorentzian metric}

A <Lorentzian inner product> is a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form with one negative and the remaining positive eigenvalues in the mostly-plus convention. Two nonzero future causal vectors have nonpositive inner product; if either is timelike, it is strictly negative.

= Null covector
{parent=Lorentzian inner product}
{title2=$g^{\alpha\beta}\ell_\alpha\ell_\beta=0$}

A nonzero <covector> $\ell$ is null when its squared dual <Lorentzian inner product> is zero: $g^{\alpha\beta}\ell_\alpha\ell_\beta=0$. Raising its index gives a <null vector>. Null covectors describe normals to characteristic <light cones> and are the test directions in the <classical null condition for wave equations>.

= Causal vector
{parent=Lorentzian metric}

A nonzero <causal vector> is timelike or null: $g(v,v)\le0$ with <metric signature> $(-,+,+,+)$. The zero vector is commonly included when stating the <dominant energy condition>.

= Timelike vector
{parent=Lorentzian metric}
{title2=$g(v,v)<0$}

= Timelike direction
{synonym}

A <timelike vector> $v$ obeys $g(v,v)<0$ in <metric signature> $(-,+,+,+)$. Its orthogonal complement has positive-definite metric; the two timelike cones give the local future and past choices.

= Timelike vector field
{parent=Timelike vector}

A <timelike vector field> assigns a timelike vector continuously or smoothly to every event in its domain. A globally defined such field gives a <time orientation>, but it need not be a <Killing vector field> or a <timelike geodesic vector field>.

= Oblate spheroidal coordinates
{parent=Metric tensor}
{wiki}

With parameter $a\geq0$, oblate spheroidal coordinates on Euclidean three-space are
$$
x=\sqrt{r^2+a^2}\sin\theta\cos\phi,
\quad
y=\sqrt{r^2+a^2}\sin\theta\sin\phi,
\quad
z=r\cos\theta.
$$
Writing $\Sigma=r^2+a^2\cos^2\theta$ and $\Delta=r^2+a^2$, their spatial line element is
$$
dl^2=\frac\Sigma\Delta dr^2+\Sigma d\theta^2
+\Delta\sin^2\theta\,d\phi^2.
$$

= Outgoing null oblate spheroidal coordinates
{parent=Oblate spheroidal coordinates}

Starting from the <Minkowski metric> in <oblate spheroidal coordinates>, set $u=t-r$, $R=r$, and choose an azimuth $\varphi$ satisfying
$$
d\varphi=d\phi-\frac{a}{R^2+a^2}dR.
$$
Then
$$
ds^2=-du^2-2\,du\,dR
+2a\sin^2\theta\,dR\,d\varphi
+(R^2+a^2\cos^2\theta)d\theta^2
+(R^2+a^2)\sin^2\theta\,d\varphi^2,
$$
so the coordinate vector $\partial_R$ is null.

= Inverse metric
{title2=$g^{\mu\nu}$}
{parent=Metric tensor}

The inverse metric satisfies $g^{\mu\rho}g_{\rho\nu}=\delta^\mu_\nu$ and raises covariant tensor indices.

= Two-dimensional hyperbolic metric in polar coordinates
{title2=$ds^2=a^2(dr^2+\sinh^2r\,d\phi^2)$}
{parent=General relativity}

This metric is the <hyperbolic plane> with curvature scale $a$. Its nonzero connection coefficients are
$$
\Gamma^r_{\phi\phi}=-\sinh r\cosh r,
\qquad
\Gamma^\phi_{r\phi}=\Gamma^\phi_{\phi r}=\coth r.
$$

= Coordinate geodesics of the hyperbolic polar metric
{parent=Two-dimensional hyperbolic metric in polar coordinates}

Every radial coordinate line $\phi=\text{constant}$ is a geodesic. No nonconstant coordinate circle $r=r_0>0$ is a geodesic.

= Curvature of the two-dimensional hyperbolic polar metric
{parent=Two-dimensional hyperbolic metric in polar coordinates}

For the curvature convention
$$
R^\alpha{}_{\beta\gamma\delta}
=\partial_\gamma\Gamma^\alpha_{\beta\delta}
-\partial_\delta\Gamma^\alpha_{\beta\gamma}
+\Gamma^\mu_{\beta\delta}\Gamma^\alpha_{\mu\gamma}
-\Gamma^\mu_{\beta\gamma}\Gamma^\alpha_{\mu\delta},
$$
the independent nonzero mixed components are
$$
R^r{}_{\phi r\phi}=-\sinh^2r,
\qquad
R^\phi{}_{r\phi r}=-1,
$$
and the Ricci scalar is $R=-2/a^2$.

= Ricci scalar
{title2=$R$}
{parent=General relativity}
{c}

The Ricci scalar is the full metric contraction of the <Ricci tensor>:
$$
R=g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}.
$$

\Include[geodesic-congruence]{parent=General relativity}

= Null hypersurface
{parent=General relativity}
{wiki}

A null hypersurface has a degenerate induced metric. Its normal covector raises to a null vector tangent to the hypersurface, whose integral curves are its null generators.

= Null hypersurface normal generates null geodesics
{parent=Null hypersurface}

If $n_a=\nabla_a u$ is normal to a null level set, then on the hypersurface
$$
n^b\nabla_bn_a=\frac12\nabla_a(n^2)=\kappa n_a.
$$
Thus the normal is tangent to null pregeodesics and can be rescaled to give an affine null tangent.

= Killing horizon
{parent=Null hypersurface}
{wiki}

A Killing horizon is a <null hypersurface> whose null generators are orbits of a <Killing vector field> $K$. On the horizon, $K^b\nabla_bK^a=\kappa K^a$ defines its <surface gravity>.

= Horizon angular velocity
{parent=Killing horizon}
{title2=$\chi=k+\Omega_Hm$}

In an axisymmetric stationary spacetime, the <horizon angular velocity> is the constant coefficient in the null horizon generator $\chi=k+\Omega_Hm$, where $k$ is the stationary Killing vector normalized at infinity and $m$ generates axial orbits with period $2\pi$. It measures rotation relative to the stationary frame at infinity. For a <Kerr black hole>, $\Omega_H=a/(r_+^2+a^2)$. It enters the rotating physical-process first law through the horizon flux of $E-\Omega_HJ$.

= Bifurcate Killing horizon
{parent=Killing horizon}
{title2=$K=\kappa(V\partial_V-U\partial_U)$}

A bifurcate Killing horizon consists of two intersecting null <Killing horizons> of the same <Killing vector field>, which vanishes on their spacelike intersection, the <bifurcation surface>. In regular Kruskal coordinates the horizons are $U=0$ and $V=0$, and their intersection is $U=V=0$. The displayed field is tangent and normal to each horizon branch and zero on the intersection.

= Degenerate Killing horizon
{parent=Killing horizon}
{title2=$\kappa=0$}

A <degenerate Killing horizon> has zero <surface gravity>. In the radial quotient of an <extremal black hole>, a double horizon root produces a pole in the <tortoise coordinate> rather than the logarithm of a simple root. Such a horizon has no bifurcation surface of the usual nondegenerate type.

= Optical scalars on a Killing horizon
{parent=Killing horizon}
{title2=$\theta=\hat\sigma=\hat\omega=0$}

The <null expansion>, <null shear> and <null twist> of a congruence tangent to the generators of a <Killing horizon> all vanish there. If $k$ is its Killing generator and $\ell=fk$ is an affine generator, the <screen-space projector> kills every term proportional to $k$. The symmetric part of the projected derivative of $\ell$ therefore vanishes by the <Killing equation>. The antisymmetric part vanishes by <Frobenius theorem>, since $\ell$ is hypersurface-orthogonal. These conclusions extend through a regular <bifurcation surface> by continuity.

= Cosmological horizon
{parent=Killing horizon}
{wiki}

A cosmological horizon is a causal boundary beyond which events cannot communicate with a specified observer because of the global expansion or geometry of spacetime.

= Surface gravity
{title2=$\kappa$}
{parent=Killing horizon}
{wiki}

The surface gravity $\kappa$ measures the failure of a horizon-generating <Killing vector field> to be affinely parametrized. For a static metric $ds^2=-f(r)dt^2+f(r)^{-1}dr^2+\cdots$ with a simple horizon at $r=r_+$, $\kappa=|f'(r_+)|/2$.

= Signed surface gravity of a de Sitter horizon
{parent=Surface gravity}
{title2=$\kappa_{\rm future}=1/R,\quad\kappa_{\rm past}=-1/R$}

For the future-directed central-time <Killing vector field> in a <static patch of de Sitter spacetime>, the outgoing null chart gives the future branch coefficient $+1/R$ in $\nabla_k k=\kappa k$. The ingoing chart gives $-1/R$ on the past branch. Both follow from $d(k^2)=-2\kappa k^\flat$; the cross-term sign changes the horizon covector. The physical <Hawking temperature> uses $|\kappa|$, not a signed negative temperature.

= Surface gravity of a static spherical horizon
{parent=Surface gravity}
{title2=$\kappa=\tfrac12\sqrt{A'(r_+)B'(r_+)}$}

For a <static spacetime> with $g_{tt}=-A(r)$, $g_{rr}=1/B(r)$ and simple simultaneous horizon zeros, the squared <surface gravity> of $\partial_t$ is $\lim BA'^2/(4A)=A'(r_+)B'(r_+)/4$. Choose the positive value for the outer horizon. A rescaling of the time generator rescales its <surface gravity>.

= Surface gravity of a five-dimensional charged black hole
{parent=Surface gravity of a static spherical horizon}
{title2=$\kappa_\pm=\pm2\sqrt{M^2-Q^2}/r_\pm^3$}

The time translation is normalized at infinity. For the roots $r_\pm^2=M\pm\sqrt{M^2-Q^2}$, evaluate $V'(r)/2$ to get the signed horizon values. The outer <Hawking temperature> in natural units is $\sqrt{M^2-Q^2}/(\pi r_+^3)$. The extremal limit has zero surface gravity; the neutral case has only the outer positive-radius horizon and temperature $1/(2\pi\sqrt{2M})$.

= Surface gravity from the Killing derivative
{parent=Surface gravity}
{title2=$\kappa^2=-\tfrac12(\nabla_aK_b)(\nabla^aK^b)$}

At a regular <Killing horizon>, the generator is hypersurface orthogonal and satisfies $\nabla_KK=\kappa K$. The <Killing derivative contraction identity> gives the displayed squared <surface gravity>, including through a <bifurcation surface> by continuity. The generator's normalization is part of this formula.

= Affine horizon-generator scaling
{parent=Surface gravity}
{title2=$\xi=\kappa\lambda k$}

If a <Killing horizon> generator $\xi$ has constant nonzero <surface gravity> $\kappa$, write $\xi=F(\lambda)k$ with $k=d/d\lambda$ affine. The equation $\nabla_\xi\xi=\kappa\xi$ implies $dF/d\lambda=\kappa$, so shifting the affine origin gives $\xi=\kappa\lambda k$.

= Schwarzschild surface gravity
{parent=Surface gravity}
{c}
{title2=$\kappa=1/(4M)$}

For a <Schwarzschild black hole> of <mass> $M$ in <geometrized units>, $f=1-2M/r$ gives $\kappa=f\prime(2M)/2=1/(4M)$. The <Killing vector field> is normalized at infinity; changing that normalization changes $\kappa$.

= Bifurcation surface
{parent=Killing horizon}

A bifurcation surface is a spacelike codimension-two cross-section on which the Killing generator of a bifurcate Killing horizon vanishes. Affine parameters on the future and past branches can be chosen to vanish there.

= Gaussian null coordinates
{c}
{parent=Null hypersurface}
{wiki}

Gaussian null coordinates adapt one coordinate to the generators of a <null hypersurface>, one affine coordinate to transverse null geodesics, and the remaining coordinates to a spacelike cross-section. If the generator parameter is affine, the metric can locally be written
$$
ds^2=2\,dr\,d\lambda+r^2F\,d\lambda^2+2rh_i\,d\lambda\,dy^i+h_{ij}\,dy^i\,dy^j.
$$

= Trapped surface
{parent=Null hypersurface}
{wiki}

A trapped surface is a closed spacelike codimension-two surface whose two future-directed orthogonal null congruences both have negative expansion.

= Marginally outer trapped surface
{parent=Trapped surface}
{title2=$\theta_+=0$}

A closed spacelike two-surface is marginally outer trapped when its future outgoing <null expansion> is zero. For the future black-hole type, the ingoing <null expansion> is negative. An <apparent horizon> on a chosen spatial slice is ordinarily its outermost such surface under suitable regularity assumptions. The notion depends on the slice and is not the global definition of an <event horizon>.

= Apparent horizon
{parent=Trapped surface}
{wiki}

An <apparent horizon> is the outer boundary of the future-trapped region on a chosen spatial slice, under the usual regularity conditions. Its location depends on the slice; it is not the globally defined <event horizon>. At a regular boundary the outward <null expansion> vanishes while the inward <null expansion> is negative. Its area cannot be substituted into every version of the <Penrose inequality> without the appropriate area and initial-data hypotheses.

= Anti-trapped surface
{parent=Trapped surface}

An anti-trapped surface is the time reverse of a trapped surface: both future-directed orthogonal null expansions are positive.

= Null geodesic completeness
{parent=General relativity}

A spacetime is null-geodesically complete when every inextendible affinely parametrized null geodesic has an affine parameter extending without a finite endpoint in both directions.

A <complete manifold> requires completeness for every geodesic, whereas this condition only tests null ones.

= Null geodesic incompleteness
{parent=Null geodesic completeness}

= Null-geodesically incomplete
{synonym}

<Null geodesic incompleteness> means that some <maximal geodesic> with a <null tangent> has a finite endpoint of its <affine parameter>. This can indicate a removable missing region or a genuine obstruction; it does not alone assert curvature divergence.

= Penrose singularity theorem
{c}
{parent=Null geodesic completeness}
{wiki}

The Penrose singularity theorem states that a globally hyperbolic spacetime with a noncompact Cauchy hypersurface, a closed trapped surface, and the null convergence condition is future null-geodesically incomplete. Time reversal replaces the trapped surface by an anti-trapped surface and gives past incompleteness.

= Penrose theorem at a Cauchy horizon
{c}
{parent=Penrose singularity theorem}

The globally hyperbolic <maximal Cauchy development> of appropriate <Reissner-Nordstrom spacetime> data obeys the <Penrose singularity theorem> when it contains a closed <trapped surface>. Its resulting <null geodesic incompleteness> can include generators reaching a smoothly extendible inner <Cauchy horizon> in finite <affine parameter>. The full analytic extension is not globally hyperbolic and cannot be substituted into that version of the theorem. Incompleteness alone does not establish curvature blowup at every endpoint.

= Compactness of the future horismos of a trapped surface
{parent=Penrose singularity theorem}

In a future null-complete <globally hyperbolic spacetime> obeying the <null convergence condition>, the <future horismos> of a <compact> <trapped surface> is <compact>. Normalize its future null normals continuously using a timelike field. <Compactness> gives a uniform negative bound $\theta\leq-c<0$ on both initial <null expansions>. The <null focusing theorem> bounds all boundary generators by affine length $2/c$. Their endpoints lie in the image under a <continuous map> of the <compact> bundle of normalized null normals times $[0,2/c]$. The <future horismos> is <closed>, so is a <compact> subset of that image.

= Causal hierarchy
{parent=General relativity}

The causal hierarchy orders restrictions on the causal structure of a <spacetime>, from exclusion of <closed timelike curves> through <stable causality> and <global hyperbolicity>.

= Observer event horizon
{parent=Causal hierarchy}
{title2=$H^+_\gamma=\partial I^-(\gamma),\quad H^-_\gamma=\partial I^+(\gamma)$}

An observer's future event horizon bounds the events that can ever signal to its future-inextendible worldline. Its past event horizon bounds the events reachable by signals from its past-inextendible worldline. A complete inertial observer in <Minkowski spacetime> has neither. The uniformly accelerated worldline $t=a^{-1}\sinh(a\tau)$, $x=a^{-1}\cosh(a\tau)$ has $I^-(\gamma)=\{t-x<0\}$ and $I^+(\gamma)=\{t+x>0\}$. Their boundaries are its two <Rindler horizons>. This observer dependence is distinct from a black-hole horizon defined using <future null infinity>.

= Cosmological event horizon
{parent=Observer event horizon}
{title2=$\chi_{\mathrm{eh}}(t)=\int_t^\infty\frac{dt'}{a(t')}$}

For a comoving observer in a spatially flat <Friedmann-Lemaitre-Robertson-Walker metric>, a finite future <conformal time> interval gives an event horizon at the displayed comoving radius. Its physical radius at time $t$ is $a(t)\chi_{\mathrm{eh}}(t)$.

= Cosmological round-trip communication horizon
{parent=Cosmological event horizon}
{title2=$d_{\mathrm{round}}(t)=\frac{a(t)}2\int_t^\infty\frac{dt'}{a(t')}$}

A light signal sent from a comoving observer to a comoving reflector at separation $\chi$, and immediately returned, consumes <conformal time> $2\chi$. The supremum of the initial physical reflector distances allowing a return is therefore half the <cosmological event horizon> radius. Equality generally requires infinite return time; finite-time reception requires a strictly smaller distance. For a <cosmological domain wall network> with $a\propto t^2$, the supremum is $t/2=1/H$.

= Time orientation
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

A <time orientation> is a continuous choice of future cone throughout a time-orientable <spacetime>. A continuous nonzero <timelike vector field> supplies such a choice. For <metric signature> $(-,+,+,+)$, a nonzero causal vector $Z$ is future-directed relative to a future timelike $W$ exactly when $g(W,Z)<0$.

= Causal curve
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

A <causal curve> has an everywhere nonspacelike tangent of consistent time orientation; its smooth segments are timelike or null. More generally, curves with local <Lipschitz continuity> and future causal tangent almost everywhere are allowed. The <causal future> and <causal past> are defined by reachability along these curves.

= Causal past
{parent=Causal hierarchy}
{title2=$J^-(S)$}

The <causal past> is the time-reversed <causal future>. Relative to a specified component $\mathcal I^+$ of <future null infinity>, the <black hole> region is the complement of its <causal past>: $\mathcal B=\mathcal M\setminus J^-(\mathcal I^+)$.

= Causal future
{parent=Causal hierarchy}
{title2=$J^+(S)$}

The <causal future> of $S$ consists of points reached from $S$ by future-directed causal curves, including points of $S$ itself. In a <globally hyperbolic spacetime>, the <causal future> of a <compact> set is <closed>.

= Future horismos
{parent=Causal future}
{title2=$E^+(S)=J^+(S)\setminus I^+(S)$}

The <future horismos> is the part of the <causal future> outside the <chronological future>. For a <compact> set in a <globally hyperbolic spacetime>, it equals the <boundary> of the <chronological future>. Its normal <null geodesic> generators cannot have passed a <conjugate point to a spacelike surface> when the initial set is that surface.

= Chronological future
{title2=$I^+(S)$}
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

The chronological future $I^+(S)$ of a subset $S$ of a <spacetime> is the set of points reachable from $S$ by future-directed <timelike curves>. The chronological future is an <open set>.

= Chronologically saturated subset
{parent=Chronological future}

A subset $U$ is chronologically saturated in both directions when $I^+(U)=U=I^-(U)$. If the ambient <spacetime> is connected and $U$ is nonempty, then $U$ is the whole spacetime.

= Chronological past
{title2=$I^-(S)$}
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

The chronological past $I^-(S)$ is the set of points from which a future-directed <timelike curve> reaches $S$. Equivalently, it is the time reverse of the <chronological future>.

= Closed timelike curve
{parent=Causal hierarchy}
{wiki}

A closed timelike curve is a closed curve whose tangent is everywhere timelike, so an observer following it returns to the same spacetime event.

= Closed timelike circles in negative-radius Kerr spacetime
{parent=Closed timelike curve}
{title2=$g_{\phi\phi}=r^2+a^2+2Ma^2/r<0\quad(r<0\text{ near }0)$}

On the equatorial plane of the <Kerr metric>, the displayed component is negative for sufficiently small negative $r$. A curve of constant $t,r,\theta=\pi/2$, with $\phi$ increasing through $2\pi$, is therefore a regular <closed timelike curve>. It avoids the ring at $r=0$. Such circles occur on the negative-radius sheet of the exact analytic extension, not in the exterior region accessible from positive-radius infinity.

= Stable causality
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

A spacetime is stably causal when its light cones can be widened slightly without creating a <closed timelike curve>. Equivalently, it admits a continuous time function strictly increasing along every future-directed causal curve.

= Strong causality
{parent=Causal hierarchy}

= Strongly causal
{synonym}

Strong causality means that every event has arbitrarily small neighborhoods which no causal curve can leave and then re-enter. <Stable causality> implies strong causality.

= Future-distinguishing spacetime
{parent=Strong causality}

A spacetime is future-distinguishing when $I^+(p)=I^+(q)$ implies $p=q$. Every <strongly causal> spacetime is future-distinguishing.

= Globally hyperbolic spacetime
{parent=Causal hierarchy}
{wiki}

= Global hyperbolicity
{synonym}

= Globally hyperbolic
{synonym}

A globally hyperbolic spacetime has no causal pathologies and admits a Cauchy hypersurface intersected exactly once by every inextendible causal curve.

= Initial data in general relativity
{parent=Globally hyperbolic spacetime}

= Initial data
{synonym}

Gravitational <initial data> on a spatial manifold consist of a <Riemannian metric> $h$ and an <extrinsic curvature> $K$ satisfying the <Hamiltonian constraint> and <momentum constraint>. Matter theories require their own data and constraints as well. The data determine a <maximal Cauchy development>.

= Maximal Cauchy development
{parent=Initial data in general relativity}

A <maximal Cauchy development> is the largest globally hyperbolic solution, unique up to an appropriate <isometry>, determined by admissible <initial data>, with the initial hypersurface a <Cauchy hypersurface>. It can sometimes be extended as a spacetime across a <Cauchy horizon> even though that extension is no longer globally determined by the initial hypersurface.

= Strong cosmic censorship conjecture
{parent=Maximal Cauchy development}

The <strong cosmic censorship conjecture> asserts that the <maximal Cauchy development> of generic admissible <initial data> is inextendible in a specified regularity class. The matter model, topology on initial data, and extension regularity are part of the assertion. An exceptional exact solution with a smooth <Cauchy horizon> need not violate this generic claim.

= Achronal set
{parent=Globally hyperbolic spacetime}

An achronal set contains no pair of points connected by a timelike curve.

= Achronal boundary
{parent=Achronal set}

An achronal boundary is the boundary of a chronological future or past. Its null generators cease to belong to the boundary after developing a conjugate point or intersecting another generator in the relevant way.

= Cauchy surface
{parent=Globally hyperbolic spacetime}
{wiki}
{c}

= Cauchy hypersurface
{synonym}

= Cauchy slice
{c}
{synonym}

A Cauchy hypersurface is an achronal hypersurface met exactly once by every inextendible timelike curve. Its topology records the global spatial topology of a globally hyperbolic spacetime.

= Future domain of dependence
{parent=Cauchy surface}
{title2=$D^+(\Sigma)$}

The set $D^+(\Sigma)$ of events for which every past-inextendible <causal curve> meets $\Sigma$. This causal characterization permits an initial sign condition on a <null geodesic congruence> to propagate throughout the regular region by integrating a focusing inequality from $\Sigma$.

= Cauchy horizon
{parent=Cauchy surface}
{wiki}

The future or past Cauchy horizon of a partial Cauchy hypersurface is the boundary of the region whose physical evolution is determined by data on that hypersurface. Crossing it can destroy uniqueness of the maximal globally hyperbolic development.

= Dependence of a Cauchy horizon on the initial hypersurface
{parent=Cauchy horizon}
{title2=$H^\pm(\Sigma)$}

A complete <Cauchy hypersurface> in <Minkowski spacetime>, such as $t=0$, has full <domain of dependence> and empty <Cauchy horizons>. This does not forbid horizons for a non-Cauchy initial surface in the same spacetime. The smooth edgeless spacelike hyperboloid $t=\sqrt{a^2+r^2}$, $a>0$, has domain of dependence $t>r$ and a past <Cauchy horizon> on the future light cone of the origin. Every causal curve starting inside that cone crosses the hyperboloid in the direction needed for dependence: $t^2-r^2$ passes through $a^2$, while a null generator of the cone never meets it. The time-reversed hyperboloid gives the analogous future horizon.

= Inner-horizon blueshift coordinate
{parent=Cauchy horizon}
{title2=$V_-\propto-e^{-\kappa_-v}$}

Near a nondegenerate outgoing inner <Cauchy horizon>, a regular null coordinate behaves as $V_-\propto-e^{-\kappa_-v}$, where $\kappa_->0$ is the magnitude of the inner-horizon <surface gravity>. Transforming stress components gives $T_{V_-V_-}\propto e^{2\kappa_-v}T_{vv}$. Even a small power-law tail can therefore cause an unbounded locally measured flux, motivating <mass inflation>.

= Mass inflation
{parent=Cauchy horizon}

<Mass inflation> is the growth of the interior effective mass and curvature near an inner <Cauchy horizon> under suitably perturbed black-hole data.
